    innertopmargin=10pt,
    linecolor=cyan!20,
    linewidth=2pt,
    topline=true,
    rightline=true,
    bottomline=true,
    roundcorner=3pt,

\chapter*{Preface}\label{sec:preface}
\markboth{PREFACE}{PREFACE}
Preston and I began working on the Flight Dynamics sub-team in the fall of 2023. From May 2024 to January 2026, we focused our full efforts on creating a 6-\gls{dof} model from scratch. During this time, we learned a great deal about the mathematics and physics involved in creating such a model. We often faced frustration with different conventions and poor explanations of many topics. This made much of the material intractable. In this series, we hope to provide detailed explanations of the necessary concepts to make this journey easier for those who may need it in the future. Furthermore, we hope that this book can be used by those outside the realm of flight dynamics to better understand the methodologies and inner workings of a 6-\gls{dof} model. Often, we have seen that a 6-\gls{dof} model is treated as a `black box'. We hope that this book can provide a level of understanding adequate to allow greater collaboration between those working within and outside of flight dynamics.

In this book, we will be including excerpts of MATLAB and Python where applicable. Code which is longer than a page has been included in a GitHub link in the Appendix. Our hope is that our explanations are thorough enough to facilitate the creation of a 6-\gls{dof} in any coding language. MATLAB and Python are used because they facilitate short and readable code. To use this document to its fullest extent, we recommend that you write scripts as you learn and use this document for reference and as a guide as you build or improve your own models. Doing the practice problems in each chapter will also greatly aid in your understanding.

In this first volume, we primarily cover the translational dynamics and the basics of rotational dynamics needed to set up a 6-\gls{dof} model. We mainly focus on the implementation of these equations into code and best practices. The methods of deriving equations of motion are also heavily discussed for their importance. These are, in our opinion, the fundamental things that need to be understood before working on other aspects of a flight dynamics model. As such, these subjects comprise the majority of the first volume.

Because of the depth of the subjects described here, we have decided to cover most of attitude dynamics in a separate volume. In this volume, we focus on describing how attitude dynamics can be implemented into code. Volume II itself contains relevant theory about attitude dynamics. This second volume is especially useful for the mathematically inclined reader or those who want to implement attitude dynamics in another programming language.

As a note, while our work on flight dynamics has mainly focused on modeling rockets, we hope that this series can be used more widely for a variety of 3-DoF and 6-DoF models. As such, we have attempted to include content that is useful for other systems, such as aircraft modeling and orbital dynamics. That being said, to limit the scope of this series, we have decided to explore these concepts in the context of rocket modeling, and hope that our explanations provide enough clarity for the intrepid reader to explore modeling in other domains.

Some other areas are also covered in this document but are curtailed because Preston and I do not have the experience to give full authority on these subjects. In Volume II and III, we expand upon select sections of this book, especially those related to attitude dynamics, energy methods, and control theory, to better serve those in the future who may hope to recreate and extend the work we have done heretofore.

\chapter*{Notation and Standards}
\markboth{NOTATION \& STANDARDS}{NOTATION \& STANDARDS}

Mathematical notation for the subjects that we discuss is largely standardized, but there are some areas where we use notation slightly different to other sources. You may often see different notation when looking at papers and literature for future work on the 6-\gls{dof}. To remove any doubt, we describe the notation that we use here at the beginning for quick reference. We also choose notation that is used in the Purdue AAE classes. Some of these may be unfamiliar if you have not encountered this coursework yet, but we do our best to describe notation throughout this document and in this section.

For derivatives with respect to time, we will use the compact Newtonian notation. For example, $\dot{x}=\frac{dx}{dt}$, $\ddot{x}=\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}$, and so on. Generally, we avoid the Lagrange notation such as $\frac{dx}{dt}=x'$ as this notation does not make clear what we are taking the derivative with respect to. The prime notation is only used in specific circumstances where the other notation is not easily applicable.

For vectors, any vector with a hat like $\hat{x}$ refers to a vector with a magnitude of 1 (normalized vector). These are often seen as basis vectors or unit vectors describing the direction of a force. Vectors with an ‘arrow’ hat like $\vec{x}$ refer to a general vector with a non-normalized magnitude. Vectors with a non-normalized magnitude may also be represented by a bold-faced symbol. When taking the time derivative of a vector, we prefer to write using this boldface notation, such as $\dot{\textbf{x}}$ instead of $\dot{\vec{x}}$. One exception is the quaternion, which we denote with a boldface $\textbf{q}$ to distinguish it from generalized coordinates.

\subsection*{Vectors and Matrices}
The sheer number of vector quantities used in this document make it difficult to list every instance, but we include the most important here. Notably, we stray from the typical notation of $\vec{r}_{P/O}$ in favor of $\vec{r}^{\ op}$ because of its usage in Purdue AAE.

Common usages are documented here:
$$\begin{array}{ll}
     \vec{F}&  \text{force}\\
     \vec{a}& \text{acceleration}\\
     \vec{v}& \text{velocity}\\
     \vec{V}_\infty&\text{freestream velocity}\\
      \vec{r}^{\ op}& \mathrm{position  \ vector \ from \ o \ to \ p}\\ 
      \vec{X}& \text{state vector}\\
      \vec{M}^{o}& \mathrm{moment\ about\ point\ o}\\
      {}^i\vec{x}& \mathrm{inertial\ vector\ quantity}\\
      \vec{\omega}&\mathrm{angular \ velocity}\\
       \vec{\alpha}& \mathrm{angular \ acceleration}\\
\end{array}$$
\subsection*{Scalar Quantities and Symbols}
$$\begin{array}{ll}
     J&  \mathrm{functional}\\
     \delta&\mathrm{variation}\\
      S& \mathrm{action}\\
      L & \mathrm{Lagrangian}\\
              q& \mathrm{generalized \ coordinate}\\
         \dot{q}& \mathrm{generalized \ position}\\
       k& \mathrm{spring \ constant}\\
        \mathcal{M}& \mathrm{number\ of\ degrees\ of\ freedom}\\
     T& \mathrm{kinetic \ energy}\\
      U& \mathrm{potential \ energy}\\
    \mathcal{H}& \mathrm{Hamiltonian}\\
     Re& \mathrm{Reynolds\ number}\\
     M & \mathrm{Mach \ number}\\
      \alpha & \mathrm{angle \ of \ attack}\\
       C_x& \mathrm{aerodynamic \ force \ coefficient}\\
        \Delta t& \mathrm{timestep}\\
\end{array}$$
\subsection*{Coding Standards}
A good 6-\gls{dof} not only models the motion of a vehicle as accurately as possible, but is also well-structured and easily readable. Strict adherence to an agreed upon set of coding standards is essential for readability. These standards cover everything from naming conventions to header structure, and make the integration of code from multiple contributors more seamless.

For short pieces of code written inline, we use a mono-spaced font with the appearance of "code snippet" as shown. Longer code is included in listings with line numbers using the same mono-spaced font. Generally, we use the following conventions in our coding practice:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Variables utilize camelCase where possible
    \item Symbolic variables may use underscores
    \item File names, functions, and classes utilize PascalCase


\end{itemize}
Script file headers should be used to document code and functions. We use the following standard for our MATLAB code:

\begin{matlabscript}{Script Header}
% PSP FLIGHT DYNAMICS:
% Title:
% Author:
% Last Modified: 
% 
% Description:
% 
% Inputs: 
% 
% Outputs:
\end{matlabscript}

\chapter{Overview}
\pagenumbering{arabic}
\pagestyle{fancy}

\begin{chapquote}{Carl Sagan}
``If you wish to make an apple pie from scratch, you must first invent the universe.''
\end{chapquote}

In much the same way, we must construct the conceptual background required to build a 6-\gls{dof} from the ground up. This chapter details the general requirements and an outline of the process we will be following.

\section{Document Outline}
A general overview of the scope of this document is given in the preface.  Here, we will go into more detail about the layout of each section. Our goal is to present information in a similar manner to a textbook while still remaining approachable and interesting to read. Our pedagogical approach is strongly reliant on examples and trying problems. This is the best method for readers to learn the content. In each chapter (aside from this overview chapter), there will be some additional content at the end that may be used for quick reference. For instance, we include a `Key Ideas' section that may be used to review the important takeaways from each chapter.

Additional resources are included in footnotes and the appendix which the curious reader may benefit from. Many topics are covered in this document; many of which deserve their own book-length discussion. Because we have neither the desire nor capability to write such a long book, we have linked these beneficial resources whenever possible. It is certainly not necessary to read any/all of these sources to comprehend the content here, but they may prove useful for deeper research into topic areas that interest the reader.

The end of some sections also provides several practice problems to ensure the key ideas of each topic are understood by the reader. These problems are mainly to validate the reader's understanding and provide an example of using the presented content. For readers new to this content, it is expected that some problems may take some consultation with other sources to complete. However, we have attempted to make these problems challenging while not being out of reach. Problems are designed to make the reader apply the knowledge and truly learn.

\section{Necessary Background}
The goal of this document is to provide a comprehensive overview of the process and means of creating, maintaining, and building on a 6-\gls{dof} model. Although we strive to make the document as easy as possible to understand, there are some instances where technical jargon is necessary and an understanding of mathematical concepts is required. The first time important technical jargon is used, we will \textit{italicize} the word for recognition. It is important to learn these terms to effectively and concisely describe your work. That being said, we attempt to keep the usage of jargon to a minimum to make this text approachable. A glossary with these terms has been provided so that all of these words are included in a single location. 

To fully comprehend and successfully use this book, it is recommended that you have a good understanding of calculus, linear algebra, and calculus-based physics. A rudimentary understanding of differential equations and dynamics will also prove useful but is not strictly required. Some concepts will arise in this book that are derived from graduate-level coursework and complex mathematics. In this book, we will not derive equations from first principles, but will describe necessary features for building a 6-\gls{dof} model. Often, these more complex derivations and ideas are presented in Volume II and III.

\section{Why 6-DoF?}
A \acrlong{dof} (\gls{dof}) refers to the number of ways an object can move in space. More precisely, a \gls{dof} describes an independent parameter in the motion of a system. When modeling rigid body dynamics, it is common to see 1-\gls{dof}, 3-\gls{dof}, and 6-\gls{dof} models used in aerospace applications. Each of these are useful constructs, and sometimes a simulation with more degrees of freedom is not necessarily better.
\subsection{1-DoF}\label{overview: 1dof}
The initial modeling that is often done for rocket sizing may use 1-\gls{dof} modeling. In this type of model, there is only one axis along which an object may translate or rotate about. In this case of rocket modeling, the only degree of freedom is the altitude of the rocket. The goal of this type of model is mainly to provide a heuristic for determining rocket performance in the very preliminary stages of sizing by estimating the maximum altitude and speed they can achieve.

Another example of this type of model is a simple mass-spring system along one axis. Such models may be useful for estimating the vibrations of bodies or as a first-order approximation of fuel slosh. These models are typical examples where a single \gls{dof} is perfectly acceptable for analysis.

We will use the example of a 1-\gls{dof} in \autoref{sec: The 1DoF Case} to explain the creation of equations of motion in the simplest way. However, these models are often too constrained to give the most valuable information about a system, which is why 3-\gls{dof} and 6-\gls{dof} models are preferred in the context of flight dynamics.
\subsection{3-DoF}
After 1-\gls{dof}, the next step up is often a 3-\gls{dof} model. This type of model should be familiar to all flight dynamics members, as it is the subject of our onboarding project. In a 3-\gls{dof} model, there may be any combination of translational degrees of freedom and rotational degrees of freedom. Often, 3-\gls{dof} modeling is utilized when either the rotational dynamics or translational dynamics of an object are considered unimportant. One example of this may be the calculation of an interplanetary transfer. The primary concern of the simulation is not the orientation of the satellite, but rather the location and velocity of the satellite and planets at various points in 3D space. 

Often, problems in engineering are constrained to planar motion. We can consider one of the translational degrees of freedom and two of the rotational degrees of freedom to be non-important to the problem. This simplification allows us to greatly simplify analysis. One consequence of this action is that rotations are only permitted in a single axis. With two translational and one rotational degree of freedom, we can create useful models of many systems without great complexity.

Lastly, we see 3-\gls{dof} models used when we are considering only the rotational dynamics of a system. For objects such as gyroscopes and other spinning bodies, we may choose to model rotational motion in this way.

Although the rest of this document will mainly discuss 6-\gls{dof} models, really consider if you need that full complexity for your model! Often, a 3-\gls{dof} or 4-\gls{dof} model is perfectly adequate for a wide variety of problems without the hassle of a 6-\gls{dof} model. 
\subsection{6-DoF}
6-\gls{dof} models are the most complex representation of a single rigid body mechanical system. In 3D-space, there are only 6 possible degrees of freedom, and we simulate the dynamics in all of them.

Doing so is quite a difficult challenge, but it is not insurmountable. With careful planning, it is possible to gain an appreciation and understanding of the dynamics of a full 3D system. Doing so allows unrivaled modeling potential. In the case of a rocket, the primary reason that modeling in 6-\gls{dof} is so important is because it allows us to analyze the aerodynamic stability of the system. In both 1-\gls{dof} and 3-\gls{dof} modeling, this important aspect of the system is largely neglected.

\section{The General Picture}

It is often useful to see where we are going -- and our general method of getting there -- before we start. With this `map' at our disposal, it is much easier to get through the complexities of the mathematics involved in creating a 6-\gls{dof} model.

The essence of the 6-\gls{dof} is to simulate the movements of a system through time. To do so, we need some description of how the system may respond to certain forces. To describe these forces, we must declare the characteristics of our system and surroundings. This is where all known, fixed parameters of the vehicle, atmosphere, and physics are initialized to be called later in the program.

We will spend quite a while discussing how these forces arise and how we may use them to understand the subsequent motion of a vehicle. However, as many of you likely remember from your physics classes, forces acting on a rigid body (or more generally, any forces acting about a point) give rise to moments. These moments impart some angular motion on our system, which motivates the study of mathematically describing orientation due to these rotations. As it turns out, these forces and moments are tightly coupled and interact with each other, creating complex non-linear equations. This conundrum is mostly avoided through numerical integration, which allows us to approximate the motion of our vehicle governed by these derived equations. This integration scheme steps through time, calculating the motion of the vehicle through space.

We must also consider how to accurately model the aerodynamic forces such as lift and drag on our vehicle. While equations are known, certain parameters within these equations are difficult to ascertain and require substantial effort to model accurately. Finally, at the very end, we will be discussing the 6-DoF itself and the coding practices used in its construction. 

An important final step is visualize the simulation results. This is done by writing pertinent information to matrices, animating the movement of the vehicle from launch to landing, and outputting graphs of important metrics for the user to visualize. The details of this process will be discussed in more detail throughout this book.

% To help you better understand the framework, we have included a diagram below for a visualization.

\chapter{Newtonian Dynamics}\label{sec: Newtonian Dynamics}
\begin{chapquote}{Isaac Newton}
``If I have seen further than others, it is by standing upon the shoulders of giants.''
\end{chapquote}

Newtonian dynamics is the framework which allows us to understand the motion of objects due to the application of forces. This framework is very fundamental in the creation of 6-\gls{dof} models. In our case, we define Newtonian dynamics as the translational dynamics of the system as well as the moments acting on the system. Our definition strays from particle dynamics because we are considering a rigid body which can exhibit rotation. The exploration of this rotation will be explored in much more detail in \autoref{sec:energy methods}, \autoref{sec:attitude dynamics}, and Volume II and III. Here, we will introduce the basic methods used in our future analysis.
\section{The 1-DoF Case}\label{sec: The 1DoF Case}
To begin our exploration of Newtonian dynamics, we will return to the example of the 1-\gls{dof} which we mentioned in \autoref{overview: 1dof}. Our goal with this is to use a simple example that you are familiar with from high school physics and bring it up to speed with the terminology and techniques we will use for more advanced analysis.

In \autoref{fig: 1DoF forces}, we show an example of a very simple 1-\gls{dof} system free body diagram. We will use this example to explain the process of calculating the Newtonian dynamics in 1-dimension, which we then generalize to the full 3D case.

    \label{fig: 1DoF forces}
\end{figure}

A total of three forces are shown, all of which act along the $\hat{y}$-axis. Because all of the forces lie along this axis, the dynamics can be described with only scalars. This feature is what makes 1-\gls{dof} model simple. The three forces shown are the thrust force, the drag force, and the gravity force. Following what you may do in an introductory physics class, we will describe each of these forces via their functional form. These are described in \eqautoref{force table}:

\begin{equation}\label{force table}
    \begin{array}{cc}
    \mathrm{\textbf{Force}}&\mathrm{\textbf{Functional Form}}\\
    \vec{F}_g &  -mg\\
     \vec{F}_D& -\frac{1}{2} \rho_{\infty} V^2_{\infty}SC_D\\
     \vec{F}_T & F_T\cdot[1-u(t-t_b)], t\ge0
\end{array}
\end{equation}
Let's take a second to discuss the important notation used here. Terms with the subscript $\infty$ denote \gls{freestream} quantities, those that are freely flowing far away from the body of interest. Also of note is the unit step function, $u(t-t_b)$, which is a function which has value zero until reaching time $t_b$, where it thereafter equals one. The quantity $\rho_\infty$ refers to the density of the fluid, and $S$ to the \gls{ref area}. The \gls{drag coeff} is $C_D$, a term which encapsulates the very complex nature of the fluid flow around an object. Calculating the value of $C_D$ will be explored more deeply in \autoref{sec: aerodynamics}. For now, we may assume a constant value.

It is helpful to think about these quantities as vectors, because we can add them together to achieve a resultant.\endnote{More formally, we define these vectors as existing a linear space, where multiplicative and additive properties are preserved. This formal definition allows us to apply this to any arbitrary number of dimensions. For our uses, this will usually be up to 3 dimensions.} In the 1-dimensional case this is quite trivial since all vectors lie along the $\hat{y}$ unit vector, but this will become increasingly important as we move to higher dimensions.

\subsection{Numerical Integration in the 1-DoF}\label{sec: Numerical Integration in the 1DoF}

Now we will move into something that you may not have seen before. Given all the forces, we apply Newton’s 2\textsuperscript{nd} Law, $\sum{\vec{F}}=m\vec{a}$ , to arrive at an expression for the acceleration, known as an \gls{eom}, as shown in \eqref{eq2}. Doing so might look something like this:
\begin{gather}
F_T \cdot [1-u(t-t_b)]-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\infty}V_{\infty}^2SC_D-mg=m\vec{a}\label{eq1} \\
\vec{a}=\frac{F_T \cdot [1-u(t-t_b)]-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\infty}V_{\infty}^2SC_D}{m}-g\label{eq2}
\end{gather}
 From here, we use integration to find the velocity and then position of the object with time. Formally, we define this as:
 $$\vec{v}=\int{\vec{a}}{dt}$$
However, you may notice something strange with our equation. In our expression for acceleration, we need to know the velocity to compute the drag. However, we do not know the velocity without integrating the equation first. Since this dependence is non-linear, this becomes even more complicated to solve. In general, there is no known analytical solution to this paradigm! We must take a different approach to the problem. \endnote{Sometimes systems like this can be solved with traditional ODE methods. The most easy identifier is if the system is linear or not. However, most complex systems like our 6-\gls{dof} have no known analytical solutions. We will focus on numerical integration because it will be more useful in general. }


The way we resolve this is to make an approximation of the solution in a process called \gls{numerical int}. Here, we will outline the simplest type of \gls{numerical int}, known as Euler’s method. This section aims to show how to implement Euler’s method in code, and the mathematical reasoning will be given a more formal treatment in \autoref{sec:euler integration}. We will describe more complex \gls{numerical int} schemes and why you might want to use them, but it’s good to see the code for a simple case first.

Euler’s method involves discretizing our time interval into many small pieces. You may remember the concept of a Riemann sum from calculus, where you approximate an integral in discrete time steps. We will take a similar approach with Euler’s method. Euler’s method is applicable to solving first order ordinary differential equations (ODE’s). However, in our problem, we have a second order differential equation, because $\vec{a}\equiv\ddot{y}$.

To resolve this issue, we will make this one differential equation into a system of two first-order differential equations. In general, we can convert an $n^\text{th}$-order ODE into a system of $n$ first-order ODEs. In this case, we define a variable, $v=\dot{y}$. Now, we can express equation \eqref{eq2} as a system of two differential equations:

\begin{gather}\label{system}
        \dot{v}=\frac{F_T \cdot [1-u(t-t_b)]-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\infty}V_{\infty}^2SC_D}{m}-g\\
    \dot{y}=v
\end{gather}
Now, we will apply Euler’s method to solve the problem. We will use the following steps to do so, using equation \eqref{system} as our example:

1. Rewrite the system in Leibniz notation, where $\dot{x}=\frac{dx}{dt}$.

2. Perform separation of variables, to arrive at $dx=v\cdot dt$.

3. Discretize the equation by ‘converting’  $dx$ and $dt$ to $\Delta x$ and $\Delta t$.\endnote{Note that we are not ‘converting’ in the sense of an equality, since the equations are no longer equivalent when we convert a differential element into a discrete one. We are just making an approximation of the true solution. Luckily, \gls{numerical int} schemes can get us quite close to true solutions.}

4. Rewrite $\Delta x$ as $x_{new}-x_{old}$. We can rearrange the equation as $x_{new}=v\cdot \Delta t + x_{old}$

5. To start, we define an initial state. This initial state will be the first value of $x_{old}$. 

6. Iterate through values of $\Delta t$ until the desired time of simulation is achieved.

What we have done is formally called writing a \gls{difference equation}.  Not only is this important for numerical integration, but in digital processing, because computers must discretize a signal! We will see these equation again when we study practical control systems in Volume III.

We follow the same process to write the \gls{difference equation} for the integration of acceleration to find the velocity. Of note, we will use the previous value of $\vec{v}$ in the computation of the new acceleration. 
\begin{example}[Discretizing the velocity equation]
Since the position-velocity relation is quite simple in this situation (it almost always will be) it is helpful to see how we discretize the equation for the velocity-acceleration relationship. We will follow the same steps as before. We will, however, reduce the equation $$\frac{F_T \cdot [1-u(t-t_b)]-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\infty}V_{\infty}^2SC_D}{m}$$
to the more compact form $$\frac{F_T -F_D}{m}$$
We can then start with the same steps as before.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Rewrite in the Liebniz notations: $$\frac{dv}{dt}=\frac{F_T-F_D}{m}-g$$
    \item Perform separation of variables:
$$dv=\left(\frac{F_T-F_D}{m}-g\right)dt$$
We notice that $F_D$ has a dependence on $v$, but there is no way to isolate it to the other side of the equation. We will just leave it as is.
    \item Next, we discretize the equation:
    $$\Delta v=\left(\frac{F_T-F_D}{m}-g\right)\Delta t$$
    \item We expand the $\Delta v$. We leave $\Delta t$ the same since that is our timestep. Generally, we want to write an equation that has $v_{new}$ isolated on the LHS of the equation. Starting with the expansion, we have:$$v_{new}-v_{old}=\left(\frac{F_T-F_D}{m}-g\right)\Delta t$$
We remember that $F_D$ is a function of $v$. We will show this by writing $F_D=\kappa v^2$. The variable $\kappa$ is used since all other parts of the drag are independent of velocity. Since we don't know $v_{new}$, we have to use $v_{old}$ as the value of $v$ in the drag equation. So, we can do a rearrangement of the equation to arrive at the final form:
$$v_{new}=\left(\frac{F_T-\kappa {v_{old}}^2}{m}-g\right)\Delta t+v_{old}$$
Lastly, we define $v_0$ to be 0 for most cases. The iteration through values of $\Delta t$ is the exact same as before. As we can see, the only real difference seen is that we use the value of $v_{old}$ in our drag expression! Otherwise, this integration looks nearly identical to the example with velocity.
\end{enumerate}

We should note that this method of numerical integration is not used too often in practical applications. However, writing difference equations \textit{is} quite important, so it's still a good skill to have nonetheless. In future applications of numerical integration, the most important skill to have is to be able to construct the \gls{eom} and to separate the equation into a system of first order equations.
\end{example}

If you still feel slightly unsure about some details, note that a more formal definition of Euler’s method is given in \autoref{sec:euler integration}. This may help clear up any confusion here. We also show this Euler integrator example in code below. Note that $\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\infty}SC_D$ is called $k$ in the script for simplicity. This simplification assumes that $\rho_{\infty}$ and the $C_D$ are constant.

\newpage
\begin{matlabscript}{Euler 1-DoF}
% Euler Method 1DoF

%constants:
m = 1;    % mass [kg]
g = 9.8;  % gravity [m/s^2]
k = 1e-3; % drag const [kg/m]
tb = 5;   % thrust time [s]
Ft = 100; % force of thrust [N]

% define constant forces
Fg = -m*g;

% pos, vel and time init:
x = 0; v = 0;
t = 0;

% array init:
i = 1;

% timestep
dt = 0.01; 

while v >= 0
    % variables updated every loop
    Fd = -k*v(i)^2;
    Ft = Ft * (1-heaviside(t(i)-tb));

    % Euler Integration:
    a(i+1) = Ft+Fg+Fd;
    v(i+1) = a(i)*dt + v(i);
    x(i+1) = v(i+1)*dt + x(i);

    % Iteration update
    i = i+1;
    t(i) = i*dt;
end
\end{matlabscript}

There are a few important things to note about the MATLAB implementation of the script. Firstly, the only force that is defined outside of the loop is gravity, because the magnitude and direction of this force is independent of the current state of the system. We contrast this with the drag force and the thrust, which must be calculated on every iteration through the algorithm to find an updated value for the force. We also note the use of the "heaviside" function. This is functionally identical to a unit step function, just a different notation.

\section{Frame of Reference}\label{sec:Frame of Reference}
Before attempting to describe movement in multiple dimensions, we should first discuss how we can describe important vector quantities using various reference frames. Later, it will become apparent that a problem is easier to solve when using certain reference frames. 

A frame of reference is an \gls{orthonormal} set within three-dimensional space. The \gls{orthonormal} nature of the basis vectors that create every frame of reference allows for any other vector in 3-dimensional space to be described as a linear combination of those three basis vectors (hence why we call it a basis for 3-space). This tool becomes incredibly useful within the study of Newtonian and attitude dynamics, as every arbitrary vector can be broken down into three set ``scalar'' equations. Naturally, the question arises of how we determine which basis vectors to utilize, and so we will be discussing a few frames of reference critical to the creation of a 6-\gls{dof} and aerospace engineering as a whole.

\subsection{The Inertial Frame}

The first and most important frame of reference is the \gls{inertial frame}. Typically denoted by $e$ or $i$, or in our case $(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z})$, this motionless frame is the one that allows for the use of Newton’s second law in the first place, as the frame itself has ``zero'' translational and rotational movement.\endnote{An \gls{inertial frame} can have some constant velocity and still be inertial. Of course, the earth has some velocity around the sun and with respect to the distant stars which we can approximate as constant for our time scales and thus ignore.} We say “zero” as this motion must be negligible in relation to the vehicle of interest. For example, our specific 6-\gls{dof} sets the \gls{inertial frame} to be anchored within the Earth on the launch pad. And while we are well aware the Earth is moving and rotating through space at a non-zero rate, this motion is negligible relative to the motion of our vehicle which remains within the Earth's atmosphere for a short period of time. Therefore, we can assume that the Earth acts appropriately as an \gls{inertial frame} of reference, and Newton’s second law is valid for this frame. 

Returning to the specific example of a 6-\gls{dof}, the directions you set your basis vectors to point in are mostly left to the coder’s discretion. If your chosen directions retain the \gls{orthonormal} nature of a frame of reference, as well as obeying the \gls{rhr}, the choice is all yours. Within the example we are following, our \gls{inertial frame}’s origin is anchored within the launch pad as previously stated. We follow a standard East-North-Up (ENU) local geodetic frame. The first basis vector points East, the second points North, and the third points up. An image for quick reference is provided in \autoref{fig:InertialFrame}:

\centering

\subsection{The Body Frame}

The second most important frame of reference is the \gls{body frame}. Typically denoted by $b$, or in our case $(\hat{X},\hat{Y},\hat{Z})$, this frame of reference is always anchored at some point in the vehicle and allows us to describe the \gls{state vector} in relation to the vehicle itself. This frame is moving and rotating with respect to the \gls{inertial frame}. For every \gls{body frame} you create – from a frame for the entire vehicle to a frame for a small electronic part – you want simplicity. This means that in aerospace, it is often the case for a general \gls{body frame} to have a basis vector pointing along the longitudinal axis of the vehicle, as well as one out a wing. A generic image of such a convention is provided in \autoref{fig:BodyFrameTypical}. In rotational dynamics, the offset of each basis vector in the \gls{body frame} will correspond to the pitch, yaw, and roll of the vehicle. But more on that later.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
    
\includegraphics[width=0.65\linewidth]{Images/TypicalBodyFrame.png}
    \caption{Typical Body Frame Orientation}
    \label{fig:BodyFrameTypical}
\end{figure}

For our current model, the \gls{body frame} of our 6-\gls{dof} has its origin in the nose of the vehicle. It has the first body basis vector, $\hat{X}$, pointing up along the longitudinal axis of the rocket following standard convention. The second basis vector, $\hat{Y}$, points right due East when on the launch pad, and the third, $\hat{Z}$, points due North on the launch pad. We use the cardinal directions of the launchpad as there are no conventional wings on the rocket. It also allows for a simple initial rotation to orient the vehicle pointing up on the launch pad with respect to the inertial frame. 

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.6\linewidth]{Images/RocketBodyFrame.png}
    \caption{Body Frame Convention of our Rocket}
    \label{fig:BodyFrameRocket}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The Wind Frame}

The final important frame of reference is the \gls{wind frame}. Much like the body frame, this frame of reference is fixed at some point within the vehicle. Unlike the body frame, it is not defined using the geometry of the vehicle, rather it is initialized with respect to the freestream velocity. As such, the first basis vector of this frame of reference is aligned with the velocity of the vehicle itself. A typical representation of the wind frame on an aircraft is shown below:






The wind frame is certainly less intuitive than the body frame. 


they are offset from one another by the angle of attack and sideslip. 




\section{Derivative Transport}
The last issue that we tackle when different reference frames get involved is the fact that objects are moving around and accelerating. We can create various reference frames, but to correctly apply Newton's second law, we must use our inertial frame as the original reference!

Consider, for example, riding in your car. When your car turns around a sharp corner, you feel a force pushing you outward. We call such forces `fictitious', because they are the result of the observer (you) being in a non-inertial frame. This is good motivation for us to understand how to express these accelerations and forces in an inertial reference frame, even when our measurements and data come from a non-inertial frame.

A reference frame may very well translate a vector from one frame to another, but transforming a derivative requires a bit more subtlety.  The tool we use to do this `derivative frame translation' is called derivative transport, or the \gls{bke}. The \gls{bke}, then, is just a tool that we use to convert derivatives between various reference frames.

\subsection{The Frisbee}
    To understand the \gls{bke}, let us consider a scenario. You are out on a lovely day with your dog, Hans. Hans loves to play Frisbee with you. Of course, when you throw a Frisbee, you apply some spin to make sure the Frisbee remains stable as it is flying. The motion of the Frisbee is now quite complex. There is translational motion, but the Frisbee is also rotating about its vertical axis. What should you do if you want to find the velocity of a point at the edge of the Frisbee?

    We will use some of our tools with reference frames to help us. We will establish an inertially fixed frame, $e$. This frame will stand still and gives us a `ruler' to measure everything against. We also want a reference frame that is embedded in the frisbee, which we may call $\hat{a}$. We can embed the $a$-frame in the Frisbee such that $\hat{e}_3$ and $\hat{a}_3$ are both pointing out of the page. We can construct a visual of this, as shown in \autoref{fig:frisbee}. In this figure, we can see that the Frisbee has some rotation with respect to the $e$-frame.

Our end objective is to describe the position, velocity, and acceleration of this point $P$ on the Frisbee. We may start by describing the position, which is the easiest to handle. We can do this by expressing the location of point $A$ from the origin in the $e$-frame, and then go the rest of the way in the $a$-frame. That is to say, we can describe the position of the center of mass ($A$) and then it is simply a translation along the $\hat{a}_1$-direction to arrive at point $P$. Doing so would look like:
$$\vec{r}^{\ OP}=\vec{r}^{\ OA} + \vec{r}^{\ AP}$$
Let us attach some variables to this to make it more tangible. Say we have thrown the Frisbee directly north, along the $\hat{e}_2$ direction, by $30 \mathrm{\ m}$. This gives us $\vec{r}^{\ OA}$, which is $30 \hat{e}_2$. $\vec{r}^{\ AP}$ is computed similarly. Taking $R$ as the radius, this vector is $R\hat{a}_1$. Altogether:
$$\vec{r}^{\ OP}=30\hat{e}_2 + R\hat{a}_1$$

Some complication comes when we try to express the velocity of point $P$. We know that the velocity is the time derivative of the position, but our position is being described using non-inertial coordinates in the $\hat{a}$ frame!

Using our standard derivative, there is no machinery that allows us to express this motion in the $a$-frame. We can solve this by computing the rotation of one reference frame with respect to the other. This will give us the ability to compute a valid velocity.

Going back to our Frisbee, we can notice that $a$ and $e$ are offset by some angle. If we know this angle, and the rate at which it is changing, we can understand the relationship between the $a$ and the $e$ frame.

So we have an angle between the two frames, $\theta$, and an angular rate, $\omega=\dot{\theta}$. We want to find an expression for the infinitesimal change in $a$.

% Text Node
\draw (377,163.4) node [anchor=north west][inner sep=0.75pt]  [color={rgb, 255:red, 218; green, 44; blue, 0 }  ,opacity=1 ]  {$r$};
% Text Node
\draw (231,162.4) node [anchor=north west][inner sep=0.75pt]  [color={rgb, 255:red, 74; green, 144; blue, 226 }  ,opacity=1 ]  {$\omega $};
% Text Node
\draw (487,122.4) node [anchor=north west][inner sep=0.75pt]    {$\dot{r}$};


\end{tikzpicture}

    
    \caption{Infinitesimal rotation}
    \label{fig:inf rot}
\end{figure}
Considering an infinitesimal rotation, $r$ will be displaced orthogonal to its current direction due to the rotation by $\omega$. We can see that this $\dot{r}$ is orthogonal to both $r$ and $\omega$ (which is pointing out of the page). Following the \gls{rhr}, this direction must be given by $\omega\times\vec{r}$. This cross product also gives us an appropriate magnitude for the vector, as the magnitude will increase with a larger $\omega$ or if the length of the lever arm, $r$, increases. There are more fundamental reasons for this cross-product relationship, but these will have to wait until Volume II and III to be explained by Lie theory.\endnote{If you are curious, this relationship is a result of the rotation group $\text{SO}(3)$, where the Lie bracket is the cross product. We can consider both the rotation and translation of the body with the group $\text{SE}(3)$.}

With the rotation considered, the rest is quite simple. We must consider the change of $r$ itself, so we consider the derivative of $r$ with respect to the $a$-frame to do so. Altogether, we have the full \gls{bke}:
\begin{gather}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{
    \color{black}{}^e\frac{d}{dt}\vec{r}={}^a\frac{d}{dt}{\vec{r}}+{}^e\vec{\omega}^a\times\vec{r}}\\
    \color{boxBlue}\mathrm{Basic \ Kinematic\ Equation}
\end{gather}
Note that the superscript in front of the derivative tells us which frame that derivative is in. The first term reads `the inertial derivative of $r$ '. Our second term is `the derivative of $r$ in the $a$-frame'. Lastly, $^e\vec{\omega}^a$ tells us that this term is the angular velocity of $a$ with respect to $e$. This is just notation, nothing profound!

In general, we can use the \gls{bke} to express how frame translations affect the derivatives of states. We can perform analysis using multiple intermediate frames to build a sequence of rotations, or apply the \gls{bke} twice to get an acceleration in an inertial frame.
\newpage
\begin{exercise}[BKE Practice]\label{exer:bke_practice}
We use the \gls{bke} often when describing the motions of sensors in our rocket or aircraft. Let's consider a similar `toy scenario' where we can apply the \gls{bke}.

A small bug steps onto the middle of a gyroscope (point $O$), which is spinning at a constant rate $\omega$. The bug walks outwards at a constant rate $v$ towards the edge of the gyroscope in the rotating gyroscope frame, $b$. The bugs location is denoted by point $P$. The $b$-frame is fixed to the rotation of the disk and the $e$-frame is inertial. The bug walks directly along the $\hat{b}_1$-direction. This situation is shown in \autoref{fig:bug gyro}.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Write the bugs position vector, $\vec{r}^{\ OP}$. 
    \item Apply the \gls{bke} to find the bugs velocity in the inertial frame. Apply the \gls{bke} again to find the inertial acceleration.
    \item Assume a rotation rate of $100 \text{ rad/s}$ and a bug speed of $1 \text{ cm/s}$. What force must the bugs feet exert to stay attached to the gyroscope when the bug  is $3 \text{ cm}$ from the middle? The bug weighs $1\text{ gram}$. (Gravity may be ignored).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{exercise}[BKE in Code]\label{exer:bke_code}
Using MATLAB or Python, create a function which takes the input of a vector $\vec{r}$ and $^e\vec{\omega}^a$ and computes the inertial derivative of this vector. This function will have to use symbolic variables so that the derivative may be computed analytically.
\end{exercise}


\section{The 3-DoF Case}\label{sec: 3DoF Case}
For particle dynamics, the most complex case is full 3D translational motion. Luckily, nothing very fundamental changes compared to the 1-\gls{dof} case. The most important difference is the use of vector notation for compactness. For position ($\vec{r}$), velocity ($\vec {v}$), and acceleration ($\vec{a}$), we represent them as tri-dimensional column vectors:

\begin{gather}
    \vec{r}=\begin{bmatrix}
        x_1\\
        x_2\\
        x_3
    \end{bmatrix},
    \vec{v}=\begin{bmatrix}
        \dot{x}_1\\
        \dot{x}_2\\
        \dot{x}_3\\
    \end{bmatrix},
        \vec{a}=\begin{bmatrix}
        \ddot{x}_1\\
        \ddot{x}_2\\
        \ddot{x}_3\\
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{gather}
This vector representation is additionally used to facilitate the use of matrix operations for translation between reference frames. For this reason, expressing these elements in vector form will become crucial as we move forward.

\subsubsection{Vectors in Code}
In code, we express a tri-dimensional column vector as:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
pos=[0;0;0];
\end{matlabscript}

Where each semicolon represents a new row of the vector. We can do a similar operation in Python using $\texttt{numpy}$, and each group of brackets represents a new row.
\begin{pythonscript}{}
import numpy as np
pos = np.array([[0],[0],[0]])
\end{pythonscript}

We define a row vector by using a comma instead of a semicolon: 
\begin{matlabscript}{}
pos=[0,0,0];
\end{matlabscript}
Or in Python:
\begin{pythonscript}{}
pos = np.array([[0,0,0]])
\end{pythonscript}

Often, it is useful to convert between a row and column vector because some functions will expect the input to be in a different format. We can do so with a transposition. This looks like: 
\begin{matlabscript}{}
pos=[0,0,0]';
\end{matlabscript}
\begin{pythonscript}{}
pos = np.array([[0,0,0]]).T
\end{pythonscript}

We have to be a bit careful with $\texttt{numpy}$. If we had instead input `$\texttt{np.array([0,0,0]).T}$' this transpose operation would not work. We need the second set of brackets to explicitly say that this array is a vector and not just a list of numbers. When we wrap the whole expression in another set of brackets it tells $\texttt{numpy}$ that our vector should live in a two-dimensional space, like $\mathbb{R}^{2\times 1}$. We have to explicitly inform $\texttt{numpy}$ of array dimensions via the brackets, which is a bit different than MATLAB.

\subsubsection{Newton's Laws with Vectors}

Expressed in vector notation, we write Newton's 2nd Law in the 3D case as $\vec{F}=m{}^i\ddot{\vec{r}}^{\ op}$. The presence of $i$ indicates that this acceleration vector must be in the \gls{inertial frame}. The quantity ${}^i\ddot{\vec{r}}^{\ op}$ can be deduced by standard differentiation when operating in an inertial frame, or by using \gls{bke}.

Nothing functional changes about Newton's laws when we move to this 3D space. All we have to do is solve the equations along each of our dimensions. We could in fact expand $\vec{F}=m{}^i\ddot{\vec{r}}^{\ op}$ and get three equations for our system:
\begin{gather}
    F_x=m^i\ddot{r}_x\\
    F_y=m^i\ddot{r}_y\\
    F_z=m^i\ddot{r}_z
\end{gather}
Notice that using the vector notation we described above, we can neatly package these three equations into one vector equation.

As compared to the 1-\gls{dof} case, we have three new quantities that are present. The first of these is $V_{\infty}$, the \gls{freestream} airflow vector.\endnote{\Gls{freestream} velocity  is drawn in the direction of incoming air by convention. However, for calculations, we will use the convention that $\vec{V}$ is the direction of the velocity of the vehicle (opposite sign to drawing). These direction of the vector is only dependent on the reference frame of the observer.} This is represented diagrammatically because it is no longer constrained along the $\hat{y}$ direction.

When we have an angle between the nose (the direction through which the thrust force points in \autoref{fig:3 DoF Forces}) and the \gls{freestream} velocity, we refer to this as an \gls{angle of attack}. This \gls{angle of attack} is often denoted with the letter $\alpha$. We will discuss \gls{angle of attack} and its effects more in \autoref{sec: aerodynamics}. For now, we simply need to understand how to calculate $\alpha$ given the vector through the nose and the \gls{freestream} velocity vector. We show the \gls{angle of attack} in \autoref{fig:AoA}.

    \centering

% Text Node
\draw (168,160) node [anchor=east] [inner sep=0.75pt]    {$V_{\infty }$};
% Text Node
\draw (189,124.4) node [anchor=north west][inner sep=0.75pt]  [color={rgb, 255:red, 74; green, 144; blue, 226 }  ,opacity=1 ]  {$\alpha $};


\end{tikzpicture}

    \caption{Angle of Attack}
    \label{fig:AoA}
\end{figure}

To do this, we use the fact that the dot product is related to the cosine of the angle between the vectors. Denoting the vector through the nose of the rocket $\hat{X}$, we can find the \gls{angle of attack} as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:alpha}
    \alpha=cos^{-1}\left(\frac{\hat{X}\cdot \vec{V}_{\infty}}{||\hat{X}||\ ||\vec{V}_{\infty}||}\right)
\end{equation}

The next new quantity is $\vec{F}_L$, the force of lift. The force of lift is always perpendicular in direction to ${V}_{\infty}$. Knowing this, we can find the direction of the force of lift as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Lift}
    \hat{L}=\frac{({V}_{\infty} \times \hat{X}) \times {V}_{\infty}}{||({V}_{\infty} \times \hat{X}) \times {V}_{\infty}||}
\end{equation}
Here, the cross product is used because it generates a vector orthogonal to the two input vectors. This is exactly the property that we need when defining lift. For the sake of completeness, we should also note that the force of drag lies opposite the direction of $\vec{V}_{\infty}$. This is much simpler to calculate, and looks like:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:drag}
\hat{D}=\frac{-\vec{V}_{\infty}}{||\vec{V}_{\infty}||}
\end{equation}
The last force directions to define are the direction of thrust and the direction of gravity. Luckily, these are easily defined because they point directly along basis vectors.\endnote{We assume here that there is no misalignment in the thrust vector and it is coincident with the  vector. A more complex formula can be used when thrust misalignment is present, but this geometrical argument is not shown here.} The force of gravity is defined with respect to the inertial reference frame and is defined in \eqref{eq:Fg}. The force of thrust is defined with respect to the vector pointing through the nose, $\hat{X}$, and is shown in \eqref{eq:Ft}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Fg}
    \vec{F}_g=-mg\cdot \hat{y}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ft}
    \vec{F}_T=F_T\cdot \hat{X}
\end{equation}
\subsection{Moments}\label{sec:moments}
The last new quantity is the moment about point $A$, denoted $\vec{M}^A$. For now, we will simply note that for an arbitrary selection of point $A$, we will have a rotational moment that is generated because the forces on the body do not necessarily act through point $A$. In \autoref{fig:3 DoF Forces}, we have drawn all the forces acting through a single point for simplicity, but this is generally not the case.

For our rocket modeling, it is generally assumed that gravity and thrust act about the center of gravity, and the aerodynamic forces about the center of pressure. We will show how to compute this moment if the location of the center of gravity and the center of pressure is known. To show this, we will briefly return to the 2D case for illustration, but keep in mind that this concept is extensible to 3D. We show this in \autoref{fig:Moments}.

In \autoref{fig:Moments} we include a few new symbols. All force magnitudes, however, are equivalent to what we show in Figure 2 (with the assumption that they lie in the x-y plane for this example). We denote the location of the center of gravity as $\vec{r}_{cg}$ and the location of the center of pressure as $\vec{r}_{cp}$.\endnote{The different notation of a subscript is used because this is not a standard position vector from one point to another, they rather denote the center of mass and center of pressure.} The difference between $\vec{r}_{cg}$ and $\vec{r}_{cp}$ is denoted as $\vec{r}_{ma}$. We refer to this as the moment arm of the aerodynamic forces. It should be noted that of these are tridimensional vectors. 

We also define a new vector, $\vec{F}_{Aero}$, the resultant aerodynamic vector, which is the vector sum of $\vec{F}_D$ and $\vec{F}_L$. Because free rotations occur about the center of mass, it is most helpful to calculate our final moment from this location. To do so, we use the moment equation:
\begin{gather}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\vec{M}^{cg}=\vec{r}_{ma}\times \vec{F}_{Aero}}
\end{gather}

We note that the direction of this moment is in the $-\hat{z}$ direction from the \gls{rhr}. If we assume that our forces are planar, this moment will generate a rotation about a single axis.

One thing to note is that some quantities are more easily defined with respect to the body frame. A good example is \eqautoref{eq:alpha} and \eqautoref{eq:Lift}. As such, we will need a method in which we can convert between the body and \glspl{inertial frame}. This conversion between frames is explored in \autoref{sec:TheDCM}. We should also note that our moments will be in this \gls{body frame} as well.

The last thing that we note in this section is that our forces and moments are coupled. This means that the aerodynamic forces are a function of the orientation of the vehicle, so the moments and forces must be solved as a system.
\subsection{State Vector}
Let us briefly discuss the concept of a \gls{state vector}. The \gls{state vector} is a column vector that contains information on the current state of our system. The state vector tells us everything we need to know to predict the future of our system. In the example of our 3-\gls{dof}, we will include the position, velocity, rotation, and rotation rate of the system in the \gls{state vector}. Given this state vector for a 3-\gls{dof}, we can completely determine the future behavior of the system. The state vector is not unique. In the 3-\gls{dof} case, our state vector may look like: \endnote{The order of the state vector does not particularly matter, so long as we are consistent with the location of the elements. Typically, it is common to write all vectors first and then their derivatives, but there is no hard rule. We follow this convention in our discussion.}
$$\textbf{X}=\begin{bmatrix}
    x\\y\\\theta\\\dot{x}\\\dot{y}\\\dot{\theta}
\end{bmatrix}$$
It is also useful to define the derivative of our \gls{state vector}, which would just be the derivative of each of its elements. This is expressed as
$$\dot{\textbf{X}}=\begin{bmatrix}
    \dot{x}\\\dot{y}\\\dot{\theta}\\\ddot{x}\\\ddot{y}\\\ddot{\theta}
\end{bmatrix}$$
For the sake of compactness, these state vectors are generally not written in their full form. In this text, we will often refer to a \gls{state vector} as just $\vec{X}$ or $\textbf{X}$. Note that we also use the vector $\hat{X}$ to denote a unit vector in the \gls{body frame}, so the difference in the hat becomes an important distinction here.
\section{Euler Rotation Equations}\label{Euler Rotation Equations}

In \autoref{sec:moments} we have explored the moments that are created when a force acts at a point that is not through the center of mass. Here, we will use that fact to make the jump from particle dynamics into \gls{rigid body} dynamics. In analogy to Newton's 2nd Law of motion, there is an equally important relationship for rotational dynamics. This equation is known as Euler's 2nd Law:
\begin{gather}\label{eq:Euler 2nd Law}
\color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\vec{M}^o=\frac{{}^id\vec{H}^o}{dt}}\\
\mathrm{\color{boxBlue}Euler's \ 2nd \ Law}
\end{gather}
This equation reads as ``The moment about point $O$ is the inertial time derivative of the angular momentum, H, about point $O$.'' The superscript $i$ refers to the fact that this derivative must be taken in the \gls{inertial frame}. Notice that a moment is $\vec{r}\times \vec{F}$, and $\vec{H}=\vec{r}\times m\vec{v}$. This equation is in some sense $\vec{r}\times(\text{Newton's 2nd Law)}$, if you want a conceptual understanding of the equation. 

We also have the relationship between angular momentum, $\vec{H}$, and the \gls{moment of inertia}, $I$, for a \gls{rigid body}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Iw}
    {}^i\vec{H}={}^i(I\vec{\omega})
\end{equation}
This equation is an exact analogy to the linear momentum, $\vec{p}=m\vec{v}$. Through this analogy, we see that $I$ is roughly equivalent to $m$. It tells us how much resistance there is to rotation. You may also notice that our \gls{moment of inertia} here is defined with respect to the \gls{inertial frame}. As a result, when our body changes orientation, the \gls{moment of inertia} will be expressed differently! Often however, it is more helpful to define this \gls{moment of inertia} in terms of our \gls{body frame}. We can soon rewrite this equation so that $I$ can be expressed in body coordinates. For now, let's define $I$ more rigorously.

\subsection{Moment of Inertia and Inertia Tensor}\label{moment of inertia}
The \gls{moment of inertia} of a body is a measure of its resistance to rotational acceleration. It is in essence, the rotational analogue of mass (mass is a measure of resistance to linear acceleration, by the same line of reasoning). Since we need the \gls{moment of inertia} to describe our relationship for angular momentum in \eqref{eq:Iw}, it is useful for us to see its derivation here.

We can start by showing that the mass of a region can be found by integrating the density over its volume. Mathematically, we show this as:
$$\iiint\limits_V{\rho}{dV}$$
A brief aside: To be very explicit and precise, we should expand this $dV$ as $dxdydz$ and note that $\rho$ is a function of $x,y,z$ coordinates. Written in full, this integral is:
$$\iiint\limits_V{\rho(x,y,z)}{dxdydz}$$
From here, we'll go back to the $dV$ notation, but remember that we are really doing an integral over all three coordinates.

Similarly, we know that the \gls{moment of inertia} of a particle is $mr^2$, which is it's mass multiplied with the length of the moment arm, $r$, squared. This is often referred to as the `second moment' of mass. By integrating this second moment, we have:
$$\iiint\limits_V{\rho}{r^2}{dV}$$
This is the equivalent of $I=\sum mr^2$ in the continuous limit. We can thus apply the integral equation for distributed bodies, like a rocket.

It is useful to define the \gls{moment of inertia} along each body axis direction. We can rewrite $r$ in terms of $x$, $y$, and $z$ for each of the component directions:\endnote{We use the fact here that $r^2=x^2+y^2+z^2$.}
\begin{gather}\label{eq:moi}
    I_{xx}=\iiint\limits_V{\rho}{\left(y^2+z^2\right)}{dV}\\
    I_{yy}=\iiint\limits_V{\rho}{\left(x^2+z^2\right)}{dV}\\
    I_{zz}=\iiint\limits_V{\rho}{\left(x^2+y^2\right)}{dV}
\end{gather}
For our rocket, we will find that these are the only quantities that we need to define. We also take define these quantities with respect to the center of mass since this is the point about which free rotations occur. Our coordinate system should have the origin at the center of mass for these calculations!

In general, we also have three more quantities, which are called the products of inertia. These are defined by the product of two of our elements, such as $xy$. This will have the same dimensions as the quantity $r^2$, so it can be applied in a similar way to the \gls{moment of inertia}. The products of inertia are defined as follows:
\begin{gather}
    I_{xy}=I_{yx}=\iiint\limits_V{-xy\rho}{dV}\\
    I_{xz}=I_{zx}=\iiint\limits_V{-xz\rho}{dV}\\
    I_{yz}=I_{zy}=\iiint\limits_V{-yz\rho}{dV}
\end{gather}
For most rockets, because of the symmetry about the $\hat{X}$ axis, these quantities go to zero. However, more complex models may include fuel slosh or mass asymmetries. In that case, the products of inertia are not zero and must be calculated.

We express our \gls{inertia tensor}, denoted $I$, as the $3\times3$ matrix which contains the moments and products of inertia.\endnote{This quantity is called a tensor because the elements of the matrix transform in a specific way under a coordinate transformation. The inertia tensor itself never changes, just the way in which we represent it.}
$$I=\begin{bmatrix}
    I_{xx}&I_{xy}&I_{xz}\\
    I_{yx}&I_{yy}&I_{yz}\\
    I_{zx}&I_{zy}&I_{zz}
\end{bmatrix}$$
For a simple axisymmetric rocket, we get the simplification that:
$$I=\begin{bmatrix}
    I_{xx}&0&0\\
    0&I_{yy}&0\\
    0&0&I_{zz}
\end{bmatrix}$$

In fact, we can always find axes such that the products of inertia are zero. These are called the \gls{principal axes} and are important in many applications, including structures.\footnote{See \cite{baker_statics_2020}.} For our rocket, it just happens that these \gls{principal axes} are the ones we have chosen to define as our \gls{body frame}. The \gls{moment of inertia} along these \gls{principal axes} are solutions to the eigenvalue problem $I\vec{\omega}=I^*\vec{\omega}$, where $I^*$ is a scalar. Essentially, we are finding a matrix $I$ such that the output is just a scalar multiple of the input, $\omega$. We can see that having this property will greatly simplify our results. This is another reason why we like to perform many calculations in the \gls{body frame}. Using the \gls{inertia tensor}, a matrix form of \eqref{eq:Iw}  is written as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IwVec}
    \vec{H}=I\vec{\omega}
\end{equation}
In the simplification using the \gls{principal axes}, we often refer to the terms $I_{xx}$, $I_{yy}$, and $I_{zz}$ simply as $I_x$, $I_y$, and $I_z$. These are called the \glspl{pmoi}.

\subsection{Parallel Axis Theorem}
Similar to the way in which we described the \gls{moment of inertia} of a particle to be $mr^2$, we can describe the change in \gls{moment of inertia} of a body due to the offset of its axis. We accomplish this with the \gls{par axis thm}, which relates the moment and products of inertia through different axes of rotation. To use \gls{par axis thm}, we simply take the known \gls{moment of inertia} and add $mr^2$ along that component direction. This looks like:
$$I_{x'x'}=I_{xx}+m\left(y^2+z^2\right)$$
This is similarly computed for the other axes, with the $r$ always being the axis that is not the one we are computing the \gls{moment of inertia} of. We can think of this intuitively because we are translating the center along the two axes perpendicular to the one describing the \gls{moment of inertia}.

We must also do the same for the products of inertia. We note that even if we select body axes with no products of inertia, we may still have them in the translated center! The \gls{par axis thm} for the products of inertia is given as:
$$I_{x'y'}=I_{xy}-mxy$$
This is similarly computed for the other axes.
\subsection{Rotations in the Body Frame}\label{sec:rotations in the body frame}
Now that we have defined our \gls{inertia tensor} in the \gls{body frame}, it is useful to reexamine \eqref{eq:Euler 2nd Law} and \eqref{eq:Iw} to express them in terms of the \gls{body frame}. We can express equation \eqref{eq:Euler 2nd Law} in terms of the \gls{body frame} using the \gls{bke}. We write this as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bke}
{}^i\frac{d\vec{H}^o}{dt}={}^b\frac{d\vec{H}^o}{dt}+{}^e\vec{\omega}^b\times\vec{H}^o
\end{equation}
In the case of \gls{principal axes}, \eqref{eq:IwVec} can be simplified to yield:
$${}^iH^o=I_x\omega_x\hat{b}_1+I_y\omega_y\hat{b}_2+I_z\omega_z\hat{b}_3$$
Plugging this result into the RHS of \eqref{eq:bke}, we get the following:
$${}^b\frac{d}{dt}\left(I_x\omega_x\hat{b}_1+I_y\omega_y\hat{b}_2+I_z\omega_z\hat{b}_3\right)+{}^e\vec{\omega}^b\times \left(I_x\omega_x\hat{b}_1+I_y\omega_y\hat{b}_2+I_z\omega_z\hat{b}_3\right)$$
Where ${}^e\vec{\omega}^b$ is $\omega_x\hat{b}_1+\omega_y\hat{b}_2+\omega_z\hat{b}_3$. Assuming a rigid body along with a principal axis system, we say the time derivative of our inertia tensor is zero. The algebraic manipulation and vector math here is left as an exercise for the reader. Collecting the final terms, our final expression is:
$${}^i\vec{H}^o=\left[I_x\dot{\omega}_x+\left(I_z-I_y\right)\right]\hat{b}_1+\left[I_y\dot{\omega}_y+\left(I_x-I_z\right)\right]\hat{b}_2+\left[I_z\dot{\omega}_z+\left(I_y-I_x\right)\right]\hat{b}_3$$
Equating this with the moments in the \gls{body frame} that were found in \autoref{sec:moments} as ${}^b\vec{M}^o=M_x\hat{b}_1+M_y\hat{b}_2+M_z\hat{b}_3$, we arrive at the final form of our equations for Euler rotations\endnote{Very observant readers may notice that this form of the equation is technically incorrect for a rocket, since our center of rotation, $O$, will change throughout the flight as the center of mass changes position. Moreover, the moments will change as fuel is drained, so another derivative should exist. We will have some small effects that are not accounted for in this equation. For our purposes here, these are ignored for simplicity of presentation. However, a more general approach that implements this is used in our modeling.}:
\begin{gather}\label{eq: moment equations}
\color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\begin{array}{ll}
     M_x=I_x\dot{\omega}_x+\left(I_z-I_y\right)\omega_y\omega_z
     & \\M_y=I_y\dot{\omega}_y+\left(I_x-I_z\right)\omega_x\omega_z\
     &\\ M_z=I_z\dot{\omega}_z+\left(I_y-I_x\right)\omega_y\omega_x
\end{array}}
\\\mathrm{\color{boxBlue}Euler \ Rigid \ Body \ Equations}
\end{gather}
We can rearrange this to a form that is more useful for us (although somewhat less sightly), putting the unknown $\dot\omega$ ($\vec{\alpha}$) quantities alone:
\begin{gather}\label{eq: moment eq 2}
    \dot{\omega}_x=\frac{M_x-\left(I_z-I_y\right)\omega_y\omega_z}{I_x}\\
    \dot{\omega}_y=\frac{M_y-\left(I_x-I_z\right)\omega_z\omega_x}{I_y}\\
    \dot{\omega}_z=\frac{M_z-\left(I_y-I_x\right)\omega_y\omega_x}{I_z}
\end{gather}
These sets of equations are especially powerful because they allow us to numerically integrate the angular velocity. Our moments can be found using Newtonian dynamics and the \glspl{pmoi} are quantities that are easily found using the integrals described above in \autoref{moment of inertia}. We will use these angular velocity derivatives extensively to describe the time rate of change of the attitude of the rocket.

\section{Applying Rotation Equations}
We've just laid out a large amount of material, so it is best to show a few examples to get the gist of things.

\subsubsection{Moments of Inertia}
To start analyzing the rotations, we'll first need to compute the moments of inertia, as these will define the `rotational mass' of the satellite. Since the satellite is cylindrical, we have symmetry about the $\hat{x}$-direction. So, we will convert into polar coordinates, which gives the following relations:
$$\begin{array}{l}
    {x}={x}\\
    {y}=r\cos\theta\\
    {z}=r\sin\theta
\end{array}$$
We can use these relations in our moment of inertia integrals \eqref{eq:moi}. We'll start by computing the moment in the $\hat{x}$-direction, substituting in our polar coordinates:
\begin{gather}
    I_{xx}=\iiint\limits_V{\rho}{\left(y^2+z^2\right)}{dV}=\iiint\limits_V{\rho}{\left[(r\cos\theta)^2+(r\sin\theta)^2\right]}{dV}
\end{gather}
Next, we change the integral bounds. These integration bounds will be the same for all $I_{xx}$, $I_{yy}$, and $I_{zz}$ since the bounded areas are identical. We use the volume of the cylinder as the integration bounds, and use the Jacobian associated with polar coordinates for $dV$ (which is $rdrd\theta dx$).
$$ I_{xx}=\int_0^{r}\int_0^{2\pi}\int_{-\frac{h}{2}}^{\frac{h}{2}}{\rho}{\left[(r\cos\theta)^2+(r\sin\theta)^2\right]}{rdrd\theta dx}$$
With the integral set up, it is simply a matter of computation to complete it. Some trig relations may be useful in completing the integral. The only other thing to note is that we can rewrite $\rho$ as $\frac{m}{V}$, where $V$ for the cylinder is $\pi r^2h$.

Doing these integrals for all of the axes, we get:
$$I=\begin{bmatrix}
    \frac{1}{2}mr^2&0&0\\
    0&\frac{1}{12}m(3r^2+h^2)&0\\
    0&0&\frac{1}{12}m(3r^2+h^2)
\end{bmatrix}$$
Which is valid for all uniform density cylinders. Plugging in the given values for Explorer 1, we have:
$$\begin{bmatrix}
   0.0512&0&0\\
    0&5.3590&0\\
    0&0&5.3590
\end{bmatrix} \ \mathrm{kg{\cdot}m^2}$$
\subsubsection{Spin Stabilization}
NASA wants to spin-stabilize the satellite along the $\hat{x}$ direction with a final rotation rate of $2\pi \ \mathrm{rad/s}$. We will assume that the impulse is provided by two motors acting as a couple. The spin motors will also separate the satellite from the launch vehicle, so they are inclined $10\degree$ from horizontal to give the satellite a small forward momentum. So, we will calculate:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The total impulse needed to bring the satellite to a final rotation rate of $2\pi \ \mathrm{rad/s}$ 
    \item The average thrust if a 0.5 s burn is desired.
\end{itemize}

The first thing to do is calculate the total impulse needed, which can be found easily. The impulse is defined as the same as $H$ using the impulse-momentum equality:
$$H=I_{xx}\Delta\omega_{x}=M_x\Delta t$$
We first consider the left-hand side of the equality. Taking $\Delta \omega_x=\omega_f-\omega_i=2\pi-0$ and $I_{xx}$ from before, we have a total impulse requirement of $0.3217 \ \mathrm{N{\cdot} m{\cdot}s}$. From here, the reader may find the average thrust easily.
\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[Yo-yo Despin]\label{exer: yo-yo}
After the spin stabilization maneuver is done to inject the satellite into its final orbit, NASA wants to slow the satellite down by using a yo-yo de-spin.\footnote{You can read more about this yo-yo despin at \cite{noauthor_yo-yo_2025}.} Suppose the satellite has two weights, each of mass 0.5 kg, that are tied to massless strings on the satellite. Assuming that these weights can be approximated as point masses, find the necessary length of the strings so that the final rotation rate of the satellite is $5 \ \mathrm{rpm}$.
\end{exercise}


\section{Key Ideas}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Newton's 2\textsuperscript{nd} Law can be used to create 2\textsuperscript{nd} order differential equations that describe particle motion. These laws are only applicable in inertial frames.
    \item An $n^{\mathrm{th}}$ order differential equation can be reduced into $n$ first order differential equations.
    \item First order differential equations can be easily numerically integrated by a computer, allowing us to get approximate solutions to a huge number of problems.
    \item We can describe the properties of a body's rotation in an analogous way to Newton's 2\textsuperscript{nd} Law.
    \item We can create equations for the angular acceleration and angular velocity using Euler rotation equations.
    \item We can use different frames of reference to more easily express quantities. The \gls{body frame} and \gls{inertial frame} are the most important in our context.
    
\end{enumerate}

\section{Practice Problems}

\begin{exercise}[Mass Spring EOM]\label{exer: mass spring}

The bottom of your door has a spring door stopper. Mesmesized by the motion and sound of this device, you decide you want to model it. You can approximate the door stopper as a mass-spring system constrained to motion along the $\hat{x}$-direction. The spring has an equilibrium length of $L_0$, a spring constant $k=4 \ \mathrm{N/m}$, and mass of $1 \ \mathrm{kg}$. At time $t_0$, the system is at rest with $L=L_0$. An instantaneous impulse of $10 \ \mathrm{N\cdot s}$ is applied to the hinge in the $-\hat{x}$-direction by your hand.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Consider the system to be frictionless. Derive the \glspl{eom} for the system in both the $\hat{x}$ and $\hat{y}$ directions. Find the analytical solution to the EOM.
    \item Numerically integrate the \glspl{eom} to ascertain the position and velocity as a function of time. Graph the solution and compare it to the analytical solution. You may want to refer to \autoref{sec:numerical integration schemes} for \gls{numerical int} methods.
    \item Consider the system with a frictional force that is proportional to the velocity, with a proportionality constant of $c=1\mathrm{\frac{kg}{s}}$. Repeat part 2 with this new system. How does the friction change the system?
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Assuming 3-\gls{dof}, derive the \gls{eom} for the satellite motion assuming that gravity is the only force acting on the satellite and the earth is a point mass. \textit{Hint: You may want to use a different coordinate system.}
    \item You find a satellite in orbit with a height of 400 km. You also measure the inclination of the orbit as it passes overhead as $25\degree$. Assuming a circular orbit, what is the magnitude of the velocity and the component velocities in $\hat{x}$ and $\hat{y}$ as it passes overhead?
    \item Using your equations of motion, develop a MATLAB script to propagate the equations of motion for 90 minutes. Plot the result in Cartesian coordinates and include the earth with the appropriate radius in the plot.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Building a 6-DoF: Part I]
We'll start an exercise in this chapter which will continue through the rest of the chapters -- building a simple 6-DoF model from scratch. For this chapter, we'll start with some simple modeling. For these 6-DoF exercises, we won't provide answers. You should validate these models yourself like you would in the real world.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Start by considering a particle with mass $10 \mathrm{\ kg}$. To start, consider the particle is launched under just the influence of gravity. For this problem and subsequent parts, use the ENU convention for the inertial frame.

Consider the particle to start at position $\vec{r}=[0,0,0]^T$ and velocity $\vec{v}=[30,20,60]^T \text{ m/s}$. Simulate the trajectory numerically and plot the result. Compare this with the analytical solution.
\item Now, include a drag which acts opposite the velocity of the rocket. You can consider the quantity $\frac{1}{2}\rho V^2SC_D$ to be a constant with value of $0.2$ for now. Compare the results with the first part.
\item We won't start modeling the rotational dynamics of the rocket just yet, but we'll set up the basics. Instead of the rocket being a point mass, now consider the rocket to be a cylinder of uniform density with an initial mass of $2 \text{ kg}$, a length of $1 \text{ m}$ and a diameter of $0.05 \text{ m}$. For the first $8 \sec$, the rocket burns $0.1 \text{ kg/s}$ of fuel. The exhaust velocity is $800 \text{ m/s}$ and the nozzle is perfectly expanded. Compute the thrust of the engine, the trajectory with the addition of the thrust, and the inertia matrix of the rocket in it's body frame.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\markboth{NOTES}{NOTES}

\chapter{Energy Methods of Mechanics}\label{sec:energy methods}
\begin{chapquote}{Joseph Fourier}
    ``Mathematics compares the most diverse phenomena and discovers the secret analogies that unite them.''
\end{chapquote}

We have spent a brief time investigating Newtonian dynamics to derive \glspl{eom}. It is useful for us to likewise investigate energy based methods of mechanics. Energy methods of mechanics employ the concepts of kinetic and potential energies of a system to find an \gls{eom}. This is in contrast to the Newtonian method, which considers the forces of a system. The good news is that both will result in the same answer (we will prove this later). However, having both in your set of tools as an engineer can prove especially useful. To grasp energy methods, it is best to have an intuition for Newtonian dynamics first. A thorough reading of \autoref{sec: Newtonian Dynamics} and other sources will help prepare the reader for this section.\footnote{A good video overview of this topic can also be found at \cite{veritasium_closest_2024}.}

There are two main types of energy mechanics, referred to as Hamiltonian and \gls{Lagrangian} mechanics. In this chapter, we will focus on the \gls{Lagrangian} version of these mechanics. The discussion of Hamiltonian mechanics is saved for Volume III. The energy methods of mechanics are more advanced than the other topics discussed here. These topics will require a bit of background in multivariate calculus and are not strictly required for understanding the rest of the 6-\gls{dof} implementation. However, we believe that it is best to be exposed to these ideas early so that the reader can gain an appreciation for where more advanced mechanics (and the subsequent volumes of this text) may take them. The example problems in this chapter will help the reader understand when an energy based approach may be preferred.

For rocketry, energy methods can be especially useful when we encounter multi-body simulations -- such as a rocket and a parachute which interact with each other -- or when considering structural flexibility.  The framework that we develop here can be extended to make those problems easier to solve.

Another reason to motivate these systems is that energy is a scalar quantity. Because we deal with scalars instead of vectors, we have much more freedom, and importantly, do not need tools like the \gls{bke}. Using energy mechanics, we are allowed to employ \textit{generalized coordinates}. These generalized coordinates are very powerful and allow us much greater flexibility. We will see the usefulness of this as we proceed. A brief overview of the differences between the two schemes of \gls{eom} derivation is shown in \autoref{fig:newt v lang}.
