The discussion of energy mechanics in this volume is curtailed because it is not directly applicable to the work on our specific rocket 6-\gls{dof} model. The fundamental implications of energy methods are very important, but a full discussion is saved for Volume III. We present here only the formulaic approach and its uses for the sake of brevity.

\section{Hamilton's Principle}

Hamilton's Principle is the fact that underlies all of energy mechanics. To understand energy mechanics at all, we must study this idea closely. Before we can can describe Hamilton's principle, we need to understand a new type of calculus, one which is the calculus of whole functions. We call this the `calculus of variations'. The calculus of variations underpins the ideas of \gls{Lagrangian} and Hamiltonian mechanics, so it is important to understand the principles behind it before moving on to actual mechanics. In analogy to how single variable calculus is fundamental to Newtonian mechanics, the calculus of variations is fundamental to energy mechanics.

The fundamental idea of the calculus of variations is simple. We want to find a path that minimizes a quantity which we call the \textit{action}. Why? In physics, we often see that quantities are minimized or maximized for some reason. For example, a soap surface will minimize its surface area, or a beam of light will follow the path of shortest time. We may unite all of these into a single idea using action.

Moving to a mathematical description, our goal of minimizing action is the same as finding some function that is the minimum of some set of conditions. Of course, it doesn't mean much to take the minimum over an entire function, so we need to define something called a \gls{functional} to do so. This \gls{functional} is a type of integral. We define this as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:functional}
    J=\int_{x_1}^{x_2}f\left[y(x),y'(x);x\right]{dx}
\end{equation}
The \gls{functional}, $J$, is a quantity which maps a function onto the real number line. This gives us some way to take this entire function and map it to a single number that we can minimize. To minimize this function, we need to pick an appropriate path, $y(x)$.  The tricky part, of course, is doing so over the whole range of $x$. To make sure we understand, it's good to contrast this with single variable calculus. An example is shown in \autoref{fig:variational calc}.

Notice that in the single variable case, we do a little shift by $dx$ and our function will change by $dy$. When we are at a local minimum or maximum of the function, we do a $dx$ nudge and the change in $dy$ is zero. Everyone who has taken calculus knows that this means $\frac{df}{dx}=0$ at a minimum or maximum, of course.

Doing things in variational calculus is basically the same idea. Instead of doing a little variation $dx$, we'll do a variation of the whole path. We'll call this little variation $\varepsilon\eta(x)$. When our path $y(x)$ is the optimal one, we can do this $\varepsilon\eta(x)$ variation to our path and it doesn't change the value of $J$. That's the basic idea. Not too bad, right?

The slightly nuanced part is deriving the condition on $f[y(x),y'(x)]$ which results in this minimum. Doing the derivation is a bit involved, so we'll skip over that here. See Volume III if you're interested.\footnote{The derivations of Chapter 6 and 7 of  \cite{thornton_classical_nodate} and Chapter 5 of  \cite{noauthor_variational_2017} are also quite good.} We can come up with a condition on $f$ that will give us the extremal path. The fundamental equation that tells us how to find this function is called \gls{Euler Equation}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Euler's Diff eq}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}-\frac{d}{dx}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y'}=0}\color{black}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Variations}
We define a quantity $\delta$, the variation, which is the rough equivalent of a change $dx$. We can reformulate the problem of calculus of variations in this new notation to the following:
\begin{equation}
    \delta J= \int_{x_1}^{x_2}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}-\frac{d}{dx}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y'}\right)\delta y{dx}
\end{equation}
We see that the Euler Equation is the integrand of this equation. Since we know that this is zero, $\delta J$ must be zero! And thus, \eqref{eq: Euler's Diff eq} can be written concisely as:
\begin{equation}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\delta J=0}
\end{equation}
This shouldn't really surprise you. This is exactly what we said before. On our ideal path, we do a little variation, and the change in the action, $\delta J$, is $0$. We'll take this idea and apply it to a physical system now. When we apply the \gls{Euler Equation} to physical systems, we give it a new name, Hamilton's Principle. We define Hamilton's principle below:
\begin{theorem}[Hamilton's Principle]
For a system which travels from one point to another within a specific time interval, the path that is followed is the path which minimizes the time integral of the difference between the kinetic and potential energies.
$$\delta \int_{t_1}^{t_2}{T-U}{dt}=0$$
\end{theorem}
We can see that this is exactly the same as our formulation above. Notice that the kinetic energy ($T$) is a function of the velocity, $y'(x)$, and the potential energy ($U$) is a function of position, $y(x)$. So this is really the same equation as before in disguise. The quantity $T-U$, the kinetic energy minus the potential energy, is a quantity called the \gls{Lagrangian}, denoted $L$. 
\begin{equation}
    L\equiv T-U
\end{equation}

The time integral of the \gls{Lagrangian} is the \gls{action}, denoted $S$. This is often why Hamilton's Principle is also called the \gls{princ of least action}. 

\begin{equation}
    S=\int_{t_1}^{t_2}{L}{dt}
\end{equation}

So, our \gls{action} takes the form of the \gls{functional} from the calculus of variations. From Hamilton's principle, this \gls{functional} must be minimized to yield an extremal action.\endnote{We make no statement about whether action is maximized, minimized, or simply stationary. Sometimes we just say that the action is minimized as a sort of short hand, but that doesn't \textit{need} to be true. In classical physics, there isn't really a satisfying answer to which of the extremal conditions is met. To answer that question, we would have to turn to quantum field theory and the path-integral formulation which generalize the action principle. More information on this is provided in Volume III.} So, we sometimes see the compact form of Hamilton's Principle as:
$$\delta S=0$$
We recognized that the form of the equation was the same as before, which means that we can use \gls{Euler Equation} to solve it. Expressed using Euler's differential equation, \eqref{eq: Euler's Diff eq}, we get the \glspl{euler lagrange}:
\begin{gather}\label{eq: Euler-Lagrange}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\frac{\partial L}{\partial x_i}=\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{x}_i}}\\ \mathrm{\color{boxBlue}Euler-Lagrange \ Equations}
\end{gather}

\subsection{Some Limitations}
Before we look into some examples of how to utilize energy mechanics in practice, we should add a bit of a caveat. Energy methods don't work in every type of scenario.

You may have noticed for example that the potential energy is a function of the position only. We call such a potential \gls{conservative}, or path independent. This turns out to be no problem for fundamental physics -- all of those forces arise from such a potential function. However, for engineering applications, the implication of this is that frictional forces and aerodynamics are not modeled with the \gls{Lagrangian} because those forces depend on the path we take.\endnote{Although frictional force can be accounted for with the utilization of Rayleigh's dissipation function and other techniques, such as those discussed in \cite{rabei_hamiltonian_2004-1}.} This is the reason we do not directly use the \gls{Lagrangian} approach to derive equations of motion in the rocket 6-DoF.\endnote{Much of the rotational dynamics we describe can be represented equivalently using the \gls{Lagrangian} approach, so we do use these equations in a roundabout manner. We will explore this more extensively in Volume III.}

So, for systems that are non-conservative, it is easiest to stick with the Newtonian method of mechanics, as we do for our 6-\gls{dof}. However, for those that are frictionless, the methods described here will work well. While it may seem that frictionless systems are very idealistic, we do see systems that can be approximated as frictionless quite often! Orbits, of course, are one case where this is especially true. For many other systems, such as a spinning top or other rotating bodies, we can often ignore the small amount of friction and arrive at analytical solutions without much hassle using the \gls{Lagrangian}. These methods also become extraordinarily important for our derivations of fundamental relationships. With this limitation noted, let us use the \gls{Lagrangian} in a few examples to see how it can turn some complex Newtonian systems into fairly trivial problems.

\subsection{Mass-Spring System}
As a very basic first system, we will consider a mass spring system as shown in \autoref{fig:mass spring lagrange}. We consider the length $x$ to be referenced from the rest length of the spring.

We know the analytical form of the \gls{eom} from Newtonian Dynamics:
$$m\ddot{x}+kx=0$$
So, in essence, we are using the \gls{Lagrangian} \textit{a posteriori} to match this solution. First, we will find the \gls{Lagrangian} of the system. The kinetic energy of the particle is given by the linear translation only (there is no rotational motion for a point particle), so we have $T=\frac{1}{2}m\dot{x}^2$. The potential energy is the energy of the spring, given as $U=\frac{1}{2}kx^2$. So, the \gls{Lagrangian} is:
$$L=\frac{1}{2}m\dot{x}^2-\frac{1}{2}kx^2$$
Now, applying \eqref{eq: Euler-Lagrange} in the $\hat{x}$-direction, as this is the only degree of freedom:
$$\frac{\partial L}{\partial x}=\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{x}}$$
Taking each term individually,
$$\frac{\partial L}{\partial x}=-kx$$
$$\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{x}}=m\dot{x}$$
$$\frac{d}{dt}\left(m\dot{x}\right)=m\ddot{x}$$
So, the \eqref{eq: Euler-Lagrange} equation gives us:
$$-kx=m\ddot{x}$$
A simple change rearrangement gives the exact same result as the Newtonian approach:
$$m\ddot{x}+kx=0$$
In this problem, we used a simple case where the dynamics were constrained along a single axis of motion. Before we move to a more complex problem, we will introduce the method by which \gls{Lagrangian} mechanics deals with coordinates and constraints.

\subsection{Any Newtonian System}

In this section we will prove the \gls{Lagrangian} approach reproduces the Newtonian dynamics for any system.

\begin{proof}
We start with Lagrange's equations in rectilinear coordinates $x_i=x,y,z$:

$$\frac{\partial L}{\partial x_i}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{x}_i}=0 $$

We know that the \gls{Lagrangian} is given as $L=T-U$, so we can express the Euler-Lagrange equations as:

$$\frac{\partial (T-U)}{\partial x_i}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial (T-U)}{\partial \dot{x}_i}=0$$

We know that the translational kinetic energy is a function of the velocity only, so $\frac{\partial T}{\partial x_i}=0$. A \gls{conservative} potential is a function of the position only, so $\frac{\partial U}{\partial \dot{x}_i}=0$. This simplifies the \gls{euler lagrange} to:
$$-\frac{\partial U}{\partial x_i}=\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{x}_i}$$
For a \gls{conservative} system, $-\frac{\partial U}{\partial x_i}=F_i$, because the force is the gradient of the potential function.

We know the kinetic energy is given as $\frac{1}{2}m\dot{x}^2$. The derivative with respect to the velocity $\dot{x}$ is $m\dot{x}_i$. Taking the time derivative, we have $\frac{d}{dt}(m\dot{x}_i)=m\ddot{x}_i$ in the case of constant mass (or more generally,  $\frac{d}{dt}(m\dot{x}_i)=\dot{p}_i$).

So, we have $F_i=m\ddot{x}_i$.

\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
A result of the proof is that Newton's Laws are only valid in an inertial reference frame -- a result of the assumption of our cartesian basis.

In the Lagrangian formulation, we must consider our kinetic and potential energies in an inertial frame, but the generalized coordinates may exist in any frame or have any units. These equations are more generally extensible than the Newtonian counterpart.
\end{corollary}


\section{Generalized Coordinates}
We recall that in Newtonian mechanics, we describe reference frames for an object. We represent the position, velocity, and acceleration of a particle in terms of these coordinate systems as a tridimensional vector. 
Generally, we may write the $\vec{r}^{\ op}$  vector in a frame $a$ as:
$$x\hat{a}_1+y\hat{a}_2+z\hat{a}_3$$
Because any linearly independent tridimensional set of coordinates that we choose will span $\mathbb{R}^3$, we can represent the position with any three vectors, $q$, where the terms of $a$ can be described by the terms of $q$. That is, we could write a new basis $\tilde{a}$ like:
$$\tilde{a}_1=f(q_1,q_2,q_3), \tilde{a}_2=f(q_1,q_2,q_3)$$
and so on. We could then write the $x,y,z$ position in this new coordinate system:
$$x\tilde{a}_1\left(q_1,q_2,q_3\right)+y\tilde{a}_2\left(q_1,q_2,q_3\right)+z\tilde{a}_3\left(q_1,q_2,q_3\right)$$
As a shorthand, we will write this full set of $q$, $\left(q_1,q_2,\cdots q_n\right)$, as $q_j$. We are also free to take the derivatives of these expressions to yield generalized velocities and generalized accelerations\endnote{We don't use the concept of a generalized acceleration very much, but it does exist.}:
$$\dot{q}_j=f(q_j, \dot{q}_j)$$
$$\ddot{q}_j=f(q_j, \dot{q}_j, \ddot{q}_j)$$
When we do Newtonian dynamics, we need to use the \gls{bke} to transform these quantities if they are not in the \gls{inertial frame}. Not so with \gls{Lagrangian} mechanics! We can take the derivatives of these quantities without worry, since our function is scalar. In physics lingo, the \gls{Lagrangian} is invariant to the choice of basis.

When multiple particles are involved, we may need to increase this number of $q$ coordinates to measure each particle's motion. As a very general statement, we can say for $N$ particles, we will need $n$ generalized coordinates in $q$. 

The acceleration of the particles, when Newton's Laws are applied, are a function of the $q_j$ as well as their derivatives, $\dot{q}_j$. So, the evolution of the system exists in a $2n$-dimensional state space. However, we can use our previously mentioned \gls{Lagrangian} to circumvent the need for Newton's Law and the \gls{bke} in our derivation of the \gls{eom}.

\subsection{Constraints}\label{sec:constaints}
Constraint equations are fundamental to \gls{Lagrangian} mechanics. In the previous section, we very vaguely stated the need for $n$ generalized coordinates. To find a precise number, we turn our attention to constraints. For each particle, we need 3 values to define the position, so we need $3N$ generalized coordinates. 

However, we often have constraints that reduce this number. For example, we have many problem that are planar or where the particles are connected in a \gls{rigid body}. These constraints will reduce the number of generalized coordinates needed. Here, we are only considering constraints on the positions of particles. Constraints on the positions of particles are known as \glspl{holonomic const}. 

A \gls{holonomic const} relates generalized coordinates to each other. In the commonly used notation, the \gls{holonomic const} is expressed in terms of the $q_j$'s (and possibly as a function of time) as:
$$\Phi_k\left(q_j,t\right)=0, \ k=1\cdots K$$
or in terms of the position vectors:
$$f_k\left(\vec{r}^{\ op_1},\vec{r}^{\ op_2}\cdots \vec{r}^{\ op_N},t\right)=0$$
The number of \glspl{holonomic const}, $K$, will change the number of generalized coordinates as:
\begin{gather}\label{eq:dof}
\color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\mathcal{M}=3N-K}\color{black}
\end{gather}
The quantity $\mathcal{M}$ is the number of \gls{dof}. However, this is the number of \gls{dof} for the whole system, which is why it can exceed 6 in some cases. It is indeed sensible to talk about systems which have 9, 12, or $10^{30}$ \gls{dof}!

\newpage
\begin{example}[Particle on a path]

We can select our generalized coordinates to be the same as the cartesian coordinates, so that:
\begin{gather}
    q_1=x\\q_2=y\\q_3=z
\end{gather}
So, to write these as \glspl{holonomic const}, we can write:
\begin{gather}
    q_3=0\\
    q_2-g(x)=0
\end{gather}
With these two constraints, we have reduced the number of degrees of freedom, $\mathcal{M}$, to 1. This makes sense intuitively, as the particle can only move along the path specified by the equation $g(x)$, therefore only having one degree of freedom.
\end{example}

The power of using generalized coordinates is that we could define a coordinate that more easily describes our system. For example, our one coordinate could be the arc length along the path, $s$, defined from some starting point.

In general, these generalized coordinates do not even need to have units of length! We can select any parameters that are most convenient for defining the state, even those that are energies, so long as we can define a continuous derivative of that state and use the \gls{euler lagrange}.\footnote{See \cite{goldstein_classical_2008} and \cite{taylor_classical_2004} for more information.}

\subsubsection{Rigid Body Constraints}\label{sec: rigid body constraints}
For most of the systems that we will deal with, including our rocket, we make the assumption that particles are rigidly attached to each other, meaning that there is no change in distance between particles. Using the notation of constraints described above, we will show how this is done for a two particle system, which can then naturally be extended with more particles.

Given two particles that are connected together by a massless rigid rod of length L, we have a constraint on the particles' distance:
$$\left\Vert \vec{r}^{\ op_1}-\vec{r}^{\ op_2}\right\Vert=L$$
Written in the more standard notation:
$$f\left(\vec{r}^{\ op_1},\vec{r}^{\ op_2}\right)=\left\Vert \vec{r}^{\ op_1}-\vec{r}^{\ op_2}\right\Vert-L=0$$
So this constraint takes the 6-\gls{dof} for the two particles and reduces it to 5-\gls{dof}.

An example of these constraints would be the position of the first particle in Cartesian coordinates (giving 3 of the generalized coordinates, $x,y,z$), and the azimuthal and elevation angle, $\phi$ and $\theta$. The roll angle does not change the system in any measurable way, hence we only need 5 parameters to define a two particle system.

In the general case of $N$ particles composing a \gls{rigid body}, we have $3N$-\gls{dof} in a completely unconstrained system. However, we must constrain each pair of particles. An example of this for the 3 particle system in shown in \autoref{fig:rigid body 3 particle}.

So, we have 3 constraint equations in a system with 3 particles. This gives a total of $3N-3=6$-\gls{dof}. When any additional particles are added, they must be fully constrained in all 3 translational degrees of freedom by constraints between $r_1$,$r_2$, and $r_3$. So, we always have constraints of the form $3N-3(N-2)$ for a \gls{rigid body}. This is why we always see a \gls{rigid body} displaying 6-\gls{dof}. We can then, of course, parametrize this in general coordinates with the cartesian coordinates and 3 angles.

\subsubsection{Minimal Set of Constraints}

So far, we have only discussed holonomic constraints, those that are related to position or on the generalized parameters, and possibly have some relation in time. When a system is a \gls{holonomic sys}, that is, purely defined by \glspl{holonomic const}, it is always possible to find a set of $\mathcal{M}$ generalized parameters to describe the particle motion. In other words, the minimum number of parameters is equivalent to the number of degrees of freedom in the system.

That said, we often choose to use more parameters, particularly in the case of rotations. As we will see in Volume II, we often find it more useful to use a non-minimal set of constraints to encode rotations to avoid singularities in the solution. It is also common to see a non-minimal set of constraints used when the minimal set is cumbersome and does not give an intuitive feel to the problem.
\subsubsection{Non-Holonomic Constraints}

The other class of constraints are those that are not merely a function of the generalized coordinates. These constraints, called \glspl{non holo const}, define the set of all constraints that depend on more than just the position of particles. This is a huge class of constraints, so we will not cover every type. These are also less important to our derivations so we will only briefly discuss them.

The most common among these are constraints on the \glspl{gen vel}.  Such an example of a constraint on \glspl{gen vel} is a roller skate. A roller skate only allows a velocity in the direction of the skate and restricts velocity along the direction normal to the roller skate. One important thing about \glspl{non holo const} is that they do not reduce the number of parameters needed to define the system. 

%add more if needed.
\subsection{Lagrange's Equation in Generalized Coordinates}

The good thing about the \gls{Lagrangian} method is that it is invariant to changes in our coordinate system. Because the \gls{Lagrangian} is an equation of energy, and energy is independent of coordinate transformations, we can easily express the \gls{euler lagrange} in generalized coordinates with little hassle.

Using the generalized coordinates, the kinetic energy is a function of the generalized positions, velocities, and time. The potential is a function of the generalized position and time, so the \gls{Lagrangian} becomes:
$$L\equiv T-U=T(q_j,\dot{q}_j,t)-U(q_j,t)$$
Stated another way, the \gls{Lagrangian} is a function of the generalized positions, velocities, and time, as $L=L(q_j,\dot{q}_j,t)$. The \gls{euler lagrange} then becomes:
\begin{gather}\label{eq:Euler-Lagrange General}
\color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_j}=\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_j},\ \ \ j=1,2,\cdots M}\\
\mathrm{\color{boxBlue}Generalized \ Euler-Lagrange \ Equations}
\end{gather}
We will now see how rotational invariance and generalized coordinates proves especially useful in simplifying our derivation.

\subsection{Pendulum EOM Example}
\subsubsection{Problem Set-Up}
Suppose we have a pendulum of length L. The mass is rigidly attached to the pendulum. A reference frame $a$ that moves with the pendulum is shown in \autoref{fig:Pendulum}. The \gls{inertial frame} is also shown. The pendulum moves in only the $xy$-plane.

\subsubsection{Newtonian Approach}
You have likely seen the Newtonian approach many times, but we show it here for completeness and to contrast the approach with the \gls{Lagrangian} more directly. 

In the Newtonian Approach to deriving EOM's, we always start with the forces governing the system. In this case, we have two forces. The gravitational force, $mg$, acts in the $-\hat{y}$ direction and the tension force acts in the $-\hat{a}_r$ direction. From the constraint on length, the acceleration in the $\hat{a}_r$ direction must be zero. Thus, the component of gravity in the $\hat{a}_r$ direction must equal the tension.

We express the gravity in the $a$-frame to find this:
$$\hat{y}=-\cos\theta\hat{a}_r+\sin\theta \hat{a}_\theta$$
So, by balancing the forces in the $\hat{a}_r$ direction:
$$m{\ddot{a}}_r=mg\cos\theta \hat{a}_r-\vec{F}_T$$
In the $\hat{a}_\theta$-direction, we have unconstrained motion, so $\sum\vec{F}=m\vec{a}$. Our force equation is thus: 
$$F=-mg\sin\theta\hat{a}_{\theta}$$
Next, we need to find our acceleration in the \gls{inertial frame} using the \gls{bke} to solve the RHS of the force balance. Starting with the position vector:
$$\vec{r}^{\ op}=L\hat{a}_r$$
We can apply the \gls{bke} to find our inertial velocity:
$${}^i\vec{v}^p={}^a\frac{d}{dt}\vec{r}^{\ op}+{}^i\omega^a\times\vec{r}^{\ op}$$
Plugging in appropriate quantities:
$${}^i\vec{v}^p={}^a\frac{d}{dt}(L\hat{a}_r)+\dot{\theta}\hat{a}_3\times(L\hat{a}_r)$$
$${}^i\vec{v}^p=\dot{\theta}L\hat{a}_\theta$$
We do this again to find the inertial acceleration:
$${}^i\vec{a}^p={}^a\frac{d}{dt}{}^i\vec{v}^{\ p}+{}^i\omega^a\times{}^i\vec{v}^{\ p}$$
$${}^i\vec{a}^p={}^a\frac{d}{dt}{}\dot{\theta}L\hat{a}_\theta+\dot{\theta}\hat{a}_3\times\dot{\theta}L\hat{a}_\theta$$
$${}^i\vec{a}^p=\ddot{\theta}L\hat{a}_\theta-\dot{\theta}^2L\hat{a}_r$$
Now, we equate the forces in each direction:
$$-mg\sin\theta\hat{a}_\theta=\ddot{\theta}L\hat{a}_\theta$$
Rearranging, we arrive at our \gls{eom}:
$$\ddot{\theta}+\frac{g}{L}\sin\theta=0$$
\subsubsection{Lagrangian Approach}
In the \gls{Lagrangian} approach, we will start by defining our constraints and coordinates. Some preliminary analysis shows that using polar coordinates for this problem will be useful. If we do not recognize this, we can always convert later (a distinct advantage of the \gls{Lagrangian} approach, since it is coordinate frame invariant). Here we will choose a cylindrical coordinate system:
\begin{gather}
    q_1=r\\q_2=\theta\\q_3=z
\end{gather}
Next, we define constraints. Since there is no motion in the $z$-axis:
$$z=0$$
And the constraint on the length gives us:
$$q_1-L=0$$
So, we have 1 \gls{dof} from \eqref{eq:dof}. Since both $z$ and $r$ are constrained, we can express our \gls{eom} in terms of one generalized coordinate, $\theta$. Next, we define the \gls{Lagrangian}. Knowing that the linear velocity of the particle is $\dot{\theta}L$, we get:
$$T=\frac{1}{2}m\dot{\theta}^2L^2$$
For the potential, we choose the origin as our reference, giving us:
$$U=-mgy$$
And a coordinate transformation into polar using $y=r\cos\theta$ yields:
$$U=-mgL\cos\theta$$
This gives the \gls{Lagrangian}:
$$L=\frac{1}{2}m\dot{\theta}^2L^2+mgL\cos\theta$$
Applying the Euler-Lagrange formula for generalized coordinates, \eqref{eq:Euler-Lagrange General} on the single generalized coordinate:
\begin{gather}
    \frac{\partial L}{\partial \theta}=-mgL\sin\theta\\
    \frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\theta}}=mL^2\dot{\theta}\\
    \frac{d}{dt}\left(mL^2\dot{\theta}\right)=mL^2\ddot{\theta}
\end{gather}
So, the final system is:
$$-mgL\cos\theta =mL^2\ddot{\theta}$$
With some rearrangement:
$$\ddot{\theta}+\frac{g}{L}\sin\theta=0$$
We notice that with the \gls{Lagrangian} approach, we did not need to have an understanding of the vector directions, frame translations, or \gls{bke} to find our \gls{eom}. This, in addition to the flexibility of the generalized coordinates, is the real power of this approach.

\begin{exercise}[Lagrangian in Code]\label{ex:lagrangiancode}
Sometimes computing the derivatives in the Euler-Lagrange equation can be a bit tedious. Using MATLAB or Python, create a function which takes in symbolic expressions for $L$ and the $q_j$ terms and outputs the \glspl{eom} of the system. Test your code on the mass-spring system and the pendulum shown above.
\end{exercise}

\section{Lagrangian Derivation of Rigid Body Dynamics}

In \autoref{Euler Rotation Equations}, we explored the Newtonian method of deriving the Euler rotation equations using the \gls{bke}. Here, we present an alternative approach to rigid body dynamics that utilizes the \gls{Lagrangian} approach and is ultimately a more fundamental derivation. Some of the ideas in this section have not been discussed in full -- such as Euler angles and the principle of virtual work -- so do not worry if you cannot follow each step exactly.

\subsection{Euler's Rotations Equations}\label{sec: euler rot eq}
In \autoref{Euler Rotation Equations}, we took the fact that
$$\vec{M}^o=\frac{{}^id\vec{H}^o}{dt}$$
to be a fact of the universe. Sure, there exists some logic in relating this equation to Newton's 2nd law, but we would like a more rigorous derivation. Using the \gls{Lagrangian} approach, we may see that this is a natural consequence of Hamilton's principle.

We start with the generalized \gls{euler lagrange} formula in \eqref{eq:Euler-Lagrange General} along with an additional term for the external torque:
$$\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}_j}-\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_j}=Q_j$$
We have not discussed this term $Q_j$. This is an external force -- one which is not accounted for in the \gls{Lagrangian}. It is a bit involved to calculate $Q_j$, but it turns out this term is our external torque, $\vec{M}^o$.\footnote{See Volume III for a derivation of this fact.}

To derive Euler's rotation equation, we consider a system which is purely rotating, such that the \gls{Lagrangian} is:
$$L=\frac{1}{2}I\dot{\theta}^2$$
We choose our generalized coordinate, $\theta$. This system has no dependence on $\theta$, so we only consider the $\dot{\theta}$ term.
$$\frac{d}{dt}(I\dot{\theta})=\vec{M}^o$$
We recognize that $I\dot{\theta}$ is the angular momentum, $\vec{H}$. We have thus shown that:
$$\vec{M}=\frac{^id\vec{H}}{dt}$$
Stated this way, it seems a more obvious fact. Much like a force is the rate of change of momentum, a torque/moment should be the rate of change of an angular moentum. From here, we can continue and derive the Euler rigid body equations using the \gls{Lagrangian}. 
\subsection{Torque Free Equations}
We showed earlier in \autoref{sec: rigid body constraints} that we can describe a \gls{rigid body} system with 6 generalized parameters, three of which are angles. We may use those three angles to derive the torque free Euler rigid body equations.

In the case of no applied torque, the derivations of \glspl{Euler Equation} become surprisingly simple. We can assume that the change in height of the \gls{rigid body} is irrelevant for pure rotation, so the \gls{Lagrangian} is simply the kinetic energy of the object. We can suppose that we are also in a coordinate system where the translational kinetic energy is 0. This gives us the same \gls{Lagrangian}  as before:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rigid body KE}
L=\frac{1}{2}I\omega^2
\end{equation}
We can select our generalized coordinates as the three Euler angles, $\psi$, $\theta$, and $\phi$ (see \autoref{sec:Euler Angles}). Selecting the generalized coordinate, $\psi$, as an example, the Euler-Lagrange equation gives:
$$\frac{\partial L}{\partial \psi}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\psi}}=0$$
Using the derivation of the components of angular velocity using Euler Angles, we have:\footnote{See Volume II for information on how to derive Euler rates.}
\begin{gather}
\omega_1=\dot{\phi}\sin\theta\sin\psi+\dot{\theta}\cos\psi\\
    \omega_2=\dot{\phi}\sin\theta\cos\psi-\dot{\theta}\sin\psi\\
    \omega_3=\dot{\phi}\cos\theta+\dot{\psi}
\end{gather}
So, we can also express our \gls{Lagrangian} in terms of the angular acceleration:
$$\frac{\partial L}{\partial \omega_i}\frac{\partial \omega_i}{\partial \psi}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \omega_i}\frac{\partial \omega_i}{\partial \dot{\psi}}=0$$
At this point, the derivation is simply a matter of `plug and chug'. Calculating the derivatives with respect to $\psi$:
\begin{gather}
    \frac{\partial \omega_1}{\partial \psi}=-\dot{\phi}\sin\theta\cos\psi-\dot{\theta}\sin\psi\\
    \frac{\partial \omega_2}{\partial \psi}=-\dot{\phi}\sin\theta\sin\psi-\dot{\theta}\cos\psi\\
    \frac{\partial \omega_3}{\partial \psi}=0
\end{gather}
We notice here that $\frac{\partial \omega_1}{\partial \psi}=\omega_2$ and $\frac{\partial \omega_2}{\partial \psi}=-\omega_1$. Next, taking our derivatives with respect to $\dot{\psi}$:
\begin{gather}
    \frac{\partial \omega_1}{\partial \dot{\psi}}=0\\
    \frac{\partial \omega_2}{\partial \dot{\psi}}=0\\
    \frac{\partial \omega_3}{\partial \dot{\psi}}=1
\end{gather}
Last, we can make a substitution for the $\frac{\partial L}{\partial \omega_i}$ term by taking a derivative of the kinetic energy \eqref{eq:rigid body KE}:
$$\frac{\partial L}{\partial \omega_i}=I_i\omega_i$$
Plugging everything into the Euler-Lagrange formula, we have:
$$I_1\omega_1\omega_2-I_2\omega_1\omega_2-\frac{d}{dt}I_3\omega_3=0$$
Some simplification yields:
$$\left(I_1-I_2\right)\omega_1\omega_2=I_3\dot{\omega_3}$$
We can perform the analogous analysis for the other axes, yielding the full set of torque free \gls{rigid body} equations:\endnote{Note the change in sign as compared to \eqref{Euler Rotation Equations}. This occurs because the sign of the component in the parenthesis has been changed.}
\begin{gather}
\color{boxBlue}\boxed{
\begin{array}{ll}
\color{black}
\left(I_y-I_z\right)\omega_y\omega_z=I_x\dot{\omega_x}
     &\\
     \color{black}\left(I_z-I_x\right)\omega_x\omega_z=I_y\dot{\omega_y}
     &\\
     \color{black}\left(I_x-I_y\right)\omega_x\omega_y=I_z\dot{\omega_z}
\end{array}}\\
\color{boxBlue}
\mathrm{Torque-Free \ Rotations}
\end{gather}

\begin{exercise}[Euler Rigid Body Equations with Torque]
Following a similar line of reasoning to the derivation above, include a generalized force to derive the Euler rigid body equations with an external moment acting on the system. You may use the generalized force defined in \autoref{sec: euler rot eq}.    
\end{exercise}

\section{Key Ideas}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item We can find the \gls{eom} of a system using either the Newtonian approach or the \gls{Lagrangian} approach, and it is often easier to find an \gls{eom} using the \gls{Lagrangian} for a \gls{conservative} system.
    \item The \gls{Lagrangian} is scalar, so it is invariant to coordinate transforms. This means we can choose any variables we want for our generalized coordinates when performing derivations.
    \item The methodology of Newtonian mechanics and \gls{Lagrangian} mechanics are fundamentally different. Newtonian mechanics deals with the influence of external \glspl{action} (forces), while \gls{Lagrangian} mechanics emphasizes the internal state of the system.
\end{enumerate}
\section{Practice Problems}
\begin{exercise}[Generalized Coordinates]
Arthur keeps finding bugs on the apples that he is feeding his horse. Arthur heard that you have been learning about generalized coordinates. He wants a way to describe the position of the bugs on his apples using a minimal set of coordinates.

Find a suitable set of generalized coordinates to describe this system. Write out any equations of constraints of the system. \textit{Hint: An apple is topologically similar to a sphere!}

\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Brachistochrone (Difficult)]
You are a prominent mathematician in the year 1696. Bernoulli proposes a problem to you to find a curve from a point $(x_0,y_0)$ to another point $\left(x_f,y_f\right)$. When a particle is placed on this path, it should be a faster path than any other path the particle could take down the slope under the influence of only gravity.
%    \item brachistochrone
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Find the velocity as a function of the distance from the initial height, $x$, for the particle using energy conservation.
    \item Write a general expression for the amount of time it takes a particle to descend the path using an integral. \textit{Hint: you can write $t=\int\frac{ds}{v}$}.
    \item Use the Euler-Lagrange equation to solve for the form of this curve (You may find the integral form useful!).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

The angle of Jonah from the vertical is denoted as $\theta$. Derive the \gls{eom} of the system using energy methods. The set-up is shown in \autoref{fig:Inverted Pendulum}.

\begin{exercise}[Double Pendulum]\label{exer_doublepend}
Luckily,  Jonah was an avid reader of the Flight Dynamics Bible and aced his dynamics exam! On his way back home, he needs to grab a small model rocket for some aerodynamics testing. On the way, the parachute pops out of the nose and Jonah grabs it right before it hits the ground. Jonah is holding the rocket by the parachute and the rocket and parachute are swinging around.

The dynamics of the system can be modeled as a double pendulum with two rigid arms, both of length $L$ with masses $m$ at their ends, which are free to rotate in the $xy$-plane. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Find the \gls{eom} for the system as shown in \autoref{fig:Double Pendulum}.
    \item Simulate the system for the initial condition $\theta=\pi/2 \text{ rad}$ and $\phi=\pi-0.1 \text{ rad}$. Set $\dot{\theta}=0$ but $\dot{\phi}=0 \text{ rad/s}$. Create an animation of the output.
    \item If you are adventurous, create some small variation in the initial conditions (a normal distribution with $\sigma\approx0.01  \text{ rad}$ is a good starting point) and perform many simulations with the outputs plotted together.
\end{enumerate}

\chapter{Basic Attitude Dynamics}\label{sec:attitude dynamics}

\textcolor{red}{Add 3 Euler Angle plots, example DCM, Using Euler to construct DCM}

\begin{chapquote}{Lord Kelvin, 1892}
``Quaternions came from Hamilton after his really good work had been done; and, though beautifully ingenious, have been an unmixed evil to those who have touched them in any way, including Clerk Maxwell.''
\end{chapquote}

Attitude dynamics is the math describing the orientation of a vehicle in space as the result of applied moments. Relying in part on advanced mathematics, this concept can naturally be more difficult to grasp. In fact, we have dedicated the entirety of Volume II to the theory behind attitude dynamics, determination, and control. Following the core purpose of this volume, we will only be describing the implementation of attitude dynamics into code. However, the entirety of Volume II contains the theory necessary to gain a more definitive understanding of what, and more importantly why, everything is happening with the code in this section.\endnote{Volume II is yet to be released, and is currently scheduled to do so later along with a supplementary text.}


\section{Euler Angles}\label{sec:Euler Angles}

The first method to express the orientation of a vehicle in space is the use of \gls{Euler angles}. These form the basis for our understanding of attitude dynamics, being the most intuitive method. To keep this section as independent as possible, basic definitions and derivations will be included here to give you the essentials of what is needed in practical applications.

First and foremost, we use the body-fixed 3-2-1 rotation sequence to determine \gls{Euler angles}. This means we have an angle $\phi$ to describe the rotation about the $x$-axis, an angle $\theta$ to describe the rotation about the $y$-axis, and an angle $\psi$ to describe the rotation about the $z$-axis. By breaking the three dimensional rotation down into three sequential two dimensional rotations, it becomes much simpler to visualize. The order in which we complete these rotations is Z-Y-X, or $\psi$-$\theta$-$\phi$.\endnote{These intermediate rotations about the body frame are sequentially applied to our system as we move from frame to frame in our chosen rotation sequence.} Remembering this rotation sequence is very important, as every function call within your MATLAB script will depend on this! An example of the first of these two dimensional rotations is shown below in \autoref{fig:first321}.

    \centering

You can imagine that once you have completed this first rotation of $\psi$ radians about the $\hat{z}$-axis, you would then complete a rotation of $\theta$ radians about this newly oriented $\hat{y}'$-axis, before completing the sequence with a $\hat{x}''$-axis rotation of $\phi$ radians. Consider practicing visualizing this yourself by using your fingers pointing in three orthogonal directions, and sequentially rotating them in a select order. Applying three rotations one by one may be beneficial to our understanding, but we would like to find a way to combine them back into a single rotation for our calculations!

\subsection{The Direction Cosine Matrix}\label{sec:TheDCM}

To start, we want a way to easily convert any arbitrary vector from one reference frame to another. We do this through the use of the Direction Cosine Matrix, or DCM.\endnote{In aerospace applications, we assume our DCM $A$ always converts from inertial to body coordinates. It is important to note that other fields may define this differently.} We take this matrix and left multiply it with our vector in the \gls{inertial frame} and obtain an identical vector expressed using the \gls{body frame} as shown:
\begin{equation}
    ^b\vec{r}=A_{3,2,1} {^i}\vec{r}
\end{equation}
In MATLAB, there exists a function - "angle2dcm" - that creates this for us!\endnote{Note there exists another similar function -- eul2rotm -- that also creates a DCM; however, this function converts a vector from the body frame to the inertial frame.} By inputting the scalar values of our \gls{Euler angles} (in radians!) as well as the chosen rotation sequence, MATLAB creates the DCM as an output, allowing us to left multiply this matrix with our inertial vector we want to express in the \gls{body frame}.\endnote{Note the function allows an input of any number of rotations to find DCMs for. This is a fun little aspect of the function; however, it is irrelevant for our use as we input our new \gls{Euler angles} every time-step during the $\texttt{ode45}$ integration scheme.} One additional thing to consider: the "angle2dcm" function returns the DCM as a transformation from the \gls{inertial frame} to the \gls{body frame}. If we were to transform a vector from the body to \gls{inertial frame}, we would need to take the inverse - in this case transpose - of the given matrix.\endnote{We are allowed to use the transpose here as the DCM is an orthogonal matrix.}\footnote{Further reading on this function can be seen in Mathwork's own documentation at Source \cite{mathworks_eul2quat_nodate}.}

\subsection{Relating Euler Rates with Angular Velocity}


As with our translational values, we want to take the integral of our rate of change of these angular parameters in order to find our new, updated Euler angles. While it might be easy to assume the values we would be integrating are the angular velocities, doing so DOES NOT return the \gls{Euler angles}. Giving a quick reason as to why: angular velocity is expressed exclusively with respect to the body frame, while our Euler angles are defined about their corresponding intermediate frame in the rotation sequence. This job for integration is left to \gls{Euler rates}, something else we need to include in the code. There exists what we affectionately call the ``B'' matrix that allows us to relate angular velocities and \gls{Euler rates} through the use of \gls{Euler angles}. Unfortunately, there doesn't exist a MATLAB function that can calculate the ``B'' matrix, leaving us to our own devices to construct one from scratch. The heavy lifting for this is completed in Volume II, meaning we can copy the matrix derived there straight into our MATLAB script. That matrix and equation is as follows, for quick reference:
\begin{gather}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\begin{bmatrix}
        \dot{\phi}\\\dot{\theta}\\\dot{\psi}
    \end{bmatrix}=B\begin{bmatrix}
        \omega_x\\\omega_y\\\omega_z
    \end{bmatrix}}\\\color{boxBlue}\mathrm{Euler\ Rates}
\end{gather}
\begin{gather}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}B=\begin{bmatrix}
    1&tan(\theta)sin(\phi)&tan(\theta)cos(\phi)\\
    0&cos(\phi)&-sin(\phi)\\
    0&\dfrac{sin(\phi)}{cos(\theta)}&\dfrac{cos(\phi)}{cos(\theta)}
\end{bmatrix}}\\\color{boxBlue}\text{The ``B'' Matrix for 3-2-1 Rotations}
\end{gather}
It is important to note, that the ``B'' matrix will be different for every rotation sequence. So, the matrix given above is only valid for our 3-2-1 sequence!

\subsection{Euler Angle Code Example}\label{sec:EulerDynamicsMATLABExample}

Putting it all together, we are going to write a simple code using \gls{Euler angles} to practice using MATLAB / Python built in functions. Say we have a very important vector we want to express in the \gls{body frame} and another important vector we want to express in the \gls{inertial frame}: gravity and thrust respectively. If our rocket weighs 100 Newtons and outputs a thrust of 1000 Newtons, we know this appears in vector notation as: $-100\hat{x}$ and $1000\hat{X}$ respectively. Consider a moment in time, when -- using the 3-2-1 rotation sequence -- our vehicle is at a roll angle of 2.71 radians and yaw and pitch angles of 0.2 radians, where we want to convert our gravity vector from the \gls{inertial frame} to the \gls{body frame}. In addition, with knowledge that the angular velocity at that instant is $\begin{bmatrix}
    2.5&0.1&0.1
\end{bmatrix}^T$, we can also find the \gls{Euler rates} of the system. To complete this task, we would first initialize all of our given variables and parameters. Then, to construct our DCM, we would make the following function call:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
DCM = angle2dcm(psi,theta,phi,"ZYX");
\end{matlabscript}
\begin{pythonscript}{}
import navpy as navpy
DCM = navpy.angle2dcm(psi, theta, phi, 'ZYX')
\end{pythonscript}

As this would return the rotation matrix translating from the inertial to body frame, we can complete the following two simple calculations to find our quantities expressed in the other frame:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
gravityBody = DCM*gravityInertial;
thrustInertial = (DCM')*thrustBody;
\end{matlabscript}
\begin{pythonscript}{}
gravityBody = DCM @ gravityInertial
thrustInertial = DCM.T @ thrustBody
\end{pythonscript}

At this point, we would simply have to left multiply our ``B'' matrix by our given angular velocity to obtain the Euler rates of the system. Looking at the outputs that are generated, we obtain the following vectors for our gravitational force represented in body coordinates and thrust vector represented in inertial coordinates:
\begin{equation}
    ^b\vec{F}_{g}=\begin{bmatrix}
        -96.0530\\-26.1902\\9.3748
    \end{bmatrix};\ ^i\vec{F}_{T}=\begin{bmatrix}
        960.5305\\194.7092\\-198.6693
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}

\textcolor{red}{Code for euler rates here!}

Following this, we would use our \gls{numerical int} scheme to approximate the next timestep's \gls{Euler angles} using the \gls{Euler rates} we just found. We won't do that here, but you can see a similar implementation with quaternions in the 6DoF itself or the Dzhanibekov example!

\section{Quaternions}\label{sec:quaternions}

To match industry/academia standards and improve the robustness of our simulation, our 6DoF model uses \glspl{quaternion}. The use of quaternions allows us to avoid losing a degree of freedom at a specific orientation of our rocket (called gimbal lock), which is what makes the simulation more robust. Unfortunately, in order to remove this kinematic singularity, we must over parameterize the system. By introducing a fourth parameter to robustly describe the three parameter system, we must introduce a constraint equation. This constraint is simply the normalization of the quaternion. Just as with \gls{Euler angles}, MATLAB has an extensive suite of functions that makes the implementation of \glspl{quaternion} just as easy. 

\subsection{Quaternion Rotation Matrix}

We are going to combine a few initial steps of the code surrounding \gls{quaternion} based attitude dynamics. To start, we want to convert from \gls{Euler angles} to \glspl{quaternion}, as \gls{Euler angles} are more intuitive to define and initialize in the simulation. MATLAB's function "eul2quat" does just that. By inputting the \gls{Euler angles} in the appropriate sequence, along with the chosen rotation sequence (make sure to match the \gls{Euler angles} with the order), the function returns the corresponding \gls{quaternion}.\footnote{Mathworks documentation for this function can be found at Source \cite{mathworks_eul2quat_nodate}.}

Having initialized our \gls{quaternion}, we can now use another MATLAB function to construct our DCM. The function "quat2dcm" takes the \gls{quaternion} as an input, and returns the DCM at that instant.\endnote{This step begins the divergence from the true method of quaternion encoded rotations, as we choose to use elements of the quaternion to build the elements of the DCM for clarity's sake. True quaternion encoded rotations are discussed in-depth in Volume II.} Incredibly simple and incredibly useful; however, we must once again consider that this DCM only converts from the \gls{inertial frame} to the \gls{body frame}. Meaning just as we had done with \gls{Euler angles}, it is necessary to take the transpose (inverse) of the matrix if we want to transform from the \gls{body frame} to the \gls{inertial frame}.\footnote{The Mathworks documentation for the $\texttt{quat2dcm}$ function can be found at Source \cite{mathworks_quat2dcm_nodate}.}

\subsection{Quaternion Rates}\label{sec: quaternion rates}

Just as we had done for \gls{Euler angles} with \gls{Euler rates}, we want to find the \gls{quaternion} rates that allow us to integrate and obtain a new \gls{quaternion}. There is no MATLAB function allowing us to relate \gls{quaternion} rates with angular velocity. But just as with \gls{Euler angles}, we can derive the necessary method to do so! The outcome of this derivation is shown in \eqref{eq: quat rates}.\footnote{Additional information about this derivation can be found at \cite{narayan_how_2017}.} Plugging it into our script is an easy fix to make this operation possible. The calculation is included here for quick reference:

\begin{gather}\label{eq: quat rates}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\mathbf{\dot{q}}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}
        0&-\omega_x&-\omega_y&-\omega_z\\
        \omega_x&0&\omega_z&-\omega_y\\
        \omega_y&-\omega_z&0&\omega_x\\
        \omega_z&\omega_y&-\omega_x&0
    \end{bmatrix}\textbf{q}}
    \\\mathrm{\color{boxBlue}Quaternion \ Rates}
\end{gather}

\subsection{Quaternion MATLAB Function Example}

Let's take the exact same example as before. We have the force of gravity equal to $-100\hat{x}$ and the thrust force equal to $1000\hat{X}$. We have a roll angle of 2.71 radians, yaw and pitch angles of 0.2 radians, and we have chosen a 3-2-1 rotation sequence. We finally have an instantaneous angular velocity of $\begin{bmatrix}
    2.5&0.1&0.1
\end{bmatrix}^T$. Wanting to obtain the gravity vector expressed in the \gls{body frame} instead of the \gls{inertial frame}, and vice versa for the thrust vector, this time we are going to use \glspl{quaternion} to do so. In addition, we are now going to find the \gls{quaternion} rate at the given moment. Our general process is nearly identical to the previous process for which we used Euler angles. We begin by defining our variables and parameters, but this time, we must additionally convert our Euler angles to an attitude quaternion. This is completed by the following function call and normalization:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
quat = eul2quat([psi,theta,phi],"ZYX");
quat = quat/norm(quat);
\end{matlabscript}
\begin{pythonscript}{}
quat = navpy.angle2quat(psi,theta,phi, 'ZYX')
\end{pythonscript}
At this point, we will take advantage of a second function to construct a rotation matrix from the elements of our quaternion:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
quatDCM = quat2dcm(quat);
\end{matlabscript}
\begin{pythonscript}{}
quatDCM = navpy.quat2dcm(quat[0], quat[1:3])
\end{pythonscript}
This allows us to perform the familiar orthogonal transformation of our vector quantities just as we had done previously:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
gravityBodyQuat = quatDCM*gravityInertial;
thrustInertialQuat = (quatDCM')*thrustBody;
\end{matlabscript}
\begin{pythonscript}{}
gravityBodyQuat = quatDCM @ gravityInertial;
thrustInertialQuat = (quatDCM.T) @ thrustBody;
\end{pythonscript}

The quaternion rates would then be obtained by applying equation \eqref{eq: quat rates} into our code.

\textcolor{red}{Code for quat rates here!}

Taking a look at the outputs this generates, we see our results come out to be the following, with the gravity force expressed in the \gls{body frame} and thrust expressed in the \gls{inertial frame} as:
\begin{equation}
    ^b\vec{F}_{g}=\begin{bmatrix}
        -96.0530\\-26.1902\\9.3748
    \end{bmatrix};\ ^i\vec{F}_{T}=\begin{bmatrix}
        960.5305\\194.7092\\-198.6693
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
A quick note about this script in relation to the other: the results shown above and the results shown in \autoref{sec:EulerDynamicsMATLABExample} are identical. This gives confidence that we've implemented MATLAB's functions correctly. And once again, at this point, we would use our \gls{numerical int} scheme to approximate the next timestep's \gls{quaternion} using the \gls{quaternion} rates we just found. We won't do that here, but as a reminder, you can see the implementation in the 6-\gls{dof} itself or the Dzhanibekov example! 

\section{3D Rotation Example: Dzhanibekov Effect}\label{sec:Dzhan}

To help digest the complex nature of attitude dynamics, a full example is especially useful. One of the consequences of Euler's equations \eqref{eq: moment equations} is that rotation about an intermediate axis is unstable. This effect is known as the Intermediate Axis Theorem or the \gls{dzhan}.

This is a good phenomenon to describe 3D rotations because it requires us to put together lots of knowledge throughout this chater. Because our object rotates about all three axes, we must parametrize our rotation with \glspl{quaternion} to avoid possible singularities in the solution.

Before we can describe our system dynamics, we need to establish our coordinate system and inertia tensor for this problem.
\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering


\tikzset{every picture/.style={line width=0.75pt}} %set default line width to 0.75pt        

\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.75pt,y=0.75pt,yscale=-1,xscale=1]
%uncomment if require: \path (0,300); %set diagram left start at 0, and has height of 300


%Shape: Circle [id:dp7493270463079389] 
\draw   (195,100) .. controls (195,86.19) and (206.19,75) .. (220,75) .. controls (233.81,75) and (245,86.19) .. (245,100) .. controls (245,113.81) and (233.81,125) .. (220,125) .. controls (206.19,125) and (195,113.81) .. (195,100) -- cycle ;
%Shape: Rectangle [id:dp8745965352536226]

\subsection{State Vector}
We'll start by describing our \gls{state vector} and initial conditions for this problem. We define our original position at the origin, giving us a position vector:
$$\vec{r}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    0\\0\\0
\end{bmatrix}$$
We also define the initial velocity to be zero, giving a velocity vector:
$$\vec{v}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    0\\0\\0
\end{bmatrix}$$
These will not be of interest to us, so we can disregard them for this problem. We also need an initial orientation. We can either directly initialize a \gls{quaternion} or write our initial orientation with \gls{Euler angles} and then convert to \glspl{quaternion}. Often, \gls{Euler angles} are more intuitive, so we will use these for our initialization. We can define an initial angle of all zeros, meaning that our \gls{body frame} axes are initially coincident with the \gls{inertial frame}. This gives \gls{Euler angles} of:
$$\begin{bmatrix}
    \phi\\\theta\\\psi
\end{bmatrix} =
\begin{bmatrix}
    0\\0\\0
\end{bmatrix}$$
Using the command "eul2quat" with a specification of "ZYX" for the frame, the initial \gls{quaternion} vector is given as:
$$\vec{q}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    1\\0\\0\\0
\end{bmatrix}$$
Lastly, we need to describe the initial angular velocity. To properly demonstrate the effect, we need a small perturbation on our non-intermediate axes. We can write our omega vector as:
$$\vec{\omega}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
0.05\\0.05\\\pi
\end{bmatrix} rad/s$$

Since we only focus on the rotational dynamics of the system, the position and velocity will be excluded from our state vector. Putting all of this together, we arrive at our \gls{state vector}:
$$\vec{X}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    \vec{\omega}_0\\\vec{q}_0
\end{bmatrix}$$
Which we can expand to the full form as a 7-element column vector:
$$\vec{X}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    0.05\\0.05\\\pi\\1\\0\\0\\0
\end{bmatrix}$$
\subsection{Forces and Moments}
We can assume that we are operating in a 0-g environment (as this is where the effect is most prevalent) and that no body forces are acting on the object. We will also assume that air resistance in negligible and there are no losses. These assumptions mean that there are no forces that the acting on our body. Additionally, there are no moments on the body either. We have chosen this case for our example so that we can focus solely on the attitude dynamics.
\subsection{Attitude Dynamics}
Our attitude dynamics are fairly simple since there are no moments acting on the body. Our equations for the angular velocity rates in \eqref{eq: moment eq 2} reduce to the torque free equations:
\begin{gather}\label{eq: moment eq dzhan}
    \dot{\omega}_x=\frac{\left(I_y-I_z\right)\omega_y\omega_z}{I_x}\\
    \dot{\omega}_y=\frac{\left(I_z-I_x\right)\omega_z\omega_x}{I_y}\\
    \dot{\omega}_z=\frac{\left(I_x-I_y\right)\omega_y\omega_x}{I_z}
\end{gather}
To find our \gls{quaternion} rates, we simply use the same methods as in \autoref{sec: quaternion rates}. This gives us:
\begin{gather}
{\color{black}\dot{\textbf{q}}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}
        0&-\omega_x&-\omega_y&-\omega_z\\
        \omega_x&0&\omega_z&-\omega_y\\
        \omega_y&-\omega_z&0&\omega_x\\
        \omega_z&\omega_y&-\omega_x&0
    \end{bmatrix}\mathbf{q}}
\end{gather}
\subsection{Translating to Code}
With all of these defined, we can write the derivative of our \gls{state vector} as:
\begin{equation}
    \dot{\textbf{X}}=\begin{bmatrix}
        \vec{\alpha}\\\dot{\textbf{q}}
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
From here, we can implement this in "ode45". This code can be found in \autoref{Appendix:code} of the Appendix. Our initialization of the \gls{state vector} and all of the basic setup is contained in the main file, shown in "DzhanibekovMain.m". The "DzhanibekovIntegrator.m" code operates on the state vector using "ode45" to get the derivative. There is additionally a file to display the rotation of the body in an animation, which is "RotationVisualizer.m". All outputs can be seen by running the $\texttt{DzhanibekovMain.m}$ script.

%% add listings.
\section{Key Ideas}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Read Volume II to learn more about the theory behind MATLAB's functions.
    \item Rotations can be encoded in a variety of ways, but one of the most useful, compact, and robust methods of encoding rotations involves the use of complex numbers.
    \item The use of MATLAB's built in suite of functions is incredibly useful in the implementation of attitude dynamics in 6-\gls{dof} modeling, and rotational simulations in general.
    \item Be sure to keep your chosen rotation sequence straight when utilizing MATLAB's functions, and be sure to have a thorough understanding of how these functions work!
    \item No matter which style of describing rotations we choose -- \gls{Euler angles} or \glspl{quaternion} -- they both result in identical answers barring any singularities generated with the former.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Practice Problems}

\begin{exercise}[Spinning Top]

Your friend Jimothy, impressed by your MATLAB skills, has challenged you to model a spinning top. The top is axially symmetric about its $\hat{X}$-axis and has an initial spin rate of 2400 rpm and has an initial angle to the vertical of $20\degree$. The top spins down at a rate governed by an exponential decay of form $\omega(t)=\omega_0e^{0.1t}$ such that the spin rate is halved every 10 seconds. Gravity acts on the top in the negative $\hat{x}$-direction. The top has a mass of 20 grams. Relevant details are given in \autoref{fig:Spinning Top}.
Define any additional quantities that you deem necessary to solve the problem.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Write the \gls{state vector} for the system.
    \item  Write the \glspl{eom} for the system. If you choose the Newtonian approach, write the forces and moments of the system. If you choose the \gls{Lagrangian} approach, write the energy equations and constraint equations.
    \item Numerically integrate the system. For this problem, you may be able to use either \gls{Euler angles} or \glspl{quaternion} if you choose your singularity point carefully. 
    \item Create a visualization of the system. Create any other plots that you deem useful.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Helicopter Landing]
You find yourself flying a UH-1 Huey over Hamburger Hill in Vietnam, 1969. While preparing to land on your medevac mission, you glance at your inertial navigation system and are horrified to find it has been blown away by a RPG-7! You must now make the calculations necessary to determine how much of your thrust will be acting directly along the ground. Should this force exceed 1,000 N, the soldiers on the ground will be blown away. You decide to utilize a rotation sequence identical to what was used on the navigation system: a 3-2-1 sequence. With this, you determine the Euler angles to be 0, 0.15, and -0.05 radians, and the force of the engine is known to be 10,000 N. Will the soldiers go tumbling away as you land? A view of what you see with the reference frames imposed on it is shown below:

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{Images/HueyProblemImage.png}
    \caption{Huey Reference Frame}
    \label{fig:HueyFrameImage}
\end{figure}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Building a 6-DoF: Part II]
With some knowledge of attitude dynamics, we're ready to tackle the second part of our 6-DoF rocket simulation. We'll introduce some aerodynamic forces and calculate how these effect the attitude of the rocket.


\end{exercise}

\newpage
\printendnotes[large]
\markboth{NOTES}{NOTES}

\chapter{Aerodynamic Modeling}\label{sec: aerodynamics}
\begin{chapquote}{Werner Heisenberg}
``When I meet God, I am going to ask him two questions: Why relativity? And why turbulence? I really believe he will have an answer for the first.''
\end{chapquote}

Aerodynamics is a topic of great concern to us, as lift and drag are two of the primary forces experienced by our rocket. In \autoref{sec: 3DoF Case} we discuss the 4 fundamental forces on a vehicle and how we define their directions. In that section we did not define the magnitude of the aerodynamic forces, $\vec{F}_L$ and $\vec{F}_D$. The goal of aerodynamics, put shortly, is to calculate these values to the best of our ability.

There is a great amount of theory and analysis that can be done in order to calculate these aerodynamic forces acting on a vehicle. These aerodynamic phenomena -- especially for transonic and supersonic rocket systems -- can be very complicated. In order to keep this section relatively comprehensible, we have decided to only cover a few basic concepts. Some fairly basic modeling can get us $80\%$ of the way to good aerodynamics. The last $20\%$ requires very complex modeling which are beyond the scope of this first volume. We may start our discussion by introducing one of the most fundamental ideas in fluid dynamics: non-dimensionalization. Aerodynamicists very often use non-dimensional quantities, so we may start our exploration of aerodynamics here. 

\section{Non-Dimensionalization}

In previous chapters, you have seen equations for lift and drag. In general, these look like:
$$\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\infty}V^2_{\infty}SC_X$$
This coefficient, $C_X$, is a non-dimensional quantity. It describes the force generated via lift or drag, but the value itself has no dimension. Some quick dimensional analysis shows this to be true:
$$\frac{\text{kg}}{\mathrm{m^3}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{m^2}}{\mathrm{s^2}}\cdot\mathrm{m^2}\cdot C_X=\frac{\mathrm{kg\cdot m}}{\mathrm{s^2}}$$
As we can see, the units are already appropriate to give us $N$ of force, $C_X$ itself should just be a scalar value with no dimension. A large portion of aerodynamics is devoted to figuring out how we can calculate these non-dimensional values!

Before we start with calculations of these values, we should  understand how non-dimensionalized quantities emerge, and which ones are important for aerodynamics. This will help us understand why we have many equations with the term $\frac{1}{2}\rho V^2$, or why the Mach number is important. Along the way, we will also discover some other important properties that influence the airflow. 

Since our main goal is to understand the lift and the drag on an object, we will start by enumerating the various factors that could impact the aerodynamic forces experienced by an object. Some intuition will tell us aerodynamic forces are likely affected by the following factors:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Viscosity of the fluid - higher viscosity creates more `stickiness' in the air that needs to be countered.
    \item Velocity - higher velocity creates more interactions with the fluid at any given time, so it will contribute to our drag force.
    \item Density of fluid - higher density will create more interactions per time.
    \item Characteristic length or size - the size of the object traveling through the fluid will increase the particle interactions per time and drag.
    \item Speed of sound - the properties of the fluid will depend on the speed of sound in the fluid.
    \item \Gls{angle of attack} - the angle of the object will change the apparent size in the airflow.
\end{enumerate}
Already, we can see that some of the terms that we see in the lift and drag equations are present above. However, we do not know in what way they are correlated to each other and what else they depend on! To determine these dependencies, we can invoke \gls{dim anal}. In particular, we can use a specific type of \gls{dim anal} known as the \gls{buck pi}. This will allow us to derive dimensionless quantities that prove to be particularly useful in aerodynamics.\footnote{For more information and practice problems on \gls{buck pi}, refer to 
\cite{wassgren_buckingham_nodate}.}

\begin{theorem}[Buckingham Pi Theorem]

Given a system with $N$ parameters and $K$ physical dimensions, it is possible to express the result of this system with $P$ dimensionless $\Pi$ groups, where $P=N-K$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{corollary}\label{thm: buck pi corollary}
Each $\Pi$ group is some function of all the other $\Pi$ groups:
$$\Pi_1=f\left(\Pi_2, \Pi_3,\cdots \Pi_{N-K}\right)$$
and so on for the other $\Pi$ groups.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Aerodynamic Forces}
    We can apply \gls{buck pi} to the system described above to write the non-dimensional groups for our aerodynamic system. We start by writing the dimensions for each of the variables we enumerated:

$$\begin{array}{ccc}
     \textbf{Variable} & \textbf{Dimension} & \textbf{Description}\\
      \rho & \frac{M}{L^3} & \text{Air Density}\\
     V    & \frac{L}{T} & \text{Velocity}\\
         L & L&\text{Characteristic Length}\\
     \mu &  \frac{M}{LT} & \text{Viscocity}\\
     a & \frac{L}{T} & \text{Speed of Sound}\\
     \alpha & - &\text{Angle of Attack}
\end{array}$$

For \gls{buck pi}, we want to select repeating variables that span the base dimensions and do not want to select two variables that have the same dimensions. Somewhat arbitrarily, we select $\rho$, $V$, and $L$ as the 3 repeating variables.

With each $\Pi$ group, we solve by equating the exponents of the expressions to zero (make them dimensionless, put another way). For example:
$$\Pi_1=\rho^aV^bL^c\mu=M^0L^0T^0$$
Plugging in the dimension of each of these:
$$\Pi_1=\left[\frac{M}{L^3}\right]^a\left[\frac{L}{T}\right]^b[L]^c\left[\frac{M}{LT}\right]=M^0L^0T^0$$
This yields a system of equations with three equations:
\begin{gather}
    M:a+1=0\\L:-3a+b+c-1=0\\T:-b-1=0
\end{gather}
Solving this system gives $a=b=c=-1$. Plugging in these values for the coefficients, we arrive at:
$$\Pi_1=\frac{\mu}{\rho VL}$$
Some readers may recognize that this quantity is $\frac{1}{Re}$.  The same analysis is done for the rest of the $\Pi$ groups. A good exercise is to derive the rest of the groups shown.
\begin{align}
    \Pi_1&=Re=\frac{\rho VL}{\mu}\\
    \Pi_2&=M=\frac{V}{a}\\
    \Pi_3&=C_X=\frac{F}{\rho V^2L^2}\\
    \Pi_4&=\alpha
\end{align}

$\Pi_1$ is a quantity known as the Reynolds number ($Re$), the ratio of inertial forces to viscous forces. $\Pi_2$ is the Mach number ($M$), the ratio of velocity to the speed of sound of the fluid. $\Pi_3$ is the force coefficient ($C_X$) that we have described earlier. $\Pi_4$ is the relation for the \gls{angle of attack}, which is just itself since $\alpha$ is unitless. 

Notice that we are free to multiply any of these groups by arbitrary constants. For example, we may multiply $\Pi_3$ by any constant such that the denominator is instead $\frac{1}{2}\rho V^2 S$, where $S$ is some reference area of our choosing which can be different than $L^2$. It is only necessary that the dimensional units are not altered.

From \autoref{thm: buck pi corollary}, we also have the important idea that each group is some function of the other groups (and importantly, not a function of other variables). This proves to be especially useful if we want to determine the force coefficients for our vehicle. Instead of having to test hundreds of different parameters, we only need to vary a few to find the force coefficient. We can also say that the force coefficient must be a function of the other $\Pi$ groups:
$$\color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}C_x=f\left(Re,M,\alpha\right)}$$
The conclusion drawn from the \gls{buck pi} is that we can determine the force coefficients using only the $Re$, $M$, and $\alpha$. Thus, whenever we do modeling of aerodynamics, we only need to consider Reynolds number, Mach number, and \gls{angle of attack} in our analysis. This is a cornerstone of how we model aerodynamics in our 6-\gls{dof}.

\section{Aerodynamics in a Nutshell}

Aerodynamics has a lot of jargon and ideas associated with it that is important to understand before we can approach modeling the aerodynamics of the system. We will mostly cover aerodynamics terminology for rockets, but the ideas here should be transferable to aspects of aircraft systems as well.\endnote{The principles we describe are the same, but much of the modeling is simpler for rockets since they have axial symmetry in most cases. We will utilize these assumptions early on to simplify this section.} 

\subsection{Lift and Drag}\label{Lift and Drag}

Aerodynamic forces are sometimes treated as mysterious in nature. In fact, you can ask many engineers where lift comes from and you will often be presented with a very convoluted (or incorrect) answer. While a few pages of text won't give you an extraordinarily deep understanding, it should be enough to understand lift and drag with sufficient depth for rocket modeling -- and maybe dispel a few of the false ideas surrounding them.

The first thing to note is that what we call lift and drag are just a convenient way of representing a vector sum of components. We've seen before that:
$$\vec{F}_R=\vec{F}_L+\vec{F}_D$$
When we decompose an aerodynamic force, the convention is that we take the drag to be the component opposite the velocity, and the lift is orthogonal to the freestream velocity. Defining things in terms of the velocity direction is a useful convention and often times is very practical. As a result, the idea of aerodynamic forces have been inextricably linked to the concepts of lift and drag. To learn aerodynamics, it's best to let go of the notion of lift and drag for a bit, and consider aerodynamic forces simply as \textit{pressures} that act on our vehicle. We'll add these pressures up to get one big vector that describes the resultant force that acts on our vehicle.

Looking at things a bit more closely, where do these aerodynamic forces actually come from? The simple answer is that a fluid exerts a pressure distribution on a body. We can imagine that two differential force elements act at each point along the body. At each point, there is a pressure $p$ which acts normal to the surface and a shear stress $\tau$ (you can think of it as a friction) that acts tangentially to the surface. This shear stress arises from the viscosity of the air. These are shown in \autoref{fig:pressure and shear}.  We've added a subscript $u$ to denote that these forces are on the upper surface.

    \label{fig:pressure and shear}
\end{figure}

We can imagine summing up the $p$ and $\tau$ at every point along this body to give us the total aerodynamic force. We could then use the direction of the airflow, $V_\infty$ as our basis, which would give us the familiar lift and drag.

Since a force is a pressure multiplied by an area, $F=pA$, we can integrate our pressure distribution over an area to understand the total forces that are acting. Since we are considering differential elements, we express this in the form of an integral. The form of this integral isn't very important to us, we almost never solve it explicitly.

Briefly, we will switch into the body frame of our vehicle and describe the aerodynamic forces along the $\hat{x}$ and $\hat{y}$ directions. In this frame, we'll give the forces a new name: The \textit{normal} force and the \textit{axial} force. The axial force runs along the $\hat{x}$ direction. We'll denote the total length from the front of our object to the back as $c$, the chord length. Then, we can integrate the pressure forces to generate an axial force (along the $\hat{x}$ direction) and a normal force. Here, we've introduced a subscript $l$ which represents a pressure on the lower surface. These integrals are shown below:
\begin{gather}
    N'=\int_0^{c}{p_lu-p_u}{dx}+\int_0^{c}{\left[\tau_u\frac{dy_u}{dx} + \tau_l\frac{dy_l}{dx}\right]}{dx}\\\\
    A'=\int_0^{c}{\left[p_u\frac{dy_u}{dx} + p_l\frac{dy_l}{dx}\right]}{dx}+\int_0^c{(\tau_u+\tau_l)}{dx}
\end{gather}

Here, the apostrophe refers to the fact that the force acts over a unit length in the $\hat{z}$ direction. We are assuming a unit length into the page. More generally, we would perform this calculation in 3D, but that is beyond the scope of this example. \endnote{You can imagine doing a similar integral with $p$ and $\tau$ as 3-vectors to attain a 3-vector for the aerodynamic force. In that case the subscripts $u$ and $l$ start to make less sense. You can generalize the integration using just the vectors themselves so long as you are careful about your reference frame conventions.}

The normal force acts normal in the $\hat{y}$ direction and the axial force acts in the $\hat {x}$ direction as shown in \autoref{fig:pressure and shear}. Notice that these are \textit{not} the lift and drag, we did things in a different reference frame to make our calculations easy. More often, we see the quantities lift and drag, which are defined with respect to the \gls{freestream} velocity. We can easily transform our normal and axial forces into a lift and drag using a rotation matrix with our \gls{angle of attack}, $\alpha$. This rotation matrix takes the form:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:LDMatrix}
 \begin{bmatrix}
     D'\\L'
 \end{bmatrix}
 =\begin{bmatrix}
        \cos\alpha &\sin\alpha\\
        -\sin\alpha&\cos\alpha
    \end{bmatrix}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        A'\\N'
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
We can expand \eqref{eq:LDMatrix} to give two scalar equations:
\begin{gather}
        L'=N'\cos\alpha-A'\sin\alpha\\
        D'=A'\cos\alpha+N'\sin\alpha
\end{gather}
In 3D, we can do a similar transformation using a DCM like we discuss in \autoref{sec:attitude dynamics}. To do so, we would come up with a DCM that translates between the wind frame (where $L'$ and $D'$ are) and the body frame ($N'$ and $A'$).

So, if we know the distribution of pressure over our body, we have shown that we can find the resultant aerodynamic forces acting on our body. There we have it, knowing the pressure distribution we can easily calculate lift and drag.

There's a few complications, of course. Finding this pressure distribution is pretty difficult. One thing that aerodynamicists do is put pressure taps on a real airfoil and measure the pressure distribution. This gives us true values of the pressure distribution, and we can approximate the lift. It is difficult, however, to attain the drag that way. One other glaring issue is that we need to build either a full-scale or sub-scale model of our vehicle in order to take such measurements. It would often be better to have a theoretical method by which we could determine the lift and drag.

\subsection{Boundary Layers and Potential Flow}

To come up with a theoretical model for our lift and drag, we need to understand how fluid moves past our body. To start modeling the flow around an object, we use some key facts:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item At the surface of an object, the flow `attaches' to the surface and is still (no-slip condition)
    \item At low speeds, air is incompressible.
    \item At our scales, we treat air as a continuous medium.
\end{enumerate}

These three assumptions are empirical in nature. There is no `proof' that I can give you that shows these are true. It's simply what we observe. From these three ideas, we can come up with some simple models of aerodynamics. These models give us a good basis for understanding what our computational models are doing under the hood and how we can come up with approximate models.

Let's start with the first condition. This condition implies that our flow has some kind of layer where the air gets up to speed. It starts at zero velocity and it will eventually reach some external velocity. We call this region where the flow is rapidly changing the `boundary layer'. Depicted diagrammatically, the boundary layer looks like:

    \centering
