    \label{fig:boundary layer}
\end{figure}
In this figure, we're denoting the velocity along the $x$ direction as $u$. There's a few things of note in this figure. Notice first that as $y$ increases (the distance from the surface), the velocity gets closer to $V_{ext}$, the velocity once we're outside of the boundary layer. We typically say we're outside the boundary layer once $u=0.99V_{ext}$, but there is no hard and fast rule. 

The other thing to take from this diagram is that the slope of the line encodes something physical. In fact, this slope gives us the shear stress of the fluid:
$$\tau=\mu\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}$$
We can evaluate this function at $y=0$ and this will give us the $\tau_u$ and $\tau_l$ variables that we found. Great -- we've found a reasonable way to compute something that could give us drag. All we need to know is the viscosity of the fluid and how fast the fluid is changing velocity at the boundary. This is a more approachable problem. Using numerical solvers, we can get good approximations this way.

There's one last thing that we should say about this boundary layer before moving on. As it turns out, the pressure normal to the surface is approximately the same right outside the boundary layer and at the surface.\footnote{Look into the Blasius solution for more information. This is a reduction of the Navier-Stokes equations which we can find analytical solutions to.} This is very crucial for us! Describing the flow inside the boundary layer is complicated, but outside the boundary layer the flow is much easier to describe. Since the pressure is about the same, we can use a simple theory to understand the lift. The simple theory that we will use to understand the flow outside the boundary layer -- and the resulting lift -- is called potential flow.

\subsubsection{Potential Flow}\label{sec:potential flow}

If we can understand the velocity of air around our aircraft or rocket, we can make a pretty good estimate of this pressure distribution and understand the lift. 

When we ignore the boundary layer of air, the flow is pretty simple to describe. There's only a few assumptions that we need and we can create a pretty simple theory for our flow:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Our flow is irrotational.
    \item Our  flow is incompressible.\endnote{We relax this assumption when dealing with high subsonic flows and supersonic flows.}
\end{enumerate}

These assumptions aren't ridiculous because we've accounted for most of the flow rotation and strange effects in the boundary layer. Removing those, the flow field is quite simple and an irrotational flow is valid. There's another thing helping us too, because when our flow is going at large enough speeds, the ratio of inertial to viscous forces ($Re$) is huge. It is not uncommon for this number to exceed $10^6$ or more for aircraft and rockets in standard operation. Under such a condition, our boundary layer becomes very small and we can ignore fluid rotation and viscosity to good approximation (at least when describing lift).

With those assumptions in place, we'll come up with our mathematical model of the fluid flow. When we say that our flow is irrotational, we really mean that:
$$\nabla \times \vec{v}=0$$
where $\vec{v}$ is a vector field of fluid velocities. If you've done vector calculus, you likely recognize that the statement means that the vector field has no curl. This statement is special -- when a vector field has no curl, we can represent it as the gradient of some \textit{potential function}, $\varphi$, which comes from the following identity:
$$\nabla\times(\nabla\varphi)=0$$
\begin{exercise}
Prove that:
$$\nabla\times(\nabla\varphi)=0$$
For any scalar field $\varphi$ of the form $\varphi=\varphi(x,y,z)$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$.
\end{exercise}

Changing from $\vec{v}$ to $\nabla\varphi$ might seem a bit arbitrary, but it gives us a very nice property. Since $\varphi$ is a scalar field, all reference frames will agree on what $\varphi$ looks like. Stated a bit more precisely, $\varphi$ is invariant to coordinate transformations -- there is no need for reference frame translations or anything similar. Because $\varphi$ is scalar, we can add solutions together in superposition. Therefore, we say that the velocity is the gradient of some potential function, $\varphi$:
$$\vec{v}=\nabla\varphi$$

When our air is flowing slowly, it's also an appropriate assumption to say that the air is incompressible. Written with vector calculus, we say that the velocity field has no divergence:
$$\nabla\cdot \vec{v}=0$$
We will replace $\vec{v}$ with $\nabla\varphi$ which gives us the following expression:
$$\nabla\cdot(\nabla\varphi)=0$$
This $\nabla\cdot\nabla$ is often called the \textit{Laplacian}, and it looks like:
$$\nabla\cdot(\nabla\varphi)=\nabla^2\varphi=\left(
\frac{\partial^2\varphi}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2\varphi}{\partial y^2}+\frac{\partial^2\varphi}{\partial z^2}\right)$$
We'll write this equation in shorthand:
\begin{gather}
\color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}\nabla^2\varphi=0}\\
\color{boxBlue}\text{Laplace's Equation}
\end{gather}
This is known as Laplace's equation and it tells us how our flow works when things are moving much less than the speed of sound and there is little viscosity. Any $\varphi$ satisfying this equation can be used to tell us the nature of the flow and we can then solve for the lift using this equation.

To actually solve problems with Laplace's equation, there's one more fact that we need to know. When flow hits a surface, it shouldn't go through. In this inviscid model, we'll allow the flow to slip past the surface though. We have a condition called \textit{flow tangency}, which tells us that at a surface:
$$\vec{v}\cdot\hat{n}=0$$
where $\hat{n}$ is the surface normal.

Before we move on, let's notice one thing about potential flow. Potential flow can calculate the lift on our object. It predicts the fact that the velocity (and thus pressure) is lower on the upper side of a wing. The flow tangency condition gives us problems, though. Because the flow slips past the surface and doesn't `stick' to it, there's no way that potential flow will ever give us a measurement of drag. This is known as \textit{D'Alembert's Paradox}.

Because of this, we have to use some other techniques to solve for the drag. We don't discuss specifics here, but we often use the boundary layer we saw earlier to find approximations for the drag on the vehicle.

We won't get too carried away with potential flows here. There's a good website which shows the equations and a visualization of such flows at \cite{noauthor_potential_2026}.

That was quite a bit of new terminology that we just introduced, so let's hit the big ideas again:
\textbf{
\begin{enumerate}
    \item We can get a good estimate of lift using an irrotational potential flow.
    \item Potential flow cannot give us drag.
    \item We can confine all of our flow rotation and drag to a small `boundary layer'.
    \item Potential flows add in superposition because they are a scalar field.
    \item The pressure right outside the boundary layer is almost the same as at the surface.
\end{enumerate}
}
At this point, we'll stop with potential flow theory in this book. Things become a bit more complicated with flows that are compressible, but we can still use a modification of this theory to model those flows. For our purposes here, a conceptual understanding of potential flow is more important than solving problems with it.

For most of our applications we can use computation tools to determine the velocity field and pressure distribution on an object. Some of these models will directly utilize the potential flow which we've just discussed.\endnote{Some of the other models used in computational fluid dynamics directly solve the Navier-Stokes equations, or some modification of them.} With a pressure and velocity distribution, we can understand the lift and drag on an object.

\subsection{Center of Pressure and Stability}

We've come up with a way to compute the lift and the drag on our rocket by integrating a pressure and shear stress distribution over the entire body. Our knowledge of dynamics tells us that these aerodynamic forces will generate a moment on the rocket.

To simplify our analysis, we would like the find the point where this aerodynamic force produces no moment. That is, we would like the find the point where the resultant aerodynamic force acts. We call this point the center of pressure. Simply stated, the center of pressure is the location at which all the aerodynamic forces acting on a body produce no resultant moment. This location is particularly useful for our case because we can assume that the lift and drag forces act through the center of pressure rather than calculating lift and drag using a distributed load. As you may imagine, this representation greatly simplifies our dynamics.

To find the location where the center of pressure occurs, we can take the moment about any point first. Often, we choose to take the moment about the nose of the rocket since we define the origin here. This integral has a similar form to the normal and axial force integrals except that we multiply the force by the distance from the nose to obtain a moment.\footnote{For more information on why this is done, refer to \cite{gundersen_understanding_2020}.} Writing out this integral, we get:
\begin{gather}
    M_{LE}'=\int_0^c{p_u-p_l}{dx}+\int_0^c{\left(\tau_u\frac{dy_u}{dx}+\tau_l\frac{dy_l}{dx}\right)}{xdx}\\+\int_0^c{\left(p_u\frac{dy_u}{dx}+\tau_u\right)}{y_udx}+\int_0^c{\left(-p_l\frac{dy_l}{dx}+\tau_l\right)}{y_ldx}
\end{gather}
Knowing the normal force and the moment about the leading edge, we can move our point of reference until there is no moment produced. We can see this diagrammatically in \autoref{fig:moment leading}.

    \label{fig:moment leading}

We can write this moment balance to find how far along the $\hat{x}$ axis we need to go before the moment is zero:\endnote{We assume that the center of pressure is directly along the $\hat{x}$ axis and there is no component in $\hat{y}$. Because rockets are so narrow and symmetric, this is a fine assumption.}

$$M'_{LE}=r\times F_R$$
Notice that the axial force does not contribute to the moment, so we only need to consider the normal force:
$$M'_{LE}=x_{cp}N'$$
One last thing, aerodynamicists have adopted a very backwards convention for moments. In aerodynamics, a moment that pitches the vehicle up is positive. As a result, we have to flip the sign of our moment in this equation. Solving, we find the center of pressure location as:
\begin{gather}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}x_{cp}=\frac{-M_{LE}'}{N'}}
\end{gather}

With this equation in hand, let's notice one key fact. The center of pressure is \textit{not} a fixed location. As the angle of attack changes, the $N'$ force will change, as will the moment. There is no guarantee that these change in exact proportion to each other. Another thing that will become important later -- this location will also change with Mach number.

One quick note before moving on. We can also define a moment coefficient, which is:
$$C_M=\frac{M}{q_{\infty}Sc}$$
Unlike the $C_L$ and $C_D$, we need an additional length $c$ to make this quantity dimensionless. So, we could also express the center of pressure in terms of these dimensionless quantities:
$$x_{cp}=\frac{-C_{M_{LE}}}{C_N}c$$

For a rocket, we generally consider the body to be \gls{axisym} about the longitudinal axis, when the whole structure can be rotated about the axis which we define as $\hat{X}$.  Because our rocket is \gls{axisym}, we assume that the center of pressure always lies along the longitudinal axis. Although this assumption is not necessarily true, as we will see later, the largest effects on the aerodynamic moments will occur from changes along the longitudinal direction rather than changes along the transverse plane.

\subsubsection{Stability}

Now that we can solve for the location of the center of pressure, the question of stability comes up. We naturally have experience with aerodynamic stability: when you throw a dart forward it flies straight, but if you try to throw it backward it flails through the air awkwardly. The stability of the dart is related to the relative positions of the center of mass and the center of pressure.

\draw (133,180.4) node [anchor=north west][inner sep=0.75pt]    {$V_{\infty }$};


\end{tikzpicture}

    \caption{Unstable Dart}
    \label{fig:unstable dart}
\end{figure}

The figure caption may have given it away, but this configuration is unstable. The angle of attack of the airflow creates lift, but this lift creates a moment which points the tail of the dart further upwards! As a result, the dart spins out of control.

The situation is exactly paralleled by a rocket. To maintain stability, we need the center of pressure to be behind the center of mass for all of the expected angles of attack and mach numbers that our rocket will encounter. Remember that the center of pressure will move with angle of attack and Mach number. Thus, creating a stable rocket is a bit more difficult than it seems at first glance.

Luckily, we have a rule of thumb for rockets which allows us to maintain good stability in almost all flight regimes. We'll define a quantity called a stability caliber, which is:
$$S_C=\frac{(x_{cp}-x_{cm})}{D}$$
Where $D$ is the diameter of the rocket and $S_C$ is the stability caliber. Generally, we choose $S_C$ to be in the range of $2-4$. 

With that rule of thumb being discussed, it's important that we note some limitations of this approach. As mentioned before, the center of pressure and center of mass are both moving throughout the flight. The stability is going to change over time. Additionally, the stability caliber and our previous analysis tells us nothing about \textit{dynamic stability} -- how long will it take our rocket to converge to a stable condition, and how does it get there?

Those questions are a bit beyond the scope of our discussion here, but luckily our modeling with a 6-\gls{dof} can help us answer those questions without having to dive deeply into the theory.

\subsection{Mach Number}

We have one last stop on our short journey through `Aerodynamics in a Nutshell', which is to discuss the Mach number. We derived this quantity earlier, it's simply the ratio of our velocity and the speed of sound of the fluid:
$$M=\frac{V}{a}$$
To understand Mach number and compressible flow in detail, we need an understanding of thermodynamics. We won't cover any of those details here, but rather a more conceptual overview of what happens in various different mach number regimes.

\subsubsection{Subsonic compressible flow}

We'll start by considering flow that is moving slow. We typically consider flows where $M<0.3$ to be slow enough that the effects of compressibility are ignorable.\endnote{Our condition for `ignorable' is that there is less than a 5\% deviation between the compressible and incompressible flow models.} As we have seen before, such a flow can be modeled well by Laplace's equation / potential flow.

When our flow is faster than $M=0.3$ to about $M=0.75$, we can utilize a transformation called the Prandtl-Glauert rule. This rule scales our lift as
$$C_L=\frac{C_{L_0}}{\sqrt{1-M^2}}$$
Where $C_{L_0}$ is the lift coefficient in the incompressible regime. We can do the same for the moment coefficient:
$$C_M=\frac{C_{M_0}}{\sqrt{1-M^2}}$$
Notice that under this transformation, the center of pressure stays the same regardless of Mach number. We know from experiments and simulation that this is \textit{not} true.  This transformation is just an approximation of the true aerodynamics and it's important to keep in mind that such relations are approximate.

\subsubsection{Transonic Flow}

These equations do a fairly good job of adjusting the lift and moment acting on a rocket as it approaches higher subsonic flows. As $M\rightarrow 1$, though, the denominator goes to zero and these equations become very inaccurate. In this regime between $M\approx0.8$ and $M\approx1.2$, we are in \textit{transonic flow}, where parts of the vehicle may exceed the speed of sound and other parts are slower than the speed of sound. Quantifying the flow in such regimes is quite difficult and beyond the scope of our text here. One approach is to do some linear or sigmoid fit of the data between the high subsonic and supersonic regimes where we are more certain of aerodynamics.

\subsubsection{Supersonic flow}

Once things are moving faster than the speed of sound, our aerodynamics become quite a bit simpler again. In this regime, the flow past our vehicle is faster than the speed at which information propagates through the fluid (the speed of sound in that fluid). As a result, our flow characteristics are quite unintuitive. Luckily, we still have some analytical expressions we can use to quantify the lift and drag in this regime. We'll detail the results of supersonic linear theory here, which tells us that:
$$C_L=\frac{4\alpha}{\sqrt{M^2-1}}$$
Drag is more complicated. In supersonic aerodynamics, we have something called \textit{wave drag}, which is the result of supersonic pressure waves acting on the vehicle. This gives us an additional drag on top of the drag from the shear stress of the fluid. We can approximate this drag with the following formula:
$$C_{D_W}=\frac{4}{\sqrt{M^2-1}}\left[\alpha^2+K_2+K_3\right]$$
The functions $K_2$ and $K_3$ are dependent on the geometry of the vehicle. These are:
\begin{gather}
    K_2=\int_0^1\left(\frac{d\hat{y}_c}{d\hat{x}}\right)^2d\hat{x}\\
     K_3=\int_0^1\left(\frac{d\hat{y}_t}{d\hat{x}}\right)^2d\hat{x}
\end{gather}
Here, the hat on the values means that these have been normalized. $\hat{x}$ is normalized so that the length of the vehicle is $1$. We also adjust the $y$ variables according to the same scaling. The variable $y_c$ represents the height of the mean camber line (the mean displacement from the horizontal) and the $y_t$ variable the thickness of the body. For a rocket, the camber is almost always zero due to symmetry and we can comfortably set $K_2=0$.

\subsubsection{Summary}
Altogether, we can get a rough approximation of aerodynamics with the equations described above. There are a few things that we didn't cover in detail, like how we can calculate the drag on the vehicle accurately, or how it changes in the subsonic regime. Doing so is more complicated, but there are empirical correlations that exist. Altogether, these empirical methods usually get us to within $10\%-20\%$ of the true values of aerodynamic coefficients. They are `good enough' for preliminary analysis, sizing, and other applications where exact aerodynamic values aren't needed. In fact, most of the computational tools we discuss later follow these exact methods or some modification of them.

\section{Atmospheric Modeling}
Another hurdle to overcome when describing the aerodynamics of our system is to find values for our \gls{freestream} quantities. We explore the calculation of $V_{\infty}$ in \autoref{sec: Newtonian Dynamics}. The other parameters we need to calculate are $\rho_{\infty}$ and $a_{\infty}$. Fortunately, the standard density and Mach number of the atmosphere on Earth is well defined. In MATLAB, we can find this density at any altitude with "atmosisa" as:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
[T, a, P, rho] = atmosisa(alt);
\end{matlabscript}
In Python, we can use the $\texttt{ambiance}$ package which gives us these outputs using dot indexing.
\begin{pythonscript}{}
from ambiance import Atmosphere
sealevel = Atmosphere(0)
sealevel.pressure
sealevel.temperature
\end{pythonscript}

This gives us a standard value of $\rho_{\infty}$ and $a_{\infty}$ to use. However, the value of can vary by up to 10\% depending on the weather conditions \cite{svickova_air_2020}. Similarly, the Mach number has a strong dependence on temperature $\left(M=\sqrt{\gamma RT}\right)$, so the free-stream Mach number may also be different by 10-20\% of standard values. Further derivation of this mach relation can be found in \cite{filmer_fluid_nodate}.

All of this is to say that using "atmosisa" gives us a good baseline model of the atmosphere, but is not the final say in atmospheric modeling. Thus it is useful to use other sources of atmospheric data that give location- and time-specific data.
\subsection{Live Weather Data}
Getting live weather information is useful in improving upon the uncertainty in the calculations provided by the "atmosisa" or other standard atmosphere models. 
To do so, using live or historic weather data is a good option. Our model uses information from the $\texttt{open-meteo}$ API \cite{openmeteo_openmeteo_nodate}, though several types of services like this exist. Another benefit of such live weather data is that it allows us to compute wind at the surface level and at many pressure levels (up to $22 \mathrm{\ km}$ in height). 

Generally, MATLAB alone cannot perform requests to an API, so that task is performed by a Python script. In this case, we can use the MATLAB-Python interface to import the data from the Python script into the MATLAB environment. More information about this MATLAB Python interfacing can be found at \cite{mathworks_using_nodate}.

Sometimes, we can only get weather data for close to the surface from real time or historic measurements. In this case, it is often a good approximation to use the standard atmosphere model provided by something like "atmosisa" offset to match the temperature and pressure measured. A suitable comparison is shown in \autoref{fig:live atmos}. While temperature can change quite drastically, pressure and density profiles vary little from the standard atmosphere if they are aligned to be equal at the first ground level geopotential height.

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Images/StandardAtmosComparison.png}
    \caption{Comparison of live weather data with standard atmosphere, Mojave Desert}
    \label{fig:live atmos}
\end{figure}

Even with an API which provides close to real time weather data, the weather cannot be modeled perfectly. As a result, it is important to understand the limitations of such models and design the simulation to account for such uncertainties. 

\section{Computational Aerodynamics}\label{sec:computational aero}
In the case of our rocket, we encounter a variety of flight regimes from subsonic to high supersonic. Because of this variety, hand calculations of our aerodynamics can become very complicated. As a result, we choose to use computational tools to compute the aerodynamics of our rocket.
\subsection{RasAero}
The main tool that we use presently is RasAero. This tool allows us to input the geometry of the rocket and will output the \gls{drag coeff} as a function of the Mach number. This is less than perfect, as we lose some of the dependence on Reynolds number. Additionally, the dependence on \gls{angle of attack} is quite course (only measurements every 2$\degree$). That being said, RasAero has been used to model many rocket systems around the same scale as our rocket with an average error in apogee of $3.47\%$ and $80\%$ of the flights within $\pm10\%$ of the true apogee. For us, this is an acceptable compromise so that we can focus on flight dynamics modeling rather than aerodynamics.

More information on RasAero can be found at \cite{rogers_rasaero_2019}. The documentation on this page does a better job explaining the software than we could here in a short summary. Our implementation uses the .csv export files from RasAero in MATLAB.

\subsection{Other Computational Models}

It is worth noting that other computational models exist. RasAero does have shortcomings in modeling some of the aspects of the rocket geometry. For fast simulation, one other option is OpenRocket \cite{noauthor_openrocket_2026}.

Both OpenRocket and RasAero use empirical correlations and some basic aerodynamic theory for their models, which lets them compute aerodynamics quickly. This is good for early design, as we want to rapidly change parameters and see a rough indiciation of how this will impact the vehicle parameters.

Once a design is more locked down, more high fidelity aerodynamic models can be created. One popular option is \gls{cfd}. These methods directly solve the partial differtial equations of fluid dynamics. However, these tools are often difficult to set up and require extensive knowledge to get good results.

% \section{Wind Modeling}
% Add our wind modelling stuff here (might actually want to model wind first lmao)
\section{Key Ideas}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item When we model a system such as our rocket, we can capture most of the phenomena by capturing data at different $Re$, $M$, and $\alpha$. This is true for \gls{cfd}, wind tunnel modeling, or analytical approaches.
    \item We can use the distance between the center of pressure and center of mass to determine the aerodynamic stability of a rocket. The stability caliber gives us a good measure of static stability.
    \item While analytical solutions are hard to attain in aerodynamics, we can use computational tools to estimate aerodynamic parameters of our vehicle.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Further Reading}

This chapter has several notable gaps because we cannot cover all aspects of fluid dynamics in this book. There are many good resources for aerodynamic modeling for those with further interest. The first among these, which serves as a good introduction to the topic of fluid dynamics, is \cite{anderson_fundamentals_2017}. For more complex studies of aerodynamics and compressible flows, we recommend \cite{anderson_modern_2021}. A more theoretical perspective on fluid dynamics suited for physics is given in \cite{landau_fluid_2013}.

\section{Practice Problems}

You are flying in your P-51 Mustang (pictured in \autoref{fig:p51} for your viewing pleasure). At cruise, your aircraft is 10,000 ft above sea level.

    \label{fig:p51}

    \item Your wings provide a $C_L$ of 0.3 at cruising speed. Your plane has a mass of 4,000 kg and a planform area of $21.8 \mathrm{\ m}^2$. Find the speed necessary to maintain level light at this altitude.
    \item Your airplane is equipped with  unguided HVAR rockets. As a daring pilot, you want to model the aerodynamics of this rocket so you can launch it with astounding accuracy.

You push your throttles to max to book it back home. At full throttle, your Merlin V12 produces 1676 hp (1250 kw). Your aircraft has a $C_D$ of 0.017. \begin{exercise}[Building a 6-DoF: Part III]
We'll continue our project and introduce aerodynamics into the mix. Remember that our rocket has a length of $1 \text{ m}$ and a diameter of $0.05 \text{ m}$. Now, we'll introduce a \textit{Von Karman} nosecone of length $0.2 \text{ m}$.

These fins have a span of $0.05 \text{ m}$, a root chord of $0.1 \text{ m}$, a tip chord of $0.04 \text{ m}$, a sweep of $0.04 \text{ m}$. The fins are placed at the back of the rocket body. \chapter{Numerical Integration}\label{sec:numerical integration schemes}

In our exploration of \gls{numerical int} schemes, it will be useful to have a good understanding of ordinary differential equations. We highly recommend utilizing \cite{dawkins_differential_2023} for reviewing the necessary differential equations. 

\section{Euler Integration}\label{sec:euler integration}
We have previously discussed the simplest integration scheme, the explicit Euler method, which we used in \autoref{sec: Numerical Integration in the 1DoF}. Understanding the Euler method is key to understanding and using more complex integration schemes, so we recommend writing a few of your own Euler integration scripts in MATLAB or a coding language of your choice to really solidify your knowledge before moving on to more complex integration schemes.\footnote{For more information and derivations on this section, refer to \cite{trench_31_2020}. }

We formalize the explicit Euler integration below. Given any first order ODE of the form\endnote{This equation does not need to be linear. We may have any function of $f(x,y)$ or even a time dependence. Since we are approximating numerically, there are very few limitations on this ODE.}:
$$\frac{dy}{dt}=f\left(x,y\right)$$
With initial condition:
$$y(x_0)=y_0$$
We perform Euler integration first by defining an interval over which to perform the \gls{numerical int}. For Euler integration, we equally space these points by our timestep, $\Delta t$. We can parameterize these points as:
$$x_i=x_0+i\Delta t$$
Our next step is to find the value of the tangent line at each point. This is trivial, since our differential equation tells us what $\frac{dy}{dt}$ looks like at any value. All we have to do is evaluate:
$$\frac{dy}{dt}=f\left(x_i,y(x_i)\right)$$
The problem here is that $y(x_i)$ is an unknown quantity (if we knew it, we wouldn’t have to do integration). So, to find the value of $y(x_i)$ we take an iterative approach. We know the starting value, $y(x_0)=y_0$, so we can find an approximation of the new value by multiplying the timestep by the current slope. For the first timestep, this will look like:
$$y_1=y_0+\Delta t\cdot f(x_0,y_0)$$
Now that we have found an approximation for $y_1$, we can extend this to find approximations for $y_2$, $y_3$, etc. This formula is generally extensible as:
\begin{gather}
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}y_{i+1}=y_i+\Delta t \cdot f(x_i,y_i)}\\
    \color{boxBlue}\mathrm{Euler's \ Method}
\end{gather}

We represent this Euler integration scheme graphically with a simple example. Suppose our differential equation is $\frac{dy}{dt}=2t$ with initial condition $y(0)=0$. In this example, we can use separation of variables that the true solution curve is $y=t^2$. Performing Euler integration with a $\Delta t$ of 0.5 yields the black approximation in \autoref{fig:Euler}.

%\textcolor{red}{Make the font bigger on these plots}
\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{Images/ExplicitEuler.png}
  \caption{Explicit Euler Solution vs. True Solution}
    \label{fig:Euler}
\end{figure}

As can be seen from \autoref{fig:Euler}, large timesteps quickly lead to error in the solution. For a strictly convex curve, our Euler approximation will always give us an under-approximation of the solution. In some cases, however, our solution may diverge so greatly that it becomes unstable and values tend toward infinity.\endnote{This is especially true for periodic functions. When working with periodic functions, make sure to sample well below the Nyquist limit.}
 
% In Table 2 below, we describe the benefits and drawbacks of Euler integration.

% \begin{table}[ht]
% \begin{tabular}{ll}
% Benefits                                                                                                                                                    & Drawbacks                                                                                                                                                             \\
% \begin{tabular}[c]{@{}l@{}}Simple\\ Good for very quick implementations / testing\\ Robust and easy to implement\\ Good for simple explanation\end{tabular} & \begin{tabular}[c]{@{}l@{}}Less precise than other integrator schemes\\ More prone to solution singularities\\ Generally has fixed timestep calculations\end{tabular}
% \end{tabular}
% \end{table}

\subsection{Errors in the Euler Method}\label{sec:errors in the euler method}
We will use the example of the Euler Method to demonstrate error in numerical integrators. These same concepts will apply when we move on to higher order integrators. Errors in the integration scheme come from two different sources:

1. Euler integration schemes only account for linear terms because it only considers the slope of the tangent line at a point. Thus, any quadratic order terms or higher will not be accounted for. Thus, the error is proportional to the timestep squared, $\Delta t^2$. We call this error \gls{trunc error}.\endnote{This intuitive explanation is not entirely correct, see \cite{trench_31_2020} for a full derivation using the Taylor Series. We also note that the technically correct way to denote this is $\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^2)$, meaning the error is of order $\Delta t^2$}

2. Another problem is the fact that computers don’t have infinite precision. The loss of precision during computing is called \gls{roundoff error}. Computing more timesteps in total will increase this \gls{roundoff error}.

It is worth noting that these errors are per timestep! So over the course of a full simulation, our errors will grow much larger than the error bounds that we’ve calculated above. In fact, for the Euler Method described above, the global error is proportional to the timestep, $\Delta t$.

We can of course, reduce errors in the simulation by reducing our timestep, $\Delta t$. However, this will also increase the computation time. Additionally, doing so will increase the \gls{roundoff error} in the simulation. With more calculations being done, the \gls{roundoff error} will have a larger impact. Thus, it is worthwhile to investigate other integration schemes.

\section{Improved Euler / RK2}
Given the limitations of the explicit Euler method, we will start by investigating the improved Euler Method. This improved Euler method is the first of a family of integration schemes known as Runge-Kutta (RK) integration schemes. The number 2 represents the order of the integration scheme, which we will explore more later.

The main contribution to inaccuracies in the explicit Euler method was the slope that we calculated. We can improve on this by taking two points and finding the average slope. We choose to take a point at our start and our end point, $x_i$ and $x_{i+1}$, and evaluate the slope at both of those points and average them. From the previous section, we can remember that:
\begin{equation}
    \frac{dy}{dt}=f\left(x_i,y(x_i)\right)
\end{equation}
So, we can write the average slope as
\begin{equation}
    m=\frac{\dot{y}_1+\dot{y}_2}{2}
\end{equation}
Where
\begin{gather}
    \dot{y}_1=\frac{dy_1}{dt}=f\left(x_i,y(x_i)\right)\\
    \dot{y}_2=\frac{dy_2}{dt}=f\left(x_{i+1},y(x_{i+1})\right)
\end{gather}
Now that we have the slope, we follow the same process as we did for the explicit Euler method to find the next value. We take the slope and multiply it by the timestep, $\Delta t$, and add the current value, $y(x_i)$. This gives us our approximation as:
\begin{equation}
    y=y(x_i)+\frac{\dot{y}_1+\dot{y}_2}{2}\Delta t
\end{equation}
We can write this in general as:
\begin{equation}
    y_{i+1}=y_i+\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left[f(x_i,y(x_i))+f(x_{i+1},y(x_{i+1}))\right]
\end{equation}
To rewrite this equation in a way that is better suited for code:
\begin{gather}\label{eq:rk2}
    y_{i+1}=y_i+\frac{\Delta t}{2}(k_1+k_2)\\\\
    k_1=f(x_i,y_i)\\
    k_2=f(x+\Delta t, y_i+\Delta tk_1)
\end{gather}
We can note here that this method requires at least two evaluations since $k_2$ is dependent on $k_1$. However, this is often still more computationally efficient than the explicit Euler method because larger time steps can be used with lesser error.

\subsection{RK2 in Code}
Implementing the RK2 algorithm in code is slightly more complicated than implementing the explicit Euler method. To properly implement this, we will use \glspl{func handle} in MATLAB. \Glspl{func handle} act as pointers to an instance of a function. To explain what that means, we will examine the following code snippet:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
f = @computeSquare;
a = 4;
b = f(a)
\end{matlabscript}
where "computeSquare" is defined as:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
function y = computeSquare(x)
y = x.^2;
end
\end{matlabscript}

The \gls{func handle} allows us to assign the function to a variable, which we are calling "f". We call this a pointer because this variable ‘points’ to the function "computeSquare".

In this example, when we call this operation, we get "b=16" in the command window. The power of this is that we could instead pass in a vector of values for "a" and get outputs from the function for all of them. If we instead input:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
f = @computeSquare;
a = [4,6,10];
b = f(a)
\end{matlabscript}

We get an output of "b = [16  36  100]". The usefulness of this is immediately apparent for \gls{numerical int}. We can pass in a vector of many different elements and get outputs for each of them. 

Another useful feature of \glspl{func handle} for \gls{numerical int} is that they allow us to pass in only the variables needed for \gls{numerical int} and pass other variables in the expression as constants. In general, we want our numerical scheme to only accept our \gls{state vector} and the time vector. This is the same thing that "ode45" does, so our function replicates that behavior. Our RK2 algorithm is a direct translation of \eqref{eq:rk2} into code. We present this below:

\begin{matlabscript}{RK2}
%% RK2 Integrator
% numerically integrates an initial value problem using RK2
% inputs:
% fun - function of integration
% dt - time step [s]
% tIn - input time [s]
% xIn - initial value input vector
% outputs:
% out - final value output vector
function out = rk2(fun, dt, tIn, xIn)
    f1 = fun(tIn,xIn);
    f2 = fun(tIn + dt/2, xIn + dt .* f1);
    out = xIn + (dt / 2)*(f1 + f2);
end
\end{matlabscript}

%% add more on making functions and rk2.

\section{RK4 Integration}\label{sec: rk4}
RK4 integration is the last major integration scheme that we will explore. RK4 integration is often the best balance between the precision of the algorithm and the speed of computation. 

To explain RK4, we will go back to RK2 and explain it in a slightly different way that will shed light on what we are doing with RK4. In RK2, we described taking the average slope of the function. We can also imagine taking the Taylor expansion of our function:
$$y_{i+1}=y_i+\dot{y}_i\Delta t+\ddot{y}_i\frac{\Delta t}{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\Delta t^3\right)$$
From this Taylor expansion of the solution, we can see that our approximation accounts for linear and quadratic terms. Thus, our error is proportional to terms of the 3\textsuperscript{rd} power, represented by $\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^3)$. We know $y_i$ and $\dot{y}_i$, but the 2\textsuperscript{nd} order derivative of our function is unknown. We can approximate this 2\textsuperscript{nd} order derivative as:
$$y_i=\frac{\dot{y}_{i+1}-\dot{y}_i}{\Delta t}$$
Upon rearranging this equation, we can see that we arrive at the same expression as we did before. We can apply the same approach for RK4 integration, except we consider terms up to the 4\textsuperscript{th} order expansion of the Taylor series:\endnote{We change from the dot notation to the parenthesis when the derivative becomes high order.}
$$y_{i+1}=y_i+\dot{y}_i\Delta t+\ddot{y}_i\frac{\Delta t}{2}+{y_i}^{(3)}\frac{\Delta t}{6}+{y_i}^{(4)}\frac{\Delta t}{24}+\mathcal{O}\left(\Delta t^5\right)$$
Immediately, we can see that our error has shrunk to be proportional to terms of the 5\textsuperscript{th} order, a dramatic reduction as compared to RK2 or the explicit Euler method.

\subsection{RK4 Integration in Code}
The RK4 Integration scheme can be expressed as a set of equations very similar to what we did with \eqref{eq:rk2}. We will not derive these equations here, because the process is very similar to that used for the RK2 method. The form of these equations is shown in \eqref{eq:RK4}.
\begin{gather}\label{eq:RK4}
    y_{i+1}=y_i+\frac{\Delta t}{6}\left(k_1+2k_2+2k_3+k_4\right)\\\\
    k_1=f(x_i,y_i)\\
    k_2=\left(x_i+\frac{\Delta t}{2},y_i+\frac{\Delta t}{2}k_1\right)\\
    k_3=\left(x_i+\frac{\Delta t}{2},y_i+\frac{\Delta t}{2}k_2\right)\\
    k_4=f(x_i+\Delta t,y_i+\Delta tk_3)
\end{gather}
An implementation of this algorithm into code is shown below.

\begin{matlabscript}{RK4 Integrator}
%% RK4 Integrator
% numerically integrate initial value problem
% inputs:
% fun - function of integration
% dt - time step [s]
% tIn - input time [s]
% xIn - initial value input vector
% outputs:
% out - final value output vector
function out = rk4(fun, dt, tIn, xIn)
    f1 = fun(tIn,xIn);
    f2 = fun(tIn + dt/2, xIn + (dt/2) .* f1);
    f3 = fun(tIn + dt/2, xIn + (dt/2) .* f2);
    f4 = fun(tIn + dt, xIn + dt*f3);
    
    out = xIn + (dt / 6)*(f1 + 2*f2 + 2*f3+f4);
end
\end{matlabscript}
Often, we use a function in MATLAB called $\texttt{ode45}$ to perform RK4 integration. This function has the benefit that it will change the step size to produce high accuracy when needed and decrease computation time otherwise.

\begin{exercise}[Lorenz Attractor]
An understanding of the operation of $\texttt{ode45}$ is best motivated by an example. In the GitHub, find the script:

\texttt{Scripts\textbackslash Lorenz Attractor\textbackslash LorenzAttractor.m}

The system in question is a Lorenz attractor. This system has the following set of differential equations:
\begin{align}
\dot{x}&=\sigma(y-x)\\
\dot{y}&=x(\rho-z)-y\\
\dot{z}&=xy-\beta z
\end{align}

This example shows a simple set of ODEs integrated using $\texttt{ode45}$. We recommend reading through this code and running it, as well as changing parameters to see how it affects the outputs. 
\end{exercise}

\subsection{ode45 \& solve\_ivp Usage}
While examples are helpful, it is useful to see the inner workings of $\texttt{ode45}$ for a new user.  "ode45" relies on the \glspl{func handle} that we mentioned earlier. "ode45" takes as inputs the integration function, time span, and initial \gls{state vector}.

"ode45" only takes integration functions that are a function of time and the state variable, or "(t,x)" in MATLAB notation. If we have an integration function that is a function of more than just these two variables, we need to pass them through using \glspl{func handle}. We can do this in the following manner:
\begin{matlabscript}{Function Handle}
@(t,x) integrator(t,x,a,b)
\end{matlabscript}
\begin{matlabscript}{ode45 call}
[t,y] = ode45(@(t,x) integrator(t,x,a,b), x0, tspan, opt)
\end{matlabscript}

In this example, we have a function that performs our \gls{numerical int}, called "integrator", the output of which will be the derivative of our \gls{state vector}. We want "ode45" to see this function and only input "(t,x)", so we put that in a \gls{func handle} at the front. After the \gls{func handle}, we are free to pass in any other variables, such as "a" and "b". These will simply be read in as constants in the "ode45" integration scheme.

The benefit of adding these in as a function pass through instead of being computed in "ode45" will become apparent when we discuss code optimization in \autoref{sec: code optimization}. This also keeps our code hierarchy cleaner and more easily modifiable.

We can do the same thing in Python using the \texttt{scipy} package.
\begin{pythonscript}{}
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp

t_span = (0, 5)
t_eval = np.linspace(0, 5, 100)

sol = solve_ivp(integrator, t_span, x0, t_eval)
\end{pythonscript}

We can define our integrator function like:
\begin{pythonscript}{}
def integrator(t, y):
    return -2 * y
\end{pythonscript}

\section{Numerical Integration Considerations}\label{sec:other numerical ints}
Numerical integration requires that we understand when our algorithms may fail or perform inadequately. In this section, we will investigate sources of failure and how we can use different algorithms or adjustments to fix such issues.
\subsection{More on Error}
We briefly discuss the error of the integration schemes in each of the previous sections. However, it is useful to see examples of these to solidify understanding and show the differences in real application.

Using the same example differential equation from \autoref{sec:errors in the euler method} as $\frac{dy}{dt}=2t$, we can show the approximation for Explicit Euler, RK2, and RK4. This is shown in \autoref{fig:Numerical Comparison}.
\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{Images/ComparisonNumInt.png}
    \caption{Comparison of Numerical Integration Schemes for $y'=2t$}
    \label{fig:Numerical Comparison}
\end{figure}


As we can see from this example, even with a fairly large timestep of $\Delta t=0.5s$, the RK4 perfectly matches the true curve. For this differential equation, there are no terms of higher order than quadratic, so the RK4 will perfectly match the solution curve. RK2 shows a larger error, and the Euler integrator shows an even greater error.
\begin{exercise}[Numerical Integration Practice]
Access the code in the GitHub for this example, located at: 

\texttt{Scripts\textbackslash Numerical Integration\textbackslash Error.m} 

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Adjust the step size of the simulation. Try decreasing the step size until each integrator has a final error of less than 1\%. How small does $\Delta t$ need to be for each integrator?
    \item Try some different function for integration. You can adjust this by changing the function:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
function[out] = YDot(t,Y)
out = 2 * t;
end
\end{matlabscript}
Try a few different functions, and compare the analytical solutions against the integrator. You can also try some which you don't know the analytical solution too. How might you try to quantify error for these?

\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

The difference in these \gls{numerical int} schemes will become more apparent when modeling the behavior of different differential equation systems. Systems containing higher order terms will be better modeled by the higher order integration schemes, unsurprisingly. The true use of these methods is when analytical solutions to the differential equations are hard or impossible to obtain. That being said, we still want to know characteristics of our system so we can appropriately select integration parameters.

\subsubsection{Reducing Error with ode45}
When using $\texttt{ode45}$, there are a number of ways that we can reduce the error in the simulation. The $\texttt{ode45}$ integration comes with an options dialog that allows us to change the integration tolerances. We can use $\texttt{odeset}$ to do so:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
opt = odeset('RelTol',1e-12,'AbsTol',1e-12)    
\end{matlabscript}

These options can be passed into $\texttt{ode45}$ after all standard inputs:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
[t,y] = odes45(@(t,y)integrator(t,y), tspan, x0, opt)
\end{matlabscript}

The default values for $\texttt{RelTol}$ and $\texttt{AbsTol}$ are $\texttt{1e-3}$. The relative tolerance controls the acceptable level of error relative to the absolute magnitude of the state. Given a solution value at the current timestep, the relative tolerance bounds the local truncation error (LTE) to below the relative tolerance.
$$\text{LTE} \leq \text{RelTol} \times |y|$$
The same is done for the absolute tolerance, but there is no consideration for the magnitude of the state. It simply ensures that the LTE is below the absolute tolerance threshold:
$$\text{LTE} \leq \text{AbsTol}$$
Lowering these tolerances will often give higher accuracy solutions for most ODEs, but comes with the tradeoff of computational accuracy. Unless accuracy near floating point precision is required, the default tolerances are usually adequate.

\subsection{Sampling and Stiffness}\label{sampling and stiffness}
 To begin our discussion of sampling and stiffness, we can take the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dt}=\cos(4\pi t)$ with the initial condition of $y(0)=0$. This function is periodic in nature, so it will be good for our demonstration here. In this first example, we will keep the same timestep of $\Delta t=0.5s$. As is seen in \autoref{fig:Numerical Int Periodic}, this timestep is the same as the period of the sinusoidal solution of this differential equation. When this is the case, we can see that the solutions diverge from the original periodic function greatly for all of the integration schemes (aside from the RK2, although this is only the case when the period is exactly the same as the timestep). This occurs because we are not sampling the function at a high enough frequency to capture its periodic nature.

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Images/Numerical Integrator Nyquist 1.png}
    \caption{Numerical Integration of periodic function}
    \label{fig:Numerical Int Periodic}
\end{figure}
So, what timestep is required to accurately model a periodic function? For our example, we will use RK4 since we have shown it to be the most accurate of the integration schemes. We choose to do this to highlight effects aside from error in the numerical approximation. We can vary the timestep for this same periodic function and see what the response looks like. This is shown in \autoref{fig:Nyquist}.
\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth]{Images/Numerical Integrator Nyquist 2.png}
    
    \caption{Numerical Integration of Periodic Function with varying timestep}
    \label{fig:Nyquist}
\end{figure}

In this figure, we note several important things. When our timestep is slightly less than the period of the function, we infer a lower frequency. This is seen when we use a timestep of $\Delta t=0.4s$. We can explain this as a \gls{beat freq}. We know that the solution curve has a frequency of 2 Hz, and a wave with a period of $0.4 s$ would have a frequency of 2.5 Hz. Thus, a \gls{beat freq} of 0.5 Hz would be produced. This is exactly what is seen, as the resultant solution appears as a sine wave with a frequency of 0.5 Hz (2 second period). 


The same is seen when $\Delta t$ is close to but not quite double the frequency of the wave (called \gls{bandwidth} of the signal). The perceived signal has a changing amplitude with a superimposed sinusoid. This again, does not match the true signal. It is only when we sample at the \gls{nyquist rate} that a true approximation of the function is found. In general, we must sample our function at twice the highest frequency present to ensure a good approximation!

When we want to run a long term simulation where high frequency components are also present, the need to sample at double the highest frequency of the system is detrimental to the performance of the simulation. We call such systems that are difficult to numerically integrate \gls{stiff}.\endnote{This is not the only case in which our system dynamics will be stiff. Some differential equations also have dynamics that only rapidly change at critical values, which can contribute to stiffness as well.}

\Gls{stiff} systems make \gls{numerical int} challenging not only because of increased simulation time, but also because of aforementioned \gls{trunc error}. A simulation which requires many more timesteps will inheritly be less accurate due to roundoff error in the estimate. In a 6-\gls{dof} model such as ours, this is important if vibrational modeling is considered. As an example, slosh modeling and parachute modeling (which are not deeply discussed here, but may be important aspects of a rocket 6-\gls{dof}) are often based on mass-spring-damper systems.

It is best practice to try and decouple the simulation of high frequency vibrations from the flight dynamics of the system. Sometimes this may be unavoidable, such as aircraft with large amounts of wing flexure where aerodynamics and elastic deformation are heavily coupled. However, this decoupling should be attempted whenever possible. This problem is very complicated and is the subject of much ongoing research! \footnote{More information about such dynamics can be found in \cite{shyy_recent_2010}.}
\section{Other Integration Schemes}
While "ode45" works very well for most use cases with \gls{numerical int}, there are cases where other integration schemes should be considered. We will mainly discuss the implementations that exist in MATLAB. More information about such systems can be found at \cite{mathworks_choose_2024} and \cite{mathworks_summary_2024}.

For systems that have moderate stiffness, we can use "ode23" instead of the more typical "ode45" in MATLAB. Using a lower order integration leads to more course results, but it also allows very \gls{stiff} parts of the system to be computed faster.

On the converse side, for systems that require very high precision, such as orbits, algorithms of the 8th or 9th order are often used. In MATLAB, such algorithms are used in the "ode78" and "ode89" functions. Using a higher order, the \gls{trunc error} can be significantly reduced because fewer timesteps are needed when very high tolerances are required. However, in most scenarios, there algorithms are about 300-500\% slower than "ode45" while providing few accuracy benefits. Thus, these algorithms are mostly used for only very high precision use cases.

\subsection{Symplectic and Variational Integrators}
In addition to these \gls{numerical int} schemes, there are a variety of integration schemes which are based on energy conservation. These types of integrators are called \glspl{symplectic int}. These types of integrators use the energy methods we discussed in \autoref{sec:energy methods} to ascertain their solutions. A \gls{symplectic int} uses the Hamiltonian to formulate the integrator. A similar operation can be done to reduce the \gls{Lagrangian} to two first order equations, in a system known as a\textit{ variational} integrator.

\subsubsection{Use Case of Symplectic Integrators}
MATLAB does not have built-in functions for these integrators, but scripts to do so can be written or found online. The best use case for these algorithms is for systems with a Hamiltonian or Lagrangian description that need to be integrated over a long time span (such as a deep space orbit). Because these integrators conserve energy, they will better preserve the characteristics of these systems over long times as compared to RK integrators. In two body orbits, we prefer to use symplectic integrators because they preserve the exact solution up to numerical precision. Further discussion is in Volume III.

\subsubsection{Sympletic Euler Integrator}
One last note about symplectic schemes. When we're doing a basic Euler numerical integration of acceleration, we have typically done:
\begin{matlabscript}{Standard Euler Integrator}
v(i+1) = v(i) + a*dt;
p(i+1) = p(i) + v(i)*dt;
\end{matlabscript}

The \textit{sympletic Euler integrator} is slightly different:

\begin{matlabscript}{Symplectic Euler Integrator}
v(i+1) = v(i) + a*dt;
p(i+1) = p(i) + v(i+1)*dt;
\end{matlabscript}

As it turns out, this second option with the small change that we use $\texttt{v(i+1)}$ performs much better because it respects the symplectic structure.\endnote{If you know some classical mechanics, these equations preserve the volume of a Hamiltonian flow better, such that the volume stays approximantly constant in the phase space. A standard Euler integrator does not do this.}



\section{Numerical Integration Optimization}\label{sec: code optimization}

Often, we want to run many simulations with various parameters to gain a better understanding of our system. We call this method of running many simulations the \textit{Monte Carlo} method.\endnote{Interestingly, this is named after the Monte Carlo casino in Monaco. It was named so as a code name during the Manhattan project, where Stanisław Ulam's uncle often gambled.} Using the Monte Carlo method, we can ascertain information about the likelihood of certain events or approximate quantities that are hard to find analytically by running a large number of simulations and performing statistical analysis on the results. In this document, we will not dive into the methods of statistical analysis and the best ways to perform Monte Carlo simulations. This topic is very deep and is the focus of many peoples’ whole careers.

However, even with only rudimentary analysis, Monte Carlo can prove very useful. We will use the example of Monte Carlo to motivate the need for fast simulations. Often, we run upwards of 1000 simulations and fast computation is necessary to perform this large number of simulations. In this section, we will dive into the various methods to optimize MATLAB code and best practices for flight dynamics.
\subsection{Array and Function Optimizations}
MATLAB is generally quite computationally efficient at computing arrays and matrices. After all, MATLAB is ‘MATrix LABoratory’. However, we still need to be mindful of optimizations to our code.

It is best practice to only import what you need for a function or subroutine. For example, if you have wind data for all of the months out of the year, it is desirable to cull that down to the specific month before running the simulation.

Additionally, it is also beneficial for any imported data or long matrices to be passed into a function rather than called inside a function. This is especially important inside the \gls{numerical int} scheme! Running "readmatrix" inside of ode45 or whichever other \gls{numerical int} scheme you are using can slow down code upwards of 500\% depending on the length of the matrices being read.

Instead, it is best to pass these arrays into the function. Performing "readmatrix" can be slow because it must check if the values are numeric and organize them into tables. By passing only MATLAB arrays, computations are much faster.
\subsection{Flame Graphs for Optimization}
One important method of optimization is the MATLAB profiler. The primary capability of the profiler is the flame graph. An example of the flame graph is shown in \autoref{fig:flame1}

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Images/FlameGraph1.png}
    \caption{Flame Graph Showing Runtime of Individual Code Elements in MATLAB}
    \label{fig:flame1}
\end{figure}

The flame graph gives a visual representation of how long the code spends on each function. Functions in blue are those defined by the user. Those in grey are built in MATLAB functions.

Poorly optimized code will spend a long time running an excessive number of operations. In some cases, this is unavoidable. For example, ode45, which runs the RK4 integration scheme we discussed in \autoref{sec: rk4}, must run for every time step and is likely to comprise a large part of simulation time.

However, other operations, such as "atmosisa", should not comprise a large portion of simulation time. As seen in \autoref{fig:flame1}, calling the atmosisa function on every loop takes about 40\% of the total time spent in ‘RK4Integrator’ function. By instead creating a table of the atmosisa data before running the simulation, we can reduce the runtime quite drastically. Implementing this simple change, we can see the reduction in simulation time as shown in \autoref{fig:flame2}

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Images/FlameGraph2.png}
    \caption{Flame Graph After Optimizations}
    \label{fig:flame2}
\end{figure}

\section{Key Ideas}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \Gls{numerical int} schemes of higher order can better approximate systems. Generally, a higher order will require more up front computation but will be more accurate and will take fewer steps to achieve a given accuracy. 4th or 5th order schemes are typically a good balance for rocket dynamics.
    \item When numerically integrating functions with high frequencies or rapid changes, we need to be aware of the stiffness of the system and ensure that we sample at least the \gls{bandwidth} of the signal.
    \item Keep in mind what you need to calculate inside the integrator and what should be passed through. Calculations inside the integrator are very time expensive!
\end{enumerate}
\newpage
\section{Practice Problems}
\begin{exercise}[Building a 6-DoF: Part IV]
If you haven't already, update your 6-\gls{dof} simulation to use $\texttt{ode45}$ (MATLAB) or $\texttt{solve\_ivp}$ (Python) for better numerical integration. You can also try implementing a custom RK4 algorithm as discussed in the text.
\end{exercise}

\printendnotes[large]
\markboth{NOTES}{NOTES}

\chapter{An Example 6-DoF Simulation}\label{sec:6DoF}
In this section we will combine everything from the previous sections and explain how our 6-\gls{dof} rocket model works. We will follow a similar set up to \autoref{sec:Dzhan}  here, describing the elements of the model in detail and then attaching relevant code either in-line or in the Appendix.

Our definitions of our frames follow from those discussed in \autoref{sec:Frame of Reference}. For the earth-centered \gls{inertial frame}, we follow the conventions discussed in \autoref{fig:InertialFrame}. Recall that we follow the ENU convention for the inertial frame, where the $\hat{x}$ axis points east, the $\hat{y}$ axis points north, and the $\hat{z}$ axis finishes the right-handed coordinate system, pointing up.

For the \gls{body frame}, we follow the convention in \autoref{fig:BodyFrameRocket}. This has $\hat{X}$ pointing through the nose of the rocket and $\hat{Y}$and $\hat{Z}$ along the transverse plane. The directionality of $\hat{Y}$ and $\hat{Z}$ is not particularly important since the rocket is axially symmetric, so we arbitrarily select the $\hat{Y}$ axis to point through one of the fins and choose $\hat{Z}$ to complete the right-handed frame. For our \gls{Euler angles}, we use a 3-2-1 rotation sequence. We define the zero angles to be when the \gls{body frame} and the earth-centered \gls{inertial frame} are coincident.

\section{State Vector}
Our \gls{state vector} is very similar to that shown in \autoref{sec:Dzhan} section, but it bears repeating here because there are some important differences. Another thing to note is that some of the values of the \gls{state vector} are altered for Monte Carlo simulations. These possible changes, when applicable, are discussed.

We define our original position at the origin, giving us a position vector. It is also possible to define our initial position in another coordinate system, such as (lat, long, elevation). Generally it is good practice to set the origin to zero and add any offsets later to keep floating point errors in \gls{numerical int} small:
$$\vec{r}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    0\\0\\0
\end{bmatrix}$$
Depending on the launch location, we will initialize the last coordinate to the elevation at which we launch, where this vector is instead:
$$\vec{r}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    0\\0\\\text{elev.}
\end{bmatrix}$$
It is typical to initialize the simulation with zero velocity. In some instances, we may add an initial velocity if we are modeling a sounding rocket launched with an initial speed or similar for validation of our model:
$$\vec{v}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    0\\0\\0
\end{bmatrix}$$
We also need the initial orientation. Here, we use \gls{Euler angles} for initialization. We often choose to launch at a slight angle to simulate adverse affects that may push the launch vehicle slightly off-course during the initial launch. To point the vehicle mostly upward, we use a rotation along the $\theta$ axis:
\begin{equation}\label{eq: euler angles 6DoF}
    \begin{bmatrix}
    \psi\\\theta\\\phi
\end{bmatrix} =
\begin{bmatrix}
    0\\-\pi/2\\0
\end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}

To simulate a small randomization in this angle, we can specify that $\psi$ takes on a random value from $0$ to $2\pi$. In MATLAB:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
Psi = 2*pi*rand(1);
\end{matlabscript}
We want this random distribution to be uniform over all angles, hence the use of $\texttt{rand}$. For the $\theta$ angle, we prefer a normal distribution centered around $-\frac{\pi}{2}$, which looks like:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
-pi/2 + 0.0175*randn(1)
\end{matlabscript}
Here,the constant $0.0175$ describes $1\degree$ expressed in radians. To convert this to a quaternion, we use the command "eul2quat" with a specification of "ZYX" for the frame and chosen 3-2-1 rotation sequence. The specification is important now because we need to explicitly define our rotation sequence when two or more rotations are present. In the case of \eqref{eq: euler angles 6DoF}, this gives an initial \gls{quaternion} vector of :
$$\mathbf{q}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    0.7071\\0\\-0.7071\\0
\end{bmatrix}$$
Lastly, we need to describe the initial angular velocity. We assume for most simulations that there is no initial angular velocity. For some cases, such as the simulation of \gls{rail whip}, we may choose to put an angular velocity on the rocket.
$$\vec{\omega}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
0\\0\\0
\end{bmatrix} rad/s$$
Putting all of the together, we arrive at our \gls{state vector}:
$$\vec{X}_0=\begin{bmatrix}
    \vec{r}_0\\\vec{v}_0\\\vec{\omega}_0\\\vec{q}_0
\end{bmatrix}$$
\section{Forces and Moments in the 6-DoF}
The forces and moments on our rocket are what separate this case from the more simple case of the \gls{dzhan} in \autoref{sec:Dzhan}. We have discussed most of these forces in \autoref{fig:3 DoF Forces}, and in \autoref{sec: aerodynamics}, but the exact implementation into the 6-DoF model bears repeating, specifically relating to frame conversion.
\subsection{Forces}\label{6DoF Forces}
We define 4 main forces that act on the vehicle, those being the 4 forces described in \autoref{sec: 3DoF Case}. We will start with the most simple, which is gravity.
\subsubsection{Gravity}
Because we define the $\hat{z}$ direction as the zenith direction in the inertial earth frame, we define gravity as:
\begin{equation}
   F_g= \begin{bmatrix}
    0\\0\\-mg
\end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
For gravity, we note that because it acts through the center of mass of the rocket, we do not have a resultant moment to worry about.\endnote{There is a slight difference between the center of mass and the center of gravity, which may produce a very small moment. In this instance, it is so small that we do not consider it.  For orbiting spacecraft, this might become significant!}
\subsubsection{Thrust}
Thrust is also fairly simple to model. If we consider the thrust to be coincident with the longitudinal axis, the thrust is given by \eqref{eq:Ft}. For the magnitude, we consider a slightly more complex model than before. Because the rocket is changing altitude, the thrust of the engine will change as the pressure changes. The thrust of the engine, accounting for this pressure change, is given by:
\begin{equation}
    F_T=\dot{m}V_e+\left(P_{e}-P_{atm}\right)A_e
\end{equation}
Where $\dot{m}$ is the mass flow rate of the engine, $V_e$ is the exit velocity of the engine, $P_e$ is the exit pressure of the engine, $P_{atm}$ is the atmospheric pressure, and $A_e$ is the area of the nozzle exit. For a given propulsion system, these parameters are usually specified or can be easily derived, although we will not do so here. \footnote{See \cite{dasilva_chemical_2023} or the propulsion team for more details.}

Here, we introduce the use of the rotation matrix to convert between different frames. MATLAB allows for the creation of a rotation matrix with the input of a \gls{quaternion} using the "quat2dcm" function. Using this, we can perform frame conversion to and from the body and earth frame. We show this below.

\begin{matlabscript}{}
function [vecOut] = RotationMatrix(vecIn, quatIn, bodyOrEarth)
    
    % create the rotation matrix
    R = quat2dcm(rotIn);
    
    %perform transformation:
    if bodyOrEarth == 0
        vecOut = R * vecIn;
    
    elseif bodyOrEarth == 1
        vecOut = R.' * vecIn;
    else
        fprintf("Input for body or earth frame is invalid\n");
    end
end
\end{matlabscript}

Using this code, we can convert the thrust from the \gls{body frame} into the earth frame through the function call:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
RotationMatrix(thrustForceBody, quat, 1)
\end{matlabscript}

The input of 1 in this function means that the vector is transformed from the \gls{body frame} into the earth frame. In our case, this means that we multiply by the transpose of the rotation matrix in the MATLAB notation convention.
\subsubsection{Lift and Drag}
Our other two forces follow in a similar manner. The quantities of lift and drag are heavily discussed in \autoref{sec: aerodynamics}, so we will keep them succinct here. The directions of lift and drag in the earth frame are described by \eqref{eq:Lift} and \eqref{eq:drag}. To convert these forces into the \gls{body frame}, we use the function call:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
RotationMatrix(force, quat, 0)
\end{matlabscript}
The specification of "0" in the function converts a force in the earth frame into one in the \gls{body frame}. We need the \gls{body frame} force for computing the moments in the \gls{body frame}, as discussed in \autoref{sec:rotations in the body frame}.
\subsubsection{Parachute Forces}

The other force that sometimes acts on our rocket is the drag force imparted by the parachute(s). A parachute acts as another body, constrained to the vehicle body which it interacts with. To create this multibody simulation, all parachute drag forces are calculated in a separate function "calculateParachuteForce.m". The form of the drag equation for the parachute is identical to the aerodynamic drag: $\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\infty} V_{\infty}^2SC_D$. The only difference between the two is the reference area and drag coefficients. For the latter, we use an experimentally determined value. For the former, however, we have to estimate the area of the fully inflated parachute. Luckily, it has been shown that the equation for the circle with a deform value scale factor of approximately 0.70 is adequate to model the reference area of the parachute. 

In addition, it is necessary to consider the time delay of inflation, as the parachute will inflate gradually as it is deployed. We consider an exponential function to model the deployment. This means the equation for the area in our drag calculation is the following: $S=0.7\pi (\frac{1}{2}d)^2(1-e^{-.5t_{\text{Drogue}}})$. After the drag calculation, it is applied to the opposite of the freestream velocity. This force is only active when the vertical velocity is negative, so this is controlled by an "if/else" structure as: "if vel(1) < 0". In addition, our vehicle has both a drogue and main chute. As such, another if/else structure controls the deployment of the main chute at a specified altitude. The parachute dynamics are far more complicated than this, but currently we do not model this added complexity. 

There are no other forces that act in our 6DoF model. Internal forces and vibrations are not modeled, as we assume a completely rigid vehicle.
\subsection{Moments}
The modeling of moments in the 6-\gls{dof} is fairly straightforward. In the previous \autoref{6DoF Forces}, we converted each of our forces into the \gls{body frame}. Here, we will use these forces in the \gls{body frame} to describe the moments acting on our vehicle. 

The moment arm of the aerodynamic forces is equivalent to the distance from the center of mass to the center of pressure. 
\begin{equation}
    M^c=\vec{r}_{cm}-\vec{r}_{cp}
\end{equation}
Assuming that both lie along the longitudinal axis, we can express this moment as:
\begin{equation}
    M^c=\hat{X}(x_{cm}-x_{cp})
\end{equation}
Since gravity acts through the center of mass, the moment arm has length of 0 and no resultant moment exists. For thrust, since it is applied through the longitudinal axis, the $\vec{r}\times \vec{F}_T$ term is zero because $\vec{r}$ and $\vec{F}_T$ are parallel.

Thus, the only moments that are present are from the aerodynamic forces. The moment generated by the aerodynamic forces is:
\begin{equation}
    M^c=\hat{X}(x_{cm}-x_{cp})\times \vec{R}
\end{equation}
Where $\vec{R}$ is the resultant aerodynamic force (the combination of both lift and drag).

\subsection{Attitude Dynamics}
These moments are used to find angular acceleration, $\vec{\alpha}$ using the Euler \gls{rigid body} equations described in \eqref{eq: moment eq 2}. Lastly, the \gls{quaternion} rates are described in the same manner as in \autoref{sec:Dzhan}.
\begin{equation}
    \dot{\mathbf{q}}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}
        0&-\omega_x&-\omega_y&-\omega_z\\
        \omega_x&0&\omega_z&-\omega_y\\
        \omega_y&-\omega_z&0&\omega_x\\
        \omega_z&\omega_y&-\omega_x&0
    \end{bmatrix}\mathbf{q}
\end{equation}
So, taking all of these together, we have the derivative of our \gls{state vector} needed for \gls{numerical int}.
\begin{equation}
    \dot{\textbf{X}}=\begin{bmatrix}
        \vec{v}\\\vec{a}\\\vec{\alpha}\\\dot{\textbf{q}}
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
\subsection{6-DoF Code}
We take this integrated \gls{state vector} along with the initial conditions and integrate them using "ode45". The most important script in this process is the "RK4Integrator.m" MATLAB script. Because this MATLAB script is quite long, we have chosen not to include it in the appendix. Instead, a link to the GitHub is given where the scripts can be found. The full code can be found at \cite{reynolds_hudsonreynoldsflight-dynamics-bible_nodate} or in Appendix \ref{Appendix:code}.

\section{Validation of 6-DoF}
The validation of our 6-\gls{dof} model is achieved through modeling other rocket systems that have flown to check the validity of our model and modeling other physical phenomena. Validation can range from very simple to far more complex depending on the system that is being validated. For us, validation falls under two main categories:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Sub-component validation: validation of parts of the 6-\gls{dof} by modeling certain types of systems or doing hand calculations to verify correct behavior.
    \item Full model validation: validation of the whole 6-\gls{dof} by modeling a different rocket system and comparing 6-\gls{dof} outputs to real outputs.
\end{enumerate}

Sub-component validation may look like the Dzhanibekov modeling in \autoref{sec:Dzhan}, where we test the attitude dynamics. Full model validation is usually more complex. One such example is our validation of our model against the V-2 rocket launch on March 7, 1949. This comparison is shown in \autoref{fig:V2 sim}

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=.75\linewidth]{Images/V2 Combined.png}
    \caption{Comparison of Real V-2 Rocket Launch Data against 6-DoF Modeling}
    \label{fig:V2 sim}
\end{figure}
