\printendnotes[large]

\chapter{Simulation Architecture}\label{sec:MATLAB Practices}
In this final chapter, we discuss the techniques and practices for implementing a 6-DoF simulation in MATLAB and how we may structure such a simulation. We will also discuss more general practices for large MATLAB code bases and methods to keep these organized. As of Fall 2025, these practices have been implemented in our 6-DoF model, allowing for a full generalization of our rocket creation. A large portion of the content in this chapter comes from what we have seen as practices adopted in industry for systems much more complex and larger than our own.

As your system grows in complexity, following these practices becomes increasingly important for organization. If the reader is working on their own large code base or contribute to our code base, it is a good idea to follow these practices as well to stay organized. In this chapter, we will focus on MATLAB implementation, but similar things may be done in Python or other object-oriented languages.

\section{Object Oriented Programming}

\color{black}

Up until now, we have mainly been describing a procedural approach to programming. The procedural approach uses multiple interacting functions to run the code. Each function takes in inputs and outputs. These functions work together to run the code and built the final output. Each function is distinct from one another, and operate with only the information that we choose to pass into the function. In most introductory coding classes, this is the type of programming we learn.

This is a very good method for writing short segments of code, but for larger databases that need to have greater code reusability, there are better approaches. One such approach that is particularly powerful is \gls{oop}.\endnote{There exists some debate whether \gls{oop} is a superior method of coding as compared to functional programming. Of course, the true answer is nuanced and depends on the application. Object-Oriented programming, when done poorly, can make code quite unreadable. However, proper implementation of \gls{oop} can be extremely useful. See \cite{brian_will_object-oriented_2016}  for more information on this debate.} 

In \gls{oop}, the basis of our code is \textit{objects}. These objects hold information of all types, whether that be numeric, string, or time data. The most important aspect of \gls{oop} is that it allows us to make user interaction with a large code base simpler, and also allows us to reuse large parts of the code.

If it has not already become apparent, the 6-\gls{dof} model described so far is fairly simple in the grand scheme of things. We are modeling a single rocket and most of the subsystems are simplified. What happens if we want to model a huge rocket system with different systems, sensors, measurements, errors, and so on? In this case, we need a better scheme for organization that makes the process simpler for the end user. \Gls{oop} is one method to achieve such organization that we use for our model.

\Gls{oop}, like any skill in coding, is best understood with practice. As a starting point, we highly recommend \cite{tearle_object-oriented_nodate}, which gives a guided introduction to \gls{oop} in MATLAB. It may take several repetitions and readings to become familiar with the terminology of \gls{oop}, so try not to become discouraged by the huge influx of new concepts here!

\subsection{Objects, Properties, and Classes}

\subsubsection{Objects}

The basis of the \gls{oop} approach is, as the name suggests, objects. Objects hold much more information than standard variables. Typically, a variable contains only one piece of information, such as a string, an integer, or a floating point number. These variables have a value / values, and a type. Aside from this, they contain no other information. An object, on the other hand, can contain as much information as we want it to! 

An object is a reusable part of code that has certain attributes and functions associated within it. An object may be a "rocket", and it has features such as "propellant type", "mass", and "thrust", to name a few. The rocket might also have some functions associated with it, such as "SimulateFlight", which would simulate the rocket flight based on all the given inputs.

The addition of these new features make objects particularly powerful for complex systems. Now, we can assign real world attributes to an object and handle it in code much more simply. Let's consider a scenario in the functional programming case.

Suppose we have a ball and we want to simulate it bouncing. Some parameters we might want to consider are the radius, the coefficient of restitution, and the color (so it looks nice). In functional programming, we might have something like:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
% attributes of a ball
radius = 0.1; %[m]
restitutionCoeff = 0.8;
color = [0,0,1];
\end{matlabscript}

If we only simulate one ball, this is of course no hassle. There is no reason to complicate things more than this. However, if we want to simulate multiple balls and track each parameter separately, we can see how this would quickly get out of hand. A much better approach would have these properties be intrinsic to each ball. When we define a ball, these properties must already exist. Without going into specifics of how we set up such a system, the object oriented approach might look like:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
% create a ball with the properties from before
ball = initializeBall(0.1, 0.8, [0,0,1]);
\end{matlabscript}

We can then access these properties, say $\texttt{ball.radius}$, at whatever time. The nice thing with this approach is that we don't need to keep track of this extra $\texttt{radius}$ variable, it's part of the $\texttt{ball}$ object already. Of course, we can't type this line of code into MATLAB just yet -- we haven't created a function $\texttt{initializeBall}$ yet. To accomplish this, we will now discuss how we can programmatically create these objects and construct whichever features we desire. To do this, we use the \gls{oop} feature called classes.

\subsubsection{Classes}

Objects are inherently related to what are called \textit{classes}. A class defines the attributes and the functions that are associated with an object. The class is essentially a blueprint for what an object may look like. The attributes associated with an object are called \textit{properties} and the associated functions are called \textit{methods}. Properties and methods are exactly analogous to the variables and functions seen elsewhere, but they are applicable only to objects of that class. In MATLAB, these classes can be created by clicking the dropdown when creating a new file and selecting `Class'. The default class looks like:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
classdef untitled
    %UNTITLED Summary of this class goes here
    %   Detailed explanation goes here

    properties
        Property1
    end

    methods
        function obj = untitled(inputArg1,inputArg2)
            %UNTITLED Construct an instance of this class
            %   Detailed explanation goes here
            obj.Property1 = inputArg1 + inputArg2;
        end

        function outputArg = method1(obj,inputArg)
            %METHOD1 Summary of this method goes here
            %   Detailed explanation goes here
            outputArg = obj.Property1 + inputArg;
        end
    end
end
\end{matlabscript}

A class often has a \textit{constructor} method, which constructs an object of that class. A constructor method is often useful to instantiate an object of a given class. In MATLAB, a constructor method takes the same name as the class itself. The class constructor and some of the subsequent ideas are best demonstrated with some example code:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
    properties
        Name (1,1) string
        SamplingRate (1,1) double
        Variance double
    end

    methods
        function out = Sensor(name,samplingRate,variance)
            arguments
                name (1,1) string
                samplingRate (1,1) double   
                variance = 0;
            end
            out.Name = name;
            out.SamplingRate = samplingRate;
            out.Variance = variance;
        end
    end
\end{matlabscript}


In this code, the function "Sensor" is a class constructor method of the file "Sensor.m". The class constructor takes user input for parameters such as a "name", and "samplingRate" for the sensor, which are assigned within the function. These are then assigned to the properties of the object of the same name. If we were to make the function call `$\texttt{Sensor(`Sensy', 0.01, 0.2)}$', we would get a $\texttt{Sensor}$ object with the properties defined in the function.

Another useful property of classes which is demonstrated  here is input validation. Input validation allows us to ensure that the input into a class or one of its methods is of a certain type. In this example, the "name" can only be a "string" of size 1 (scalar). Input validation allows us to build robust functions that only allow proper data to be passed through.

You may notice that this class constructor is a "method". These "methods"  are functions that are associated with that specific type of object. In this example, the "Sensor" function can only be performed on an object of the class "Sensor". Most of the programming involved with classes will pertain to these methods themselves.

Before we dive too deep into these methods, we should describe the different types of classes that exist in MATLAB and where we might want to use them:

\subsubsection{Default Classes}
A default class, like the ones shown above, require that we create an object before we can perform operations on it. These classes list the attributes associated with the object in their properties and have methods for the object.

\subsubsection{Abstract Classes}
Sometimes, we want to create classes that are broad and describe multiple different things. In our case, we might have something like a "RocketComponent", where every single rocket component should have a name, position, mass, and material. However, this "RocketComponent" doesn't really exist on its own. Instead, we need to define more features for an individual component. A "PropulsionSystem", for example, will have all the attributes of the "RocketComponent" but should also include the thrust and burn time of the system!

For this reason, we create Abstract classes like the "RocketComponent" which do nothing by themselves, but instead describe abstract features that apply to a multitude of different subclasses. An instance of an abstract class cannot be created. Normally, we say that our other classes should \textit{inherit} the properties of the abstract class. In MATLAB, we can do this with the following at the top of the class definition:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
classdef PropulsionSystem < RocketComponent
\end{matlabscript}

This command creates the "PropulsionSystem" with all the properties of the "RocketComponent" abstract class inherited. We can inherit from any other class that we would like, even MATLAB built in functions.

\subsubsection{Constant Classes}
There are some classes that should not change. Typically, we use these classes to hold constant values or other immutable variables in our program. For this case, we use a constant class, which stores these values and allows them to be accessed globally across our program.

Having discussed the variety of classes we want to create, we can now discuss the methods which operate upon our objects.

\subsection{Methods}
Methods are the backbone of \gls{oop}, and allow us to manipulate our objects and change their attributes in useful ways. In MATLAB, methods are somewhat distinct from functions and have some special features that we can use. 

Most methods require that the first input to the method is an object of the class. This is required because methods are designed to only work on objects of that class. In MATLAB, there are two ways which a method of an object can be called. The first way is to input the object as the first input of the method:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
Plotter(app)
\end{matlabscript}
The second way which this can be done is with the `dot indexing notation' which looks like:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
app.Plotter
\end{matlabscript}

Both of these are used interchangeably when calling a method, but when you write a method, you \textit{must} use the first notation:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
out = Plotter(app, input1, input2, etc.)
\end{matlabscript}

Aside from the requirement of an object as the first input, methods are written in the same way that standard functions are written in MATLAB. Further information about methods can be found in the MATLAB onramp.

\subsubsection{Static Methods}
Sometimes, we do not want to have to call a class constructor method or create an object of a certain class to employ a method. In this case, we can use a \textit{static method}. A static method allows us to call a method without the object as an input.

A static method is associated with a class, but does not require a specific instance of that class to be run. Static methods are written by appending "Methods (static)" in the class:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
classdef MyClass
   methods(Static)
       function p = pi(tol)
           [n d] = rat(pi,tol);
           p = n/d;
       end
   end
end
\end{matlabscript}

\subsection{Overloading and Precedence}
One special feature of \gls{oop} is that it allows us to change the behavior of the default MATLAB functions when applied on certain objects. For example, overloading allows the user to change the functionality of the "plot" or "disp" functions when they are acting on a user defined object.

MATLAB gives the highest precedence to methods that are defined in classes, so a locally defined "plot" method will take priority over the built-in MATLAB "plot" when it is defined for that object.

\begin{matlabscript}{}
   methods
      function plot(obj)
         y = obj.Data;
         plot(y,'EdgeColor','r');
         title('Data Plot')
      end
   end
\end{matlabscript}

When calling plot for an object of this class, the default behavior would be replaced by the custom behavior defined above.

\subsection{Standards for Classes}
Class structure in MATLAB is very powerful. If it is used inappropriately, though, it can make coding much more difficult. When you are building a class within MATLAB, you should consider the following:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Does this functionality need a new class? Should it be a subclass of something else or a new method of an existing class?
    \item What does the additional layer of abstraction help with? Is it simplifying the code structure or convoluting it?
\end{itemize}

There are some other considerations, but these questions should start to guide you on your implementation. Understanding these considerations and building a good class structure is oftentimes more difficult than writing the code! It will take practice to gain an understanding for the specific problem and how to build the class structure around that.

Classes in MATLAB carry lots of information. As a result, documenting them is quite critical for maintaining and improving their functionality. It is suggested that you comment each method of the class, as well as the top level class, especially when the user interacts directly with that class.

\section{Continuous Integration and Testing}
Continuous development is a principle that software development should occur in small pieces that are developed rapidly, rather than the more conventional approach of doing large overhauls of a code base. We find that the continuous integration approach is more useful for us, as it allows us greater flexibility in how we code and allows for features to be developed in parallel to each other.

In practice, this style of implementation can be difficult because it can be hard to check that each piece of code that is added to the database is properly written and constructed. To tackle this, \gls{ci} development employs automatic checks for the code to ensure quality standards and that the code being pushed has no errors. 

This can be accomplished through the use of a program such as GitHub Actions, which checks code each time it is pushed to a new branch. We have set up a continuous integration platform for our 6-\gls{dof} already, but more information about setting up such a system can be found at \cite{mathworks_continuous_2025}.

It should be noted that GitHub Actions has a few limitations when using the built in Linux runner on GitHub. Firstly, the repository on which the information is stored must be public. Second, the runner only works for base MATLAB and not for any of the toolboxes such as Aerospace toolbox which we use quite frequently. For this reason, we use a self-hosted runner which allows us to avoid both of these issues.

\subsection{Unit Testing}
Unit testing works in combination with continuous integration practices and the object-oriented approach to provide the capability to test the functionality of MATLAB code. Unit testing works in collaboration with other validation methods, such as the full model validation that we discussed previously. Unit testing allows us to test individual pieces of code and ensure that it gives us desired outputs. Unit testing is built into MATLAB and can be automatically integrated with continuous integration pipelines. More information can be found at \cite{noauthor_unit_nodate, noauthor_ways_nodate}.


\section{Data Visualization}
Here, we will briefly discuss data visualization and best practices for MATLAB. No matter how good your simulation is, you must be able to appropriately share data in a way that is not only easily conveyed to a non-expert audience, but also visually interesting and descriptive.

At the most basic level, your MATLAB plots should have proper formatting. The default MATLAB plot formatting isn't up to snuff with the standards of scientific publications or presentation of your work professionally. Luckily, MATLAB has many tools to improve the presentation of figures.

MATLAB has the ability for plots to utilize LaTeX formatting for figures. We highly recommend to enable this option for all plots as it allows for much better formatting on figures. This can be done with the following commands:

\begin{matlabscript}{}
% set plot interpreter to LaTeX
set(groot, 'defaultAxesTickLabelInterpreter','latex');
set(groot, 'defaultLegendInterpreter','latex');
set(groot, 'defaultTextInterpreter', 'latex')
\end{matlabscript}

\subsection{Conventions for Plots}
There are several conventions for plots which are generally followed in engineering practices. These will change depending on where your figures are to be published, but for our purposes, we use the following conventions:
\begin{itemize}
    \item All plots should have clear axis labels. Units are typically included in brackets (e.g. [$m$] as an example of a length in meters). Units should be SI base units wherever possible.
    \item Be knowledgeable of the use of color. Overuse of color may distract the viewer from the information being presented. Additionally, if your content is to be presented to a large audience, be mindful of those with color vision deficiencies. If your content may be printed, plots should be easily readable in black and white. Using different markers and line types is a good strategy.
    \item Always include a legend when plotting multiple data on a single graph. 
    \item When the magnitudes of data are vastly different, a logarithmic axis or two y-axes are best practice.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Changing Default MATLAB Plotting}
If you want to have these changes to figures universally applied in MATLAB, you can create a file in MATLAB called $\texttt{startup.m}$ that is run when MATLAB is opened. This file must exist in the user path for MATLAB. To find the user path for MATLAB, the command "userpath" can be typed into the command line. This will output something like:
\\
"C:\Users\YourName\OneDrive\Documents\MATLAB"

The code below is the default plotting used in this document, which should be a good baseline for professional plots.

\begin{matlabscript}{}
% set linewidth
set(groot,'defaultLineLineWidth',1.5)

% set the interpreter to latex
set(groot, 'defaultAxesTickLabelInterpreter','latex'); 
set(groot, 'defaultLegendInterpreter','latex');
set(groot, 'defaultTextInterpreter', 'latex');

%change font sizes
set(groot, 'defaultLegendFontSize', 12)
set(groot, 'defaultTextFontSize', 12)
set(groot, 'defaultAxesFontSize', 10)

% grid on and box off
set(groot, 'defaultLegendBox', 'off');
set(groot,'defaultAxesXGrid','on');
set(groot,'defaultAxesYGrid','on');
\end{matlabscript}


And with that, we have completed the first Volume of the Flight Dynamics Bible. Additional information and code can be found in the Appendix. If you have any questions, concerns, or suggestions, feel free to reach out to Hudson Reynolds or Preston Wright on Slack or via email at Hudsonj.reynolds@gmail.com and preston.wright0330@gmail.com.

\addappheadtotoc

\renewcommand{\chaptername}{Appendix}

\chapter{Answers to Selected Problems}

\section{Chapter 2 Solutions}
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\autoref{exer:bke_practice}}{BKE Practice}}


We start with the position vector, which we can most easily describe in the $b$-frame:
$$\vec{r}^{\ OP}=x\hat{b_1}$$
We recognize that $\omega=\omega \hat{e}_3=\omega\hat{b}_3$. We can simply apply the \gls{bke} to solve for the inertial velocity:
\begin{gather}
    ^e\frac{d}{dt}\vec{r}^{\ OP} = ^b\frac{d}{dt}\vec{r}^{\ OP}+^e\vec{\omega}^b\times \vec{r}^{\ OP}\\\\
    ^e\vec{v}^P=^b\frac{d}{dt}\left(x\hat{b}_1\right)+\omega \hat{b}_3\times x\hat{b}_1\\\\
    \color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}
    ^e\vec{v}^P=\dot{x}\hat{b}_1+\omega x\hat{b}_2}
\end{gather}
Note the two terms, the $\dot{x}$ term, and the $\omega x$ term, which correspond to the linear velocity outwards and the angular component of the velocity, respectively. Although this velocity is expressed in terms fo the $b$-frame, it is inertial. A coordinate transformation may be used if it desired to use $e$-frame coordinates. We repeat the previous step to find the inertial acceleration.

\begin{gather}
    ^e\vec{a}^P=^b\frac{d}{dt}\left(^e\vec{v}^P\right)+\omega \hat{b}_3\times ^e\vec{v}^P\\\\
    ^e\vec{a}^P=^b\frac{d}{dt}\left(\dot{x}\hat{b}_1+\omega x\hat{b}_2\right)+\omega \hat{b}_3\times \left(\dot{x}\hat{b}_1+\omega x\hat{b}_2\right)\\\\
    ^e\vec{a}^P=\ddot{x}\hat{b}_1+\dot{\omega}x\hat{b}_2+\omega\dot{x}\hat{b}_2+\omega\dot{x}\hat{b}_2-\omega^2x\hat{b}_1
\end{gather}
We note at this point that $\ddot{x}$ is zero, since the velocity is constant, and $\dot{\omega}$ is also zero since the angular velocity is constant. This gives us:
$$\color{boxBlue}\boxed{\color{black}^e\vec{a}^P=2\omega\dot{x}\hat{b}_2-\omega^2x\hat{b}_1}$$
We see that there is a force in the $\hat{b}_2$-direction due to the bugs movement outwards. This is called the \textit{Coriolis} force. We have an additional force pushing the bug towards the center, a \textit{centripetal} force.

Finally, we can plug in numbers to compute the force. We equate the inertial acceleration to the force via Newton's 2nd law:
$$\sum F=m^e\vec{a}^P$$
\begin{gather}
\vec{F}=0.001\text{ kg}\left(2\cdot100\text{ rad/s}\cdot{0.01\text{ m/s }}\hat{b}_2-(100\text{ rad/s})^2\cdot0.03\text{ m }\hat{b}_1\right)\\\\
\vec{F}=0.002\text{ N }\hat{b}_2-0.3 \text{ N }\hat{b}_3 
\end{gather}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\autoref{exer:bke_code}}{BKE In Code}}

\subsubsection{A}
We can derive the \gls{eom} of the system using either the \gls{Lagrangian} or Newtonian methods. The Lagrangian method is detailed in \autoref{sec:energy methods}, so it will not be covered here. The \gls{eom} of the system is derived by considering the force balance of the system:
    $$\sum \vec{F}=m\vec{a}$$
    In this problem, motion is constrained to only the $\hat{x}$-direction. We also know the spring force is of the form:
    $$F_S=-k(L-L_0)\hat{x}$$
    We can substitute $F_S$ as the force, and $m\vec{a}$ remains on the RHS of the equation:
    $$ma_x=-k(L-L_0)$$
    If we choose $L_0$ to be the origin of the $x$-coordinate, we can rewrite the equation quite simply as:
    $$m\ddot{x}+k{x}=0$$
    Finally, we typically divide each term by the mass:
    $$\ddot{x}+\frac{k}{m}{x}=0$$Plugging in the given values of $m$ and $k$ into the equation will give us the final equation:
    $$\ddot{x}+4{x}=0$$

    \subsubsection{B}
    The analytical solution of this equation is quite easy to find. There are a few methods we can take, but the easiest is to create a characteristic equation (assuming the solution to take form $x=e^{rt}$). This allows us to turn the differential equation into an algebraic equation.\footnote{A more robust method, known as the Laplace transform, will be explored in Volume III.}
    $$r^2+4=0$$
    We can solve this equation in terms of $r$:
    $$r=\pm 2i$$
    Remembering that we chose $x=e^{rt}$ as the assumed solution, we have:
    $$x(t)=C_1e^{2it}+C_2e^{-2it}$$
    Using the Euler identity, we may rewrite this in terms of trigonometric functions (although you are free to input this into MATLAB as is).
    $$x(t)=A\cos{2t}+B\sin{2t}$$
    Finally, we need initial conditions. The initial position, $x(0)=0$, and for velocity, we may use the impulse-momentum theorem:
    $$J=m\Delta v$$
As a result, the initial velocity must be $10 \ \mathrm{m/s}$ in the $-\hat{x}$-direction, or $\dot{x}(0)=-10$.

We can use these two initial conditions to find the particular solution of the differential equation:
$$x(t)=-5\sin(2t)$$
This may be plotted as any function in MATLAB would be, which is shown in part C
\subsubsection{C \& D}
\begin{matlabscript}{Problem 2-1 Script}
% PSP FLIGHT DYNAMICS:
% Title: Problem 2-1 Script
% Author: Hudson Reynolds
% Last Modified:9-6-2025 
% 
% Description: This script runs a mass spring damper 
% simulation. The analytical solution is compared against
% the numerical integration.
clear;clc;close all

% Write the analytical solution:
t = linspace(0,10,300);
xAnal = -5*sin(2*t);

% Run the ODE45 integration for the no damper scenario

% pos, vel
x0 = [0;-10];
[t,xNum] = ode45(@(t,x)integrator(t,x), t, x0);

% run the ODE45 for the damper situation:

% pos, vel
x0 = [0;-10];
[t,xNumDamp] = ode45(@(t,x)integrator2(t,x), t, x0);

% plot:
figure
sgtitle('Mass Spring Damper Simulation');
subplot(2,1,1)
plot(t,xAnal)
hold on;
plot(t, xNum(:,1), '--');
title('Undamped MSD')
xlabel('Time (s)');
ylabel('Position (m)');
legend('Analytical Solution', 'Numerical Solution', 'Location','best');
hold off;
yLims = ylim;


subplot(2,1,2)
plot(t,xNumDamp(:,1))
ylim(yLims)
title('Damped MSD')
xlabel('Time (s)');
ylabel('Position (m)');


function out = integrator(t,x)
vel = x(2);
accel = -4*x(1);

out = [vel;accel];
end

function out = integrator2(t,x)
vel = x(2);
accel = -4*x(1) - x(2);

out = [vel;accel];
end
\end{matlabscript}

\section{Chapter 3 Solutions}


\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\autoref{ex:lagrangiancode}}{Lagrangian In Code}}

We choose to use MATLAB to solve this particular problem. We can construct a function with symbolic variables.
\begin{matlabscript}{Lagrangian Function}
function eom = createEOM(L, q)
% createEOM  Generate Euler–Lagrange equations
%
% L: symbolic Lagrangian
% q: vector of generalized coordinates (symbolic functions of t)

% create a time variable
syms t

% for each generalized coordinate, create an EOM

for idx = 1:length(q)

    % Generalized velocity
    qd = diff(q{idx}, t);

    % Euler–Lagrange equations
    eom(idx) = diff(diff(L, qd), t) - diff(L, q{idx}) == 0;

end

% transpose to get cleaner outputs
eom = eom.';
end
\end{matlabscript}

We can test this on any of our functions. Notice that functions of time are represented as $\texttt{x(t)}$ such that we can define the derivative in MATLAB. Also note the use of curly brackets for cell array operations here. This is done as symbolic variables of time will evaluate as $x(1)=x(t)\vert_{t=1}$. We use cell array indexing to circumvent this.
\begin{matlabscript}{}
% mass spring
syms x(t) k

L = 1/2*diff(x,t)^2 - 1/2*k*x^2

q = {x};

eom = createEOM(L, q)
\end{matlabscript}


We can even do a system with multiple generalized coordinates, such as the system in \autoref{ex:inverted_pend}:
\begin{matlabscript}{}
% inverted pendulum
syms M m x(t) I theta(t) l g

L = 1/2*((M+m)*diff(x)^2+I*diff(theta)^2) + m*l*cos(theta)*diff(theta)*diff(x) - m*g*l*cos(theta)

q = {x, theta}

eom = createEOM(L,q)
\end{matlabscript}

When put into a MATLAB livescript, this will output the result with LaTeX formatting:
$$\begin{array}{l}
\left(\begin{array}{c}
{\left(M+m\right)}\,\sigma_1 -l\,m\,\sin \left(\theta \left(t\right)\right)\,{{\left(\frac{\partial }{\partial t}\;\theta \left(t\right)\right)}}^2 +l\,m\,\cos \left(\theta \left(t\right)\right)\,\sigma_2 =0\\
\textrm{I}\,\sigma_2 +l\,m\,\cos \left(\theta \left(t\right)\right)\,\sigma_1 -g\,l\,m\,\sin \left(\theta \left(t\right)\right)=0
\end{array}\right)\\
\mathrm{}\\
\textrm{where}\\
\;\;\sigma_1 =\frac{\partial^2 }{\partial t^2 }\;x\left(t\right)\\
\mathrm{}\\
\;\;\sigma_2 =\frac{\partial^2 }{\partial t^2 }\;\theta \left(t\right)
\end{array}$$

\subsection*{3-2}
Our setup is nearly identical, but in this case we have a generalized force. We still consider the $\psi$ Euler angle.
$$\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\psi}}-\frac{\partial L}{\partial \psi}=Q$$
We can follow the same approach as \autoref{sec: euler rot eq}
 to obtain the generalized force $Q=\vec{M}$. We can then see that:
 \begin{gather}
\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{\psi}}-\frac{\partial L}{\partial \psi}=\vec{M}
\end{gather}
We consider the $\hat{Z}$-direction to obtain just one of the components:
$$I_3\dot{\omega}_3-(I_1-I_2)\omega_1\omega_2=M_3
$$
Which is the correct form of the equation. We can similarly compute the other components, which gives us the full equation.
\subsection*{3-3}
We can express a sphere quite naturally using spherical coordinates, $q_1=r,q_2=\theta,q_3=\phi$. We have a single holonomic constraint:
$$q_1-R=0$$
We thus have $\mathcal{M}=2$ and can represent the bug position using just $\theta$ and $\phi$ -- like a latitude and longitude.
\subsection*{3-4}
\subsubsection{Part A}
We start with the energy relations to find the velocity:
$$mgx=\frac{1}{2}mv^2$$
We assume no initial velocity, so we have:
$$v=\sqrt{2gx}$$
\subsubsection{Part B}
We can parameterize $ds$ as $\sqrt{dx^2+dy^2}$. We can pull out the $dx^2$ term from the integral:
$$ds=\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)^2}dx$$
We can now use the hint to write the functional:
$$\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\frac{\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)^2}}{\sqrt{2gx}}dx$$
Notice now that the equation is in a form which can be solved by the \gls{Euler Equation}, where the integrand is:
$$f=\frac{\sqrt{1+y'(x)}}{\sqrt{2gx}}$$
\subsubsection{Part C}
With an equation in terms of $y(x)$ and $y'(x)$, we can use the Euler Lagrange equations to solve for the curve $y(x)$:
    $$\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}=\frac{d}{dx}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y'}$$
    We may notice immediately that the system has no $y(x)$, so the LHS is 0. Equivalently, the derivative of the RHS must be constant. This leaves us with:$$c=\frac{\partial}{\partial y'}\left(\frac{\sqrt{1+y'(x)^2}}{\sqrt{x}}\right)$$
    After going through the calculations, we find:$$y'(x)=\sqrt{\frac{x}{2c-x}}$$
    We can perform a final integration to get our curve $y(x)$. This integral takes the form $$y(x)=\int\sqrt{\frac{x}{2c-x}}dx$$
We use a substitution with $x=c(1-\cos\theta)$, which gives us the integral as:
    $$y(x)=c\int(1-\cos\theta)d\theta=c(\theta-sin\theta)+c_1$$ This gives us the final parametric curve:
    \begin{gather}
        y=c(\theta-\sin\theta)\\
        x=c(1-\cos\theta)
    \end{gather}
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\autoref{ex:inverted_pend}}{Inverted Pendulum}}
We start by noticing our two generalized coordinates, $\theta$ and $\dot{x}$. We can set a potential of zero at the base of the cart. The \gls{Lagrangian} can be derived quite easily from here. Starting with the cart:
$$T_{cart}=\frac{1}{2}M\dot{x}^2$$
The cart has no potential. For the pendulum arm, both translational and rotational motion is permitted, so the total kinetic energy is more complicated. One `trick' we can do in the computation that is sometimes simpler is to unexpand the chain rule and express the kinetic energy as:
$$T_{arm}=\frac{1}{2}m|\dot{r}_2|^2$$
Computing the value of $\dot{r}_2$ (remembering the implicit chain rule, now that we are not explicitly writing it), we have:
$$\dot{r}_2=(\dot{x}+l\cos\theta\dot{\theta})\hat{\imath}+(-l\sin\theta\dot{\theta})\hat{\jmath}$$
Performing the norm-square of $\dot{r}_2$ is the same as the dot product, so we square each element:
$$T_{arm}=\frac{1}{2}m\left[(\dot{x}+l\cos\theta\dot{\theta})^2+(-l\sin\theta\dot{\theta})^2\right]$$
Importantly, the computation of this gives a cross term from the expansion of the first term, which we may have missed otherwise. Altogether, $T_{arm}$ is:
$$T_{arm}=\frac{1}{2}m\dot{x}^2+\frac{1}{2}ml^2\dot{\theta}^2+ml\cos\theta\dot\theta\dot{x}$$
At this point, we can identify that the term $\frac{1}{2}ml^2\dot{\theta}^2$ is exactly the rotational kinetic energy of the rod. As a result, we can replace this term with $\frac{1}{2}I\dot{\theta}^2$. Finally, summing the two values of $i$ gives us the following kinetic energy for the system:
$$T_{cart}+T_{arm}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(M+m\right)\dot{x}^2+I\dot{\theta}^2\right]+ml\cos\theta\dot{\theta}\dot{x}$$

The potential is quite easy. For the cart, no potential exists because we have defined the potential at the center of mass to be $0$. For the arm, the potential is dependent on the angle, with the relationship given by:
$$V_{pend}=mgl\cos{\theta}$$
Altogether, this gives a \gls{Lagrangian} of the system as:
$$L=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(M+m\right)\dot{x}^2+I\dot{\theta}^2\right]+ml\cos\theta\dot{\theta}\dot{x}-mgl\cos{\theta}$$
From here, we simply apply the Euler-Lagrange equations in both the $x$ and $\theta$ component directions. This is simply a matter of calculation. You may compute this by hand or use a script like is shown in the \autoref{ex:lagrangiancode} solution.


\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{\autoref{exer_doublepend}}{Double Pend Sol}}

\subsubsection{Part A}
We can solve the double pendulum in much the same way to the other problems. Here, we will use rectilinear coordinates in deriving the kinetic energy to ensure we don't miss any cross terms, much like we did with the inverted pendulum. We choose $\hat{x}$ to right and $\hat{y}$ to point upward on the page.

For the first arm, we have:
$$\vec{r}_1=l\sin\theta\hat{x}-l\cos\theta\hat{y}$$
And for the second:
$$\vec{r}_2=[l\sin\theta+l\sin(phi]\hat{x}-[l\cos\theta+l\cos\phi]\hat{y}$$
We can take the time derivative of these two terms:
\begin{gather}
\dot{r}_1=l\cos\theta\dot{\theta}\hat{x}+l\sin\theta\dot{\theta}\hat{y}\\
\dot{{r}}_2=[l\cos\theta\dot{\theta}+l\cos\phi\dot{\phi}]\hat{x}+[l\sin\theta\dot{\theta}+l\sin\phi\dot{\phi}]\hat{y}
\end{gather}

We take the kinetic energy to be:
$$\frac{1}{2}m|\dot{r}|^2$$
Which corresponds to squaring each element of the $\dot{r}$ vector. For the first arm:
$$T_1=\frac{1}{2}ml^2\dot{\theta}^2$$
The second arm is a bit more involved, since each term involves a cross term. This algebra simplifies quite a bit after application of  $\sin A\sin B+\cos A\cos B = \cos(A-B)$):
$$T_2=\frac{1}{2}ml^2\left[\dot{\theta}^2+\dot{\phi}^2+2\dot{\theta}\dot{\phi}\cos(\theta-\phi)\right]$$
The total kinetic energy is thus:
$$T=\frac{1}{2}ml^2\left[2\dot{\theta}^2+\dot{\phi}^2+2\dot{\theta}\dot{\phi}\cos(\theta-\phi)\right]$$
Note that some significant simplification has resulted from assuming that $m$ and $l$ are equal for both arms.

The potential is quite simple:
$$V=-mgl\cos\theta-mgl(cos\theta+\cos\phi)$$
$$V=-mgl(2\cos\theta+\cos\phi)$$

From which we get the Lagrangian:
$$L=\frac{1}{2}ml^2\left[2\dot{\theta}^2+\dot{\phi}^2+2\dot{\theta}\dot{\phi}\cos(\theta-\phi)\right]+mgl(2\cos\theta+\cos\phi)$$

Now, we may apply the Euler-Lagrange equations on the two generalized coordinates to get \glspl{eom}. We will take $\dot{\theta}\equiv\omega$ and $\dot{\phi}=\alpha$ to make our solution more compact. A computational tool is of great help to simplify this expression. The coupled equations are shown below. Notice that the mass is irrelevant in this case:
$$\begin{array}{c}
2\,l\,\ddot{\theta} +2\,g\,\sin \left(\theta \right)=l\,{\dot{\phi} }^2 \,\sin \left(\phi -\theta \right)-l\,\ddot{\phi} \,\cos \left(\phi -\theta \right)\\
l\,\sin \left(\phi -\theta \right)\,{\dot{\theta} }^2 +l\,\ddot{\phi} +g\,\sin \left(\phi \right)=l\,\ddot{\theta} \,{\left(2\,{\sin \left(\frac{\phi }{2}-\frac{\theta }{2}\right)}^2 -1\right)}
\end{array}$$
We can decouple and simplify the equations to get:
\begin{align}
    \ddot{\theta}&=-\frac{l\sin(\phi-\theta)\dot{\phi}^2+\cos(\phi-\theta)(l\sin(\phi-\theta)\dot{\theta}^2+g\sin\phi)-2g\sin\theta}{l(\cos(\phi-\theta)^2-2)}\\\\
    \ddot{\phi}&=\frac{2l\sin(\phi-\theta)\dot{\theta}^2-\cos(\phi-\theta)(2g\sin\theta-l\dot{\phi}^2\sin(\phi-\theta)+2g\sin\phi}{l(\cos(\phi-\theta)^2-2)}
\end{align}

If you think such a result is complicated, try the Newtonian approach and you will change your mind quickly!
\subsubsection{Part B}
Once we have the \glspl{eom} of the system, we can simulate with $\texttt{ode45}$ easily. Only the integrator function is shown for brevity:
\begin{matlabscript}{Pendulum Integrator}
% integrator
function out = integrator(t,x,l,g)

theta = x(1);
phi = x(2);
theta_dot = x(3);
phi_dot = x(4);

theta_ddot = -(l*sin(phi - theta)*phi_dot^2 + cos(phi - theta)*(l*sin(phi - theta)* ...
    theta_dot^2 + g*sin(phi)) - 2*g*sin(theta))/(l*(cos(phi - theta)^2 - 2));
phi_ddot = (2*l*sin(phi - theta)*theta_dot^2 - cos(phi - theta)*(2*g*sin(theta) - l* ...
    phi_dot^2*sin(phi - theta)) + 2*g*sin(phi))/(l*(cos(phi - theta)^2 - 2));

out = [theta_dot; phi_dot; theta_ddot; phi_ddot];
end
\end{matlabscript}
This script is also present in the linked GitHub in \autoref{Appendix:code} in the script $\texttt{DoublePend.m}$.
\subsubsection{Part C}
Finally, we can compute 

\section{Chapter 4 Solutions}

\subsection*{4-2}
To determine if the soldiers will be knocked over by the force of the engine, we need to convert that thrust force from being expressed in the body frame to the inertial frame. To do this, we will take our known rotation sequence and corresponding Euler angles, use them along with MATLAB’s built in function to construct the DCM, and then transpose that DCM in order to convert from body to inertial coordinates. Finally, we will take the magnitude of the components acting in the xy-plane and compare that to the known maximum allowable force.

Doing all of this, we find that the force acting in the inertial xy-plane is 1574 N, which exceeds our maximum value of 1000 N. This means the soldiers will indeed go tumbling away as you go in to land. The code used to perform these calculations is shown below:
\newpage
\begin{matlabscript}{Problem 4-2 Script}
% PSP FLIGHT DYNAMICS:
%
% Title: Problem 4-2 Script
% Author: Preston Wright
% Last Modified: 9-6-2025 
% 
% Description: This script computes a frame conversion of the thrust force to determine the component acting in the inertial xy-plane.
clear;clc;close all

% Initialize known variables
thrustBody = [0;0;10000];   % Thrust force in body coordinates [N]
maxForce = 1000;            % Max force that can act in xy-plane [N]
psi = 0;                    % Yaw angle/z-axis angle [rad]
theta = 0.15;               % Pitch angle/y-axis angle [rad]
phi = -0.05;                % Roll angle/x-axis angle [rad]

% Convert the thrust force to be expressed in inertial coordinates
DCM = angle2dcm(psi,theta,phi,"ZYX");
DCM = DCM';
thrustInertial = DCM*thrustBody;

% Calculate the magnitude of the force acting in the xy-plane
thrustPlane = sqrt(thrustInertial(1)^2+thrustInertial(2)^2);

% Compare that magnitude to see if it exceeds the maximum allowable force
if thrustPlane > maxForce
    fprintf("The thrust exceeds the maximum allowable force and the soldiers will be blown away!\n")
else
    fprintf("The thrust is below the maximum allowable force and the soldiers will be fine.\n")
end
\end{matlabscript}
